Odyssey's full `g13s` archive of 32,864 bases builds in 15.6 minutes (exit 0); its Cap Kingdom frames 4,200 to 6,600 match `-O2` except for scattered differences of at most 3 of 255 in three of nine frames, and their GPU time per frame (`DM_NVN_GPUPROF=every:150`, three runs each on an M-series `g18g`) has medians of 4.76 to 5.05 ms at `-O1` and 4.75 to 4.83 ms at `-O2`, within run-to-run noise.

### Specialized second functions in the offline pipeline archive

- **Symptom:** the Mac app's (or the iPhone build's) pipeline archive step stops in its first group with `[RED] pso_offline: metal-tt failed for applegpu_<arch> (exit 1): applegpu-nt: error: cannot find private metadata at offset '8' in '…/<key>_bp.metallib'`, and the app is packed without an archive, compute pipelines included. The host's prebuild then makes every base on the first start. Only a game with the near = 0 depth mode (`depth_mode zero`) whose vertex shaders declare the border corrections' constant.
- **Cause:** `pso_offline.py` specializes a function that declares the [border corrections' constant](rendering.md#unused-border-corrections-retain-sample-registers) `dm_border_off` as the host's prebuild does (`HOST_CONSTANTS` in `pso_offline.py`, `pre_fn` in `nvn_prewarm.inc`). `metal-tt` 32023.922 specializes only a library's first function: it does not find a later function's private metadata (offset 8 is the second function's entry), also in a library linked by Apple's own `metallib`. The same function with the library's two functions swapped compiles, and the then-second `main0` fails, so it is the position, not the entry. A near = 0 game asks for `main0_z`, the second function of each pulling library `<key>_bp`; Ocarina of Time, where the specialization was first checked, uses `main0`, and a pixel library's base is its first function. One failing pipeline fails its group, and a failed group ends the step.
- **Fix:** a workaround for Apple's compiler: a specialized function that is not its library's first is specialized from a library of its own (`needs_cut`, `cut_entries`). `real_libraries` writes `<library>@<entry>.metallib` with that function alone (`dmsh_side.cut`, the bytes `metallib --function-name` gives) beside links to the group's other libraries. The archive serves the host's lookup through the whole library, as a base is found by its function, not by its library file. The script and the archive key do not change for a game whose specialized functions all come first.
- **Found on:** Bayonetta Origins: Cereza and the Lost Demon (`0100CF5010FEC000`, no profile yet; [issue #2337](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/issues/2337), `applegpu_g14s`: 492 of 4,772 bases); Pokémon Legends: Z-A and Castlevania: Belmont's Curse (`applegpu_g16s`); Nintendo Switch Sports Resort (`04008E001F098000`; [PR #2371](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/pull/2371): 2,401 of 11,075 bases, from 124 such libraries); reproduced on an Apple M6, macOS 27.2, compiling a two-function library built by Xcode for `applegpu_g17p`. With the fix, Switch Sports Resort's 2,401 bases compiled in 96 s, and 11 sampled bases were found through the whole libraries.
- **Check:** `test_pso_offline.py` (`test_a_specialized_function_after_the_first_has_a_library_of_its_own`, and with Xcode `test_the_cut_library_holds_the_function_alone`); `t_pso_bundle` (a pulling library whose `main0` and `main0_z` declare the constant, archived for near = 0: `pre_desc` finds its `main0_z` base and no `main0` base; without the fix `pso_offline.py` fails).

### Tests of one uniform word folded into switches

- **Symptom:** the Mac app's (or the iPhone build's) pipeline archive step runs for many hours: a group of 1,000 bases runs past its time limit, and `compile_split` leaves out single pipelines that each ran past theirs (`[RED] pso_offline: applegpu_<gpu>: left out of the archive, the device builds it`), while `air-nt` grows past the Mac's memory and swap fills. Alone, such a pipeline does not finish in 10 minutes and takes 10 to 13 GB, where the build before took seconds. On the device, the same pipelines would hold `Preparing pipelines` for as long.
- **Cause:** a game's material shader chooses a branch by a uniform word, and the translator writes the GLSL switch as Ryujinx's structurizer gives it: one test of the word for each case, with flags for the fall-throughs and each case nested in the next (`bool _6240 = as_type<int>((*spvDescriptorSet0.fp_c5)._m0[16].w) != 102; if (!_6240) { … }`). The Metal front end's SimplifyCFG (which runs at [-O1](#front-end-at--o1-apples-control-flow-lowering) too) folds a chain of blocks that each hold only such a test of one value into a `switch`, whose cases jump into the nested blocks past their joins, and Apple's GPU compiler (`applegpu-nt`, AIR-TT 32023.922) takes that graph for hours. Turning the switches back into compares (`metal-opt -passes=lowerswitch`) does not help: the graph is the cost, not the instruction. Before the host bounded [reads at fixed offsets](#reads-past-a-uniform-bindings-size) (chain `b7249b1c2b66`), each read's bounds test sat in the test's own block, so no chain was folded; with the reads plain, Nintendo Switch Sports Resort's pixel shader `692008b305ce264f` has 42 switches in its IR (none before) and `7c2becb054a53765` 66 (6 before).
- **Fix:** every game, a chain change. `dmsh_rewrite.uniform_tests`, called by `dmsh_build.py` after the other rewrites, reads a word of a game uniform slot (`c3` and up; the driver's banks are left as written) that the shader tests for equality with an integer constant more than once through `dm_uword`, a volatile load of that word for each test (`as_type<int>(dm_uword((*spvDescriptorSet0.fp_c5)._m0[16], 3)) != 102`). The front end can neither merge one test's load with the next nor move it out of its block, so it folds no chain. A uniform load per test is less than the bounds test each read had before. A word tested once, a test against a variable, and an ordering test are left as they are.
- **Found on:** Nintendo Switch Sports Resort (`04008E001F098000`): with chain `b7249b1c2b66` the Mac app's archive ran 15 hours in its first group (vertex `1f0649e274faa626`, `1552eae0ea641bcd` and `24ce8042ec8edfff` with pixel `7c2becb054a53765`, vertex `2265ca4db20439bf` with pixel `692008b305ce264f`, each left out after about 30 minutes alone), where chain `592225da745b` had built 1,000 bases in about 3 minutes. Measured alone with `metal-tt` 32023.922 (`applegpu_g14s`, macOS 27.2, M2 Max with 32 GB), `2265ca4db20439bf` + `692008b305ce264f`: chain `592225da745b` 7.4 s and 0.25 GB; chain `b7249b1c2b66` stopped at 10 minutes and 13.4 GB; that chain's vertex library with the old pixel library 4.2 s, the old vertex library with the new pixel library stopped at 5 minutes and 10 GB; the old MSL with the fixed-offset bounds tests taken out stopped at 5 minutes; the same with the tests kept only on the 910 reads that feed an equality test, 3.1 s; with `uniform_tests`, 6.3 s and 0.21 GB. `1f0649e274faa626` + `7c2becb054a53765` with `uniform_tests`: 11.5 s and 0.45 GB.
- **Check:** `python3 dm/gfx/tests/test_dmsh_uniform_tests.py` (the forms it rewrites and leaves; with Xcode, the plain shader's IR has a switch with `dmsh_build.py`'s command and the rewritten one none).

### Bracket nesting past the front end's 256

- **Symptom:** the shader build prints `fatal error: bracket nesting level exceeded maximum of 256` and `<key>.metal: does not compile`, and the shader is left out.
- **Cause:** a translated expression nests deeper than the Metal front end's default limit of 256 brackets. Ocarina of Time's compute shaders `1f78730053feb036` and `e172bd7ec218ab8e` (3.5 MB of MSL each) reach 259.
- **Fix:** every library compile passes `-fbracket-depth=1024` (`dmsh_build.py` `METAL_DEPTH`). A source within the old limit compiles to the same bytes with the flag, on macOS and iOS. Like the precompiled header, the flag is not in the compile records, so the Metal cache keeps every library already compiled.
- **Found on:** Ocarina of Time (Switch 2, `0400486024DEC000`, PR #1299); both shaders now compile.
- **Check:** `test_shader_pch.py` (`BracketDepth`: 300 nested brackets fail without the flag and compile with it, the test shaders' bytes are unchanged, and both builds take the same depth).

### min, max and clamp with a NaN operand

- **Symptom:** the half of Mario facing the camera's left was white (GitHub issue 72, on an iPhone).
- **Cause:** spirv-cross writes `FMin` / `FMax` / `FClamp` as `fast::min` / `max` / `clamp`, which leave a NaN operand's result to the GPU. Maxwell's `FMNMX` returns the other operand and its saturation gives 0, as Metal's `precise::` functions (IEEE `fmin` / `fmax`) do. Plain `max` / `min` / `clamp` compile to the `fast_` intrinsics in every Metal math mode, relaxed included (iOS math mode (relaxed)). Galaxy's GX lighting normalizes light directions the game leaves at (0, 0, 0) (the key and fill lights of its characters) and takes `max(0, dot(ldir, NaN))` as the spot term: 0 on the M4 Pro with either function, so the attenuation is 1. The fill light sits at the camera's left in view space, so its terminator runs down the middle of a character facing the camera.
- **Fix:** `dmsh_build.py` (`precise`) writes the `precise::` functions instead (every game).
- **Found on:** Super Mario Galaxy 2 (GitHub issue 72). The NVN chain had about 124,000 `fast::max`, 98,000 `fast::clamp` and 41,000 `fast::min` in Paper Mario: The Thousand-Year Door, none guarded by its `isnan` selects.

### Integer fragment outputs declared by hand

- **Superseded (2026-11-16):** every pixel shader has the typed-output entry point ([Typed outputs for every pixel shader](#typed-outputs-for-every-pixel-shader)), and the six profiles' rows below were removed; the pipeline red line prints a row only for a shader whose `main0` has a shape `fs_uint` does not know, where the root-cause fix is teaching `fs_uint` that shape. The text below is how it was.
- **Symptom:** a pipeline red line for a float output meeting an integer attachment (the red line prints the row to add); a BC encoder's pipeline fails.
- **Cause:** with no PC version the translator has no output types and declares every output float, and Metal refuses a pipeline whose float output meets an integer attachment. A scan for outputs built from integer operations was not trusted: the translated code keeps float bits in integer variables (register reuse), so such a scan cannot tell an integer output from raw float bits stored into a float target.
- **Fix:** the profile's `shader_facts_manual.tsv` declares such outputs `uint`. When another one fails, the pipeline red line prints the row to add. Shaders that write an output from integer bits now also get integer entry points ([Integer entry points for integer colour outputs](#integer-entry-points-for-integer-colour-outputs)).
- **Found on:** The Legend of Zelda: Breath of the Wild (agl's BC4 encoder `8e2cc2671cae6020` and its twin for a 2D source `93aa5e22a8202409`, whose failure showed up in an iPhone run); The Legend of Zelda: Tears of the Kingdom (the BC4 encoder `0544282c162305db` and the Ultrahand mask `e3fb0bb58a691e10`, R8Uint, found by PR #11; the Mac route does not reach it); Pokémon Legends: Z-A (two system pixel shaders write an ID into an `R8_UI` target on the character creation screen); DRAGON QUEST VII Reimagined (one global shader writing an `R8Uint` target, Unreal Engine 4.27 shader code archives).
- **Note:** the compressed render target side is in [Compressed textures as render targets through an uncompressed shadow](rendering.md#compressed-textures-as-render-targets-through-an-uncompressed-shadow).

### A pixel shader's depth after a partly written target

- **Symptom:** a pixel shader that writes depth and only some components of a colour target writes the wrong depth: in the translated MSL, `gl_FragDepth` takes a value the shader computes a step before its last one, or a colour.
- **Cause:** the colours are read four registers per render target with any component enabled (component c of the n-th such target in R(4n + c)), then comes the sample mask register, then the depth. `TranslatorContext.GetDepthRegister` counted enabled components instead, which agrees only when each enabled target has all four. With OmapTargets 0x10f (RT0 RGBA, RT2 R) the shader computes its depth into R9, `R6 = 1 / -(z * a + b)` then two constant adds, and R6 was read.
- **Fix:** `GetDepthRegister` returns 4 × the enabled targets + 1. A chain change.
- **Check:** `DMSH_TEST_XLATE=<translator> python3 dm/gfx/tests/test_dmsh_ampere_mem.py DepthRegister` (R6 0.25, R9 0.75: the old translator writes 0.25).
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md): 46 of the 126 depth-writing pixel shaders in its shader package (mask 0x10f ×24, 0x100 ×16, 0x3f ×4, 0x30 ×2), 16 of them in its seen shaders, among them agl_common's depth pass `3a4a0270fc35b848`, whose depth now takes the step after R6 too, `(R6 * c3[32].z + 1) * c3[32].w`. Its effect on the picture has not been compared. No other local game has such a shader: the 1,105 depth-writing pixel shaders of seven Switch 1 games enable whole targets. Found by the flaviopz fork.

### Integer entry points for integer colour outputs

- **Symptom:** `failed to create render pipeline … output of type float4 is not compatible with a MTLPixelFormatR32Uint color attachment` (or `MTLPixelFormatRGBA16Uint`); every draw into the target skipped.
- **Cause:** with no PC version the translator types every fragment output float, and the shaders write bit-packed integers through `as_type<float>`. A hand-written row per shader (`shader_facts_manual.tsv`, Breath of the Wild's way, [Integer fragment outputs declared by hand](#integer-fragment-outputs-declared-by-hand)) does not scale.
- **Fix:** `dmsh_entry.py` `fs_uint` gives a pixel shader a second entry point, `main0_u` (since 2026-11-16 every pixel shader, with signed members too: [Typed outputs for every pixel shader](#typed-outputs-for-every-pixel-shader); the text below is how it began). It was given when the shader writes an output from integer bits (directly or through a float local that holds them), nothing but constants (a clear drawn into an R32Uint target) or nothing but flat inputs passed through. Its output struct holds two members on each float colour `n`, the float and the unsigned integer vector of the same bits, each present by a bool function constant (`DMSH_UINT_FC + n`, `dm_ui<n>`), and it returns each colour as whichever is present: the hardware writes the output's bits to an integer target whatever the shader computed, so the copy is exact. The host specializes it with the pipeline's integer targets (`pso_uint_mask`, `uint_fn`: one function per set, kept), whether all of them are integer or some, and the alpha test and logic operation entry points `main0_u_a` and `main0_u_l` the same way (`at_fn`, `lo_fn`). A shader without `main0_u` keeps `main0` and the red line.
- **Found on:** Luigi's Mansion 3: about 1,400 pixel shaders writing its G-buffer of two integer targets (R32Uint, RGBA8Uint), so every scene draw was skipped. Pokémon Sword: 10 pipelines as the opening's stadium loads; the scene shaders write colour 0 and 2 as floats and colour 1 from integer bits into an RGBA16Uint target (colour 1 in all 336 of Sword's). Silver Pines: URP's rendering-layers mask, an R8Uint target beside float outputs (colour 1 forward, 4 and 5 deferred), in 9,720 pixel shaders; without it only the fog drew. Trails in the Sky 2nd Chapter: its G-buffer float at colour 0 and integer at colours 1 to 3.

### Integer outputs told by the pipeline's targets

- **Symptom:** a deferred scene with part of its materials unlit or black: grass, ground and trees dark while wood and characters look right, whole areas of a map black. There may be no red line. A texture dump shows an integer G-buffer target (`fmt=73`, RGBA8Uint) holding only zeros, and the lighting pass reads it. Or `[RED] shader … writes colour target …, an integer format, as a float`.
- **Cause:** a G-buffer of float and integer targets needs a pixel shader to return some outputs as floats and others as integers. Until 2026-11-13 the build chose which from the shader's code and gave such a shader fixed mixed entry points (`main0_m`, then `main0_n`), and the host took one only when the pipeline's integer targets were exactly its set. The translator keeps registers in integer locals, so a plain float colour reaches its output as integer bits too, and the code says less than the pipeline does. A shader with no entry point for its pipeline kept `main0`, whose float output met the integer attachment: a pipeline built on its own is refused for that, but one specialized from a Metal 4 base is not, and the target stayed zero with no error. Kirby Star Allies' G-buffer is RGBA16F, RGBA8 sRGB, RGBA8Uint, RG11B10F: 283 of its pixel shaders got the mask 0b111, 8 got 0b101 and 396 none. Following each write back through the locals (`int_outputs`) fixed Kirby, and Trails in the Sky 2nd Chapter then needed four more rules in turn, each found by its red line in play: a uniform word written straight into an output, outputs of nothing but zeros, a register value every branch overwrites, a texel read with `read()`. Each was beside integer outputs into integer targets, and each rule moved other games' masks.
- **Fix:** the pipeline's formats say which outputs are integers, as they do on the hardware: `main0_u` returns each colour as a float or as integer bits by a function constant, and the host specializes it per pipeline ([Integer entry points for integer colour outputs](#integer-entry-points-for-integer-colour-outputs)). No rule reads which outputs hold integers any more; the build only decides whether a shader gets `main0_u` at all. Over five of the local games' libraries that gives `main0_u` to the shaders that had it before and a few more (PRAGMATA 403 of 15,877 pixel shaders, Pikmin 4 119, Luigi's Mansion 3 5, Fire Emblem Engage 8, none in OCTOPATH TRAVELER II, Diablo II: Resurrected, Hollow Knight: Silksong or Bastion), and with `main0_m` and `main0_n` gone, fewer entry points in all (PRAGMATA's 8,578 `main0_u` and 3,212 `main0_m` became 8,981).
- **Found on:** Kirby Star Allies: 687 pixel shaders whose integer target stayed zero. Trails in the Sky 2nd Chapter: the red line from five shaders in turn (`10cd95a2a310ea8c`, `979fd4d4d4bd7615`, `b09f3ac96129e558`, `96ec090803707686`, `aa682f8e32981529`).
- **What made a shader an integer writer** (until 2026-11-16, when every pixel shader got `main0_u`: [Typed outputs for every pixel shader](#typed-outputs-for-every-pixel-shader)): a float local that holds a register's bits counts: PRAGMATA's visibility pass writes `float _439 = as_type<float>(int(gl_PrimitiveID))` and the depth to an RG32Uint target, and without it no shader got `main0_u` (`[RED] task / mesh pipeline failed: … output of type float4 is not compatible with a MTLPixelFormatRG32Uint color attachment`). So do writes after an early return: four of those shaders (`a9c1f9ea0c76a54c`, `1282ed0df15021c7`, `a88532b3a2bbc36f`, `bfce48e3c0debea2`) return zeros early, after a discard, and write the primitive id and depth after that; read only up to the first `return out;`, they got none, and the whole 3D scene was black behind the UI.

### Integer entry points for flat inputs passed through beside constants

- **Symptom:** an Unreal landscape drawn from a runtime virtual texture is one flat lit colour on the Mac (green grass with no texture) and a grid of repeating tiles on an iPhone; rocks, characters and everything else right; no red line. A texture dump shows the virtual texture's BC tile caches full and varied, and its page table (R16Uint, `fmt=23`, 512x512 with 10 mips, the texture the landscape's pixel shader reads at `reg130`) holding 255 in every entry: level 15, page address 15, so the sample lands on one texel of the cache. Since this entry, `[RED] shader … writes colour target …, an integer format, as a float, and has no integer entry point`.
- **Cause:** `fs_uint` gave a pixel shader its integer entry point when its outputs were all written from integer bits, all constants, or all flat inputs passed through. Unreal's page table update (global shader `17eda07e4bac39f8`; its vertex shader `478292f0f6d79ae4` reads the update records from a texture buffer and packs the page's level and address into a flat varying) writes the flat input's x and the constant 0 to y, z and w: neither rule held, `main0` kept its `float4` output, a pipeline specialized from a Metal 4 base was built without an error ([Integer outputs told by the pipeline's targets](#integer-outputs-told-by-the-pipelines-targets)), and the R16Uint target took 255 at every pixel drawn. That entry's red line fires only for a shader that has an integer entry point, so nothing named the shader.
- **Fix:** `fs_uint` takes any mix of constants and flat inputs passed through. The reflection now records which colour outputs `main0` declares float (`raster.float_colors`, `raster_facts`), and the pipeline path (`pso_desc`) prints the red line above, with the `shader_facts_manual.tsv` row (`pso_fout_hint`), when such an output meets an unsigned integer attachment and no integer entry point fits, whether the shader has one or none. A chain change.
- **Check:** `python3 -m unittest dm/tools/tests/test_shader_rewrites.py` (`TypedOutputs`: since [Typed outputs for every pixel shader](#typed-outputs-for-every-pixel-shader) every shader gets the entry point, whatever it writes).
- **Found on:** DRAGON BALL: Sparking! ZERO: the landscape of the story battles' stage on an M1 (base v0), and [#1889](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/issues/1889) (an iPhone 17). Rebuilt with the change, the floor shows its dirt, grass and tracks, with no new red line; the iPhone is still to be checked.

### Typed outputs for every pixel shader

- **Symptom:** on the `MTLDevice` path (the iPhone's default) `[RED] failed to create render pipeline … output of type float4 is not compatible with a MTLPixelFormatR8Uint color attachment` for many fragment shaders, and most of the scene missing (black sky, no characters). With `DM_PSO_MTL4=1` the same pipelines build from Metal 4 bases and the picture looks right, but `[RED] shader … writes colour target 5, an integer format, as a float, and has no integer entry point` stays: the target takes undefined values. A signed integer target (`R32Sint`, …) is refused the same way, by every shader.
- **Cause:** the class of a colour output (float, unsigned, signed) is its target's: Maxwell writes the register's bits whatever the shader computed. `fs_uint` gave a pixel shader its integer entry point `main0_u` only when its code looked like an integer writer (integer bits, constants, flat inputs passed through), rules each found by a red line in play and each missing the next game: Kirby and the Forgotten Land's 76 shaders writing its R8Uint colour 5 matched none. `main0_u` had no signed members, so a signed target had no entry point at all, and the host's red line fired only for unsigned targets.
- **Fix:** every pixel shader of a known shape gets `main0_u`, whatever its code writes. Its output struct holds three members on each colour `n` declared `float`, `uint` or `int` (scalar or vector): the float, unsigned and signed vector of the same width, each present by a bool function constant (`DMSH_UINT_FC + n`, `DMSH_SINT_FC + n`; the float where neither is set), and returns the colour's bits as whichever is present (`as_type`, nothing converted); a colour of another type (`half`) or shape stays as `main0` has it. `main0_u` also holds the fixed-function work Metal lacks, each switched by a constant with its arguments present only then: the alpha test (`DMSH_TYPED_ALPHA_FC`, on colour 0's alpha before the outputs are typed, `dmsh_alpha.test`) and the logic operation (`DMSH_TYPED_LOGIC_FC`, on the typed outputs, reading the attachments `DMSH_LOGIC_FC + n` names, `dmsh_logic.parts`), so it replaces `main0_u_a`, `main0_u_l` and `main0_l`, and one function serves every variant a pipeline asks for. `main0_a` stays, with no constant, so an alpha-tested pipeline of the declared classes needs no specialization at run time. The build cuts `main0_u` into the side library `<key>_bx` (`ps_libraries`, `entry_u_side`, `entry_u_alpha`, `entry_u_logic` in the reflection), so the base library every shader loads, and the pipeline archive compiles, keeps `main0` alone with no function constant. A side library that does not build leaves `main0_u` in `<key>_b`, and one that does not build with it is built without it (`entries_lost`), as before. The reflection records which colours `main0` declares float, unsigned and signed (`raster.float_colors`, `uint_colors`, `sint_colors`); the host (`pso_cls`, `typed_fn`, one function per set of classes and switches, kept) takes `main0_u` when a target's class is not the declared one, in either direction (a `fout` fact that declares an output unsigned meeting a float target too), or when the pipeline has a logic operation, with the alpha test on when the pipeline has one; the side library is loaded once per shader on first need (`bx_lib`). An alpha test with a logic operation, which no library combined, now draws through `main0_u` (`lop_key`). The red line now names a shader whose pipeline needs a class it cannot give: one whose `main0` has a shape `fs_uint` does not know, with the `shader_facts_manual.tsv` row (`pso_fout_hint`, also on a refused pipeline, only for such a shader). The hand-written `fout` rows of six profiles went with it (Breath of the Wild's and Tears of the Kingdom's BC4 encoders and Ultrahand mask, Pokémon Legends: Z-A's, DRAGON QUEST VII Reimagined's and FINAL FANTASY RESONANCE's): their shaders take `main0_u` like any other. The BC encoders write bits no float keeps (NaN payloads, denormals) through `main0`'s float output, and `main0_u` gives them to an integer target unchanged (`t_typed_outputs`' colour 3). Replaces PR [#2807](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/pull/2807)'s second entry point `main0_t` for the same problem. A chain change.
- **Cost:** measured on 200 of Cuphead's pixel shaders with a cold compile cache (M4 Pro): the iOS libraries 13.55 → 13.92 MB (+2.8%), the Mac's 17.54 → 18.62 MB (+6.1%), build time within noise (13–14 s both once warm). A first version with `main0_u_a` and `main0_u_l` as entry points of their own took +47% and +38%: each is a whole copy of the shader's body. The base library, the pipeline archive, frame time and the draws of pipelines of the declared classes do not change. The first pipeline of a shader with a target of another class or a logic operation loads its side library and specializes `main0_u` (a synchronous `newFunctionWithName:constantValues:`), as `main0_u` and `main0_l` always did.
- **Check:** `python3 -m unittest dm/tools/tests/test_shader_rewrites.py` (`TypedOutputs`), `python3 dm/gfx/tests/test_dmsh_typed.py` (the unit, the cut and the reflection's classes), `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_typed_outputs` (every mix of float, unsigned and signed targets on colours 0 to 2, and the alpha test of a float colour 0 beside integer targets, through `pso_desc` on the Metal 4 path and the `MTLDevice`'s, the bits read back), `check_t_logic` (the logic operation through `main0_u`) and `check_t_alpha`.
- **Found on:** Kirby and the Forgotten Land on an iPhone ([#2760](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/issues/2760)): 76 shaders in Waddle Dee Town writing an R8Uint colour 5. Mortal Kombat 11's signed and unsigned material outputs (PR [#2807](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/pull/2807)).

### Integer textures and images through float declarations keep their bits

- These accesses kept their integer bits on the Apple GPU used in the original check. Metal leaves a mismatch between texture format and shader type undefined. The host adapts supported 32-bit integer buffer image formats to matching float views. See [Float views for integer buffer images](rendering.md#float-views-for-integer-buffer-images).
- **Found on:** DRAGON QUEST VII Reimagined (the volumetric lightmap's indirection texture, the light grid buffers).

### Depth mode near = 0 needs the per-mode entry points

- **Symptom:** outdoors the whole screen blurred (depth of field out of focus everywhere).
- **Cause:** a game that sets depth mode near = 0 had every vertex shader translated for GL, so all depths sat in 0.5..1.
- **Fix:** the per-mode entry points of [04, section 5](../agents/04-graphics.md#5-depth-convention).
- **Found on:** The Legend of Zelda: Link's Awakening (Mac and iPhone): the depth-of-field composite (`52fcbb4ca2a78907`, `postprocess.bfsha`) blends the sharp frame with two blurred levels by a blur amount computed from the half-size depth copy: `pow(clamp((c5[2].y / (depth + c5[2].x) − c5[3].x) · c5[3].y), c5[3].z)` above a screen line, by screen position below it. Found with a save file from the iPhone (Mabe Village outside the house), `DM_NVN_PASSDUMP` / `DM_NVN_TEXDUMP` of that frame, and LASRecomp, which rewrites z only for the GL mode.

### Open: the window Y-direction register `SR_Y_DIRECTION`

- The register is −1 under a lower-left origin. The Ryujinx recompiler answers a constant +1 (a TODO in `InstEmitMove.cs`; LASRecomp, a static recompilation of Link's Awakening, answers −1). Answering it per profile means a translator change, i.e. every game's chain.
- **Found on:** The Legend of Zelda: Link's Awakening: 2,487 of its pixel shaders read it; in the room shader the value only scales a depth slope under `abs()`; other shaders may use its sign (`dFdy`-based effects).

### Subgroup operations rewritten for a one-lane subgroup

- **Symptom:** `SPIRV-Cross threw an exception: Subgroup builtins are not available in this type of function`.
- **Cause:** Metal has no SIMD-group functions in vertex functions, and SPIRV-Cross's own subgroup emulation covers only `Elect`. RE Engine scalarizes uniform loads: ballot the active lanes, shuffle the first lane's value to all and take a fast path when all agree.
- **Fix:** with a one-lane subgroup that is exact, so `dmsh_build.py` rewrites the vertex shaders' SPIR-V that way before SPIRV-Cross (`spv_single_lane`: the ballot is the lane's own bit, shuffles and broadcasts return its own value, all / any are the predicate, all-equal and elect are true, lane ID 0, size 1). A mask component read through `OpAccessChain` / `OpInBoundsAccessChain` becomes an extraction from the constant mask, preserving the index.
- **Found on:** Monster Hunter Rise (about 4 % of its vertex shaders); Xenoblade Chronicles 3 (vertex `4b98320a0f512efe`, a prefix ballot that reads `SubgroupLtMask` through a component pointer).
- **Types the module lacks:** the rewrite's constants need `bool` and a `uint` 4-vector; a module without one (a shader that reads only the lane ID) was left as it was, and now gets the type declared with the constants.
- **Note:** in nxnative domain shaders took the rewrite too: SPIRV-Cross writes them as post-tessellation vertex functions, with the same limit (PRAGMATA's `859225c91a5fe2b2` and `f1ad8f78c6c1551e` did not build; two of Bayonetta 3's failed this way). This chain translates no domain shader, and this host draws no patches ([A shader the chain does not translate stops the split and the build](#a-shader-the-chain-does-not-translate-stops-the-split-and-the-build), [Entries for transform feedback, tessellation and ray tracing report in red](nvn-objects.md#entries-for-transform-feedback-tessellation-and-ray-tracing-report-in-red)).

### NVN2 task and mesh shaders

- **Symptom:** `[RED] NVN unimplemented function called: nvnCommandBufferDrawMeshTasks`, and a build that fails with `Ampere instruction 927 ... is not implemented` in shaders the map calls vertex or domain; what they draw is missing.
- **Cause:** NVN2 has NVIDIA's task and mesh shaders, stages 6 and 7. The control section says so (+0x56), while the SPH reads as vertex or domain (type 1 or 3), so the split filed them there. Their stage memory has instructions of its own: ISBERD reads a workgroup's number (mode 0x78) or a payload word (0x38), and 0x927 writes a task's payload word (0xb8), a mesh attribute word (0xf8), the primitive count byte (0x88, at +3) or a primitive index byte (0x80, from +4). Control +0x58 is the workgroup's threads, +0x5a the payload's bytes, +0x5e whether a mesh shader has a task, +0x5f a primitive's vertices (1 point, 2 line, 3 triangle), +0x60 and +0x62 a mesh shader's most vertices and primitives, and +0x64 the code area offset of its per-primitive SPH where +0x68 bit 7 is set. Attributes come in all the blocks of 32 vertices, then all the blocks of 32 primitives ([Mesh attributes: every vertex block before the primitive blocks](#mesh-attributes-every-vertex-block-before-the-primitive-blocks)), each component 0x80 bytes: a vertex block the components the SPH's OMAP enables (byte 0x35 bit 3 the point size, bits 4..7 the position, bytes 0x36 on a nibble a generic attribute), a primitive block the per-primitive SPH's. A task writes its launch count at payload +4 and its records from +0x10; the hardware puts a mesh workgroup's number among its task's launches at payload +0.
- **Fix:**
  - `shader_split.py` names the stages `ts` and `ms`, as `task` and `mesh` in the shader map.
  - `dmsh_build.py` translates one as compute, its SPH taken off, with its stage memory in shared memory at the offsets `dmsh_meshstage.memory` gives (job `stage_memory`, `AIsberd` and `AStageOut` in `InstEmitAmpereMem.cs`). A byte store goes through the shared memory's byte store, which the build makes one Metal byte store, as neighbouring threads write neighbouring bytes ([Stage memory byte stores through a compare-and-swap loop](#stage-memory-byte-stores-through-a-compare-and-swap-loop)).
  - On the console a workgroup of 32 threads is one warp, so the zero count each thread writes first cannot pass the count one thread writes later. Metal's threads run in no such step, and a late zero would erase the count: that store is left out, and the wrapper starts the stage memory at zero.
  - `dmsh_meshstage.to_stage` wraps the kernel as a Metal `[[object]]` or `[[mesh]]` function: it fills the inputs, calls the kernel's body (whose early returns come back to it) and hands the outputs over. A mesh shader has `main0` and `main0_z` for the two depth conventions, as a vertex shader does.
  - The host keeps these stages in places 3 and 4 (`hle_nvn.c` `st_host`, `nvn_state.inc` `gfx_stage_bits`), which were the tessellation stages' in nxnative: this host has no tessellation. `nvn_mesh.inc` `mesh_tasks_draw` draws through `draw_` with the pixel stage alone, on a mesh pipeline built in the background; the mesh stage shares the vertex stage's argument table, and a task shader has the object stage's. Their storage buffer tables in `c0` are at 0x670 and 0x770: [Task and mesh draws reading the dummy buffer](#task-and-mesh-draws-reading-the-dummy-buffer).
- **Not done:** `DrawMeshTasks3D` and the multi-draw forms have no game yet to show their arguments, so they stay red. A task or mesh shader with shared memory of its own (LDS, STS, ATOMS) is refused at build time: it would lie over the stage memory and the control section does not give its size. No game's does.
- **Found on:** Mario Kart World: 7 task and 18 mesh shaders, which draw its bushes and grass. All 11 pipelines its first race makes build, and the race holds 60 fps with them. The facts of pull request #983 were checked against the game's code and hold, except its storage buffer table offsets.
- **Check:** `python3 dm/gfx/tests/test_dmsh_meshstage.py` (the stages compile for macOS where the Metal compiler is installed), `DMSH_TEST_XLATE=<translator> python3 dm/gfx/tests/test_dmsh_ampere_mem.py` (`StageMemory`).

### Task and mesh draws reading the dummy buffer

- **Symptom:** large flat panels in front of the sky, the clouds behind them blinking at times, in some places and not others. In a `DM_NVN_PASSDUMP_ALL` dump every task and mesh shader's storage buffer shows as `st3` / `st4 sbufp reg6` or `reg7` `dummy addr=0 size=0`, though the game binds buffers for those stages.
- **Cause:** the task and mesh storage buffer tables in `c0` start at 0x670 and 0x770, and the host took them to start at 0x610 and 0x710, 0x100 apart as the other stages' are. Task shaders read `c0` 0x670 and 0x680 and mesh shaders 0x770 to 0x7b0, so the build gave them slots 6 and up, while the game binds slots from 0. Each read the shared dummy buffer. Three of those shaders write their storage, so that buffer probably held their data when the others read it (not observed directly): shapes drawn into a 128x128x5 array got values past 1,000 where the rest stay under 1, and the vertex shader that moves its vertices by that array lifted them into the sky.
- **Fix:** `DMSH_C0_SSBO_TS` and `DMSH_C0_SSBO_MS` in `dmsh_abi.h` are 0x670 and 0x770. The tables no longer lie over the graphics stages' single words at 0x610 to 0x647, so the host writes the largest point size for every graphics stage again.
- **Found on:** Mario Kart World, [issue #1186](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/issues/1186).
- **Check:** `python3 -m unittest dm/tools/tests/test_dmsh_abi.py`.

### Stage memory byte stores through a compare-and-swap loop

- **Symptom:** a game with task and mesh shaders runs at a lower internal resolution than it could, or below its frame rate, and the GPU time sits in the pass that draws its mesh work (a render pass's start stamp is written after its vertex stage, where Metal runs the object and mesh stages, so in a `DM_NVN_GPUPROF` profile the mesh work shows as the gap before that pass or as its length). `DM_NVN_SKIP_MESH=1` (diagnostic: every task and mesh draw dropped, the picture wrong) takes most of that time away.
- **Cause:** the translator stores a byte or half-word of shared memory (`SharedStore8`, `SharedStore16`: STS.U8 / .U16, and a mesh shader's primitive count and index bytes, 0x927 modes 0x88 and 0x80) through a helper from upstream Ryujinx, a compare-and-swap loop on the word, because SPIR-V's shared memory is an array of words. A mesh workgroup's threads write neighbouring index bytes, so four threads contend for each word and each store retries, divergently, about four times. Metal's threadgroup memory takes byte and half-word stores, and C++'s memory model, which MSL follows, makes each byte its own memory location: neighbouring stores need no atomics.
- **Fix:** the helper is named `dmsh_shared_store8_<n>` / `dmsh_shared_store16_<n>` (`HelperFunctionManager.cs`), and `dmsh_rewrite.py` `shared_store_msl` replaces its body in the Metal source with one store through a `uchar` pointer, or an aligned `ushort` one marked `may_alias`, over the array (threadgroup, or device where `tg_spill` moved it). Character types and `may_alias` keep the compiler's type-based alias analysis from reordering the store against the word loads around it. Two threads storing the same byte at once leave one of the two values, as the loop did. A helper of another form stops that shader's build (`shared store rewrite failed`).
- **Not taken:** an atomic AND to clear the field and an atomic OR to set it (pull request #1503) need no retries either, but two threads writing the same byte leave the OR of both values, a value neither wrote; on the GPU that is what 64 threads writing different bits to one byte give.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md): 515 of 42,848 shaders (500 mesh shaders, the foliage among them, and 15 compute kernels with half-word stores). Pull request #1503 measured the atomic AND / OR form on an M3 Pro in Kokiri Forest: the foliage pass from 8.3-8.6 ms to 3.5-4.0 ms, and the game's internal resolution from 854x480 to 1120x630 (the frame from 25-28 ms to about 21 ms). On an M6 (12 GPU cores), standing on the forest deck, the three forms measure the same: GPU p50 14.0-14.6 ms with each, the two depth passes that hold the mesh work 3.1-3.3 and 2.2-2.4 ms, against 10.3 ms with the mesh draws dropped (three runs of each form, one of them with no pipeline archive). The store has not been measured on an M3. Mario Kart World: its 18 mesh shaders with a byte store build with the store for the Mac and iOS (not run).
- **Check:** `python3 dm/gfx/tests/test_dmsh_shared_store.py` (the rewrite, a spilled kernel, and on the GPU 64 threads storing neighbouring bytes and half-words over 64 dispatches); `DMSH_TEST_XLATE=<translator>` adds the translated index byte store.

### Mesh stage memory of 6 KB and more in a device slice

- **Symptom:** a mesh shader whose stage array is 6-16 KB runs slower than its instruction count suggests, and the foliage mesh pass of Ocarina of Time takes a large share of a Kokiri Forest frame on an M3 Pro. Shrinking the array (timing only) made the same code faster.
- **Cause:** each 32-thread mesh group declares its whole stage array (`dmsh_meshstage.py` `memory()`: the input, the vertex and primitive attribute blocks, the primitive bytes) as threadgroup memory, 5-16 KB in the foliage meshes. Apple GPUs hold fewer groups at once the more threadgroup memory each takes, and the mesh bodies were occupancy-limited: about 0.09 ms per KB per 16,384 groups.
- **Fix:** `to_stage` takes the array from a device scratch buffer for a mesh shader (kind `ms`) of 32 threads and 6000 to 16384 bytes of stage memory. The stage body, the byte store helpers and the wrapper's barriers (now `mem_device | mem_threadgroup`) use the `device` address space; the mesh function takes `device uchar* dm_scr [[buffer(DMSH_MESH_SCRATCH_SLOT)]]`, slot 7 of the table the mesh stage shares with the vertex stage (a mesh draw binds only slot 0, the arguments, and this one; vertex draws rebind whatever slot they read, as `enc_r_vbuf` tracks every write). The reflection records `mesh_spill: 1` (`nvn_refl.m` into `rf.mesh_spill`).
  - **The pool.** One buffer in shared storage (`nvn_mesh.inc` `mesh_scratch`), made on first use: a head of 32-bit words, a flag word a slice, then a power of two of 16 KiB slices. The slices must outnumber the groups the GPU runs at once. With the array out of threadgroup memory an Apple GPU core holds at most 3072 threads, 96 groups of 32, and the pool is twice that so that a claim takes about two tickets at worst: the Mac reads its GPU's `gpu-core-count` from the I/O Registry (an M4 Pro's 20 cores: 4096 slices, 64 MiB), a host that cannot read the count asks for 8192, and 16384 is the most. The GPU test measured at most 161 groups running at once on an M4 Pro, far below the bound.
  - **Claiming a slice.** Lane 0 takes a ticket with a relaxed atomic add, and claims slice `ticket & mask` when its flag is 0 or holds a tag no newer than the head's `DONE` word, by compare-and-swap to its own tag; up to `DMSH_MESH_SCRATCH_TRIES` (64) tickets. The group broadcasts the slice (32 threads are one SIMD-group), passes a device-scope fence, and zeroes the slice with `uint4` stores, as the threadgroup array started (the count byte relies on it). After its last read of the slice (a barrier after the index loop) and another device-scope fence, lane 0 clears the flag. A slice is handed out again only after its group finished, however long that group ran (GitHub pull request #2308: a first version took the slice at the ticket modulo the count, and a group that 8192 later groups overtook had its slice zeroed under it).
  - **Tags.** Before each draw of a spilled mesh shader the host writes the head's `TAG` word: the fence of the submission being encoded, so at least the fence of any submission whose groups read it (the commit follows the store, and the tag only grows). `post_complete` writes `DONE` as `g_done` advances, failed submissions included. A flag with a tag no newer than `DONE` belongs to a submission that is over, so its group runs no more: groups that a failed command buffer cut off leave their flags set, and the next claims reclaim them, with no wait on the GPU and no clearing pass. Tags compare by their signed 32-bit difference.
  - **No free slice.** A group that claims none in 64 tickets adds 1 to the head's `OVER` word, sets a primitive count of 0 and returns: it draws nothing and touches no slice it does not own. The host prints a red line once (`mesh scratch: … mesh groups found none of the … slices free and drew nothing`), which would mean the GPU ran more groups at once than the pool was sized for. An earlier version used its last ticket's slice without owning it after 8192 tickets.
- **Not taken:** below about 5.3 KB the device form gained nothing in the harness, so those shaders keep threadgroup memory. A group of more than 32 threads would broadcast its slice through a threadgroup word instead; no such mesh shader was measured, so they keep threadgroup memory.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md): the real translated foliage mesh bodies in a standalone harness on an M3 Pro: 20-46 % faster for stage arrays of 6.5 KB and more (73dc0b90, 10.3 KB: 0.568 ms to 0.332 ms with a ticketed slice). In the game, Kokiri Forest on an M3 Pro (pull request #2308, the ticket-and-flag version against main, six alternating rounds): frame p50 24.70 to 23.65 ms standing and 24.95 to 23.87 ms on a replayed route, GPU p50 22.89 to 21.83 ms standing. The lease version on an M4 Pro (20 GPU cores, 2026-11-15, `scripts/bench.py` route `kokiri-deck`, three runs a side, no prebuild, the first run of each warming its own pipeline archive): 4096 slices, no group without one, no red line, the shots alike; in the two warm runs GPU p50 14.58 / 14.60 ms against 13.87 / 15.10 ms and p95 16.64 / 16.68 against 15.82 / 16.32, so the main side's frame step held two vsyncs (40 fps) and the slices' side mostly one (43.1 and 58.6 fps). Two runs are too few to call the GPU time different on that Mac.
- **Check:** `python3 dm/gfx/tests/test_dmsh_meshstage.py` (the wrapper's structure, and both depth conventions compiled for macOS). `DMSH_TEST_GPU=1 python3 dm/gfx/tests/test_dmsh_meshstage.py OnGpu` draws a spilled mesh stage on the GPU, 24,576 groups a draw, with the head's words set as the host sets them: (1) a long group among short ones, 8 submissions, where group 0 keeps reading its slice until 5,120 more tickets were taken from a pool of 4096: no lane may read a stamp another group wrote or zeroed, and every flag must be clear afterwards; (2) every flag holding a tag no newer than `DONE`, across the 32-bit wrap: all groups run; (3) every flag holding a newer tag: every group draws nothing and no byte of a slice changes; (4) a pool of 32 slices: the groups that run own their slices and the rest are counted. It skips when the GPU never let the pool's count of groups pass a running one. On an M3 Pro the ticket-modulo rule failed in the first draw (32 lanes). The host binding has no test of its own; it needs rebuilt shaders (the chain stamp changed with `dmsh_abi.h`).

### Mesh stage memory reserves the primitive index bytes for every primitive block

- **Symptom:** white blotches over Kokiri Forest's foliage, walkway and ground in Ocarina of Time, which are the house light tiles. The light-tile pass of task `a5288d0a6c4971fd` with meshes `e8c667282ba7bd9b` and `d4171cf9adf0504b` writes constant white through fragment `ea1bf96b72e72476`, and its triangles were corrupt.
- **Cause:** the guest writes the U8 index bytes of primitive `t = min(count - 1, lane + 32k)` for each of its primitive blocks, `ceil(max_p / 32)` of them. A meshlet count of 0 wraps `count - 1`, so all 128 lanes of a `max_p` 126 mesh write. With `max_p` 126 the index area was `4 + 378` bytes, so lanes 126 and 127 wrote bytes 2,702 to 2,707: word 676, past the 676-word threadgroup array, into neighbouring workgroups' memory. Meshes whose `max_p` is a multiple of 32 were never affected.
- **Fix:** `dmsh_meshstage.memory` reserves `4 + 3 * ceil(max_p / 32) * 32` bytes (rounded to a word) for the index bytes: whole primitive blocks, not the vertex blocks a mesh with more vertices than primitives has as well. Meshes of 128 vertices and 64 primitives did not write past `4 + 3 * 64` before the fix, so the guest's loop goes by primitive blocks. All 500 of Ocarina's mesh shaders were run standalone on zeroed inputs, each with a guard band past its stage array (Metal shader validation does not check static threadgroup arrays): 387 meshes wrote past the array before the fix, every `max_p` 126 mesh, and none after (pull request #1575, measured with every vertex and primitive block reserved; the primitive-block size was not run through the guard band). In the game on an M2 Pro the blotches are gone and the house doorways and windows glow as intended. This does not fix the intermittent GPU hangs seen in buffers with these meshes; those are a separate open issue.
- **Found on:** [Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md). The other mesh-shader games in the games table (Yoshi and the Mysterious Book, Mario Kart World, Splatoon Raiders) rebuild their shaders with the chain change; none was checked in the game.

### Mesh attributes: every vertex block before the primitive blocks

- **Symptom:** a mesh shader's per-primitive outputs read as other values once it has more than 32 vertices: Ocarina of Time's Poe Sisters and Phantom Ganon paintings (mesh shaders `ee4ef51a4fddc28e` and `83c0dbc51c5481e3`, 128 vertices, 64 primitives, a per-primitive colour) came out solid green or magenta (pull request #1590). A mesh shader that draws points with a point size takes the size as its position's x.
- **Cause:** the wrapper read the attribute area as blocks of 32 vertices and 32 primitives side by side, each block holding the vertex components and then the primitive components. The guest lays out all its vertex blocks first, then all its primitive blocks, each block only its own SPH's components: `ee4ef51a4fddc28e` (6 vertex components, 4 primitive components) writes vertex `v` at `(v / 32) * 768 + (v % 32) * 4` and primitive `p` at `3072 + (p / 32) * 512 + (p % 32) * 4` past the attribute area's start. The SPH's point size (byte 0x35 bit 3) takes the vertex block's first component, ahead of the position: Ocarina's point mesh `3a6340a04bf09797` writes the size at component 0 and the position at 1 to 4. A primitive's index bytes are control +0x5f of them (1 point, 2 line, 3 triangle); Ocarina's 5 mesh shaders with +0x5f 1 are the 5 with the point size, the other 495 have 3.
- **Fix:** `dmsh_meshstage.memory` and `to_stage` take the vertex blocks (`ceil(max_v / 32)`, the vertex components each) and then the primitive blocks (`ceil(max_p / 32)`, the primitive components each); the point size goes to `[[point_size]]`, the topology and the index bytes a primitive follow +0x5f, and any other value stops the shader's build. The primitive index bytes stay reserved for every primitive block ([Mesh stage memory reserves the primitive index bytes for every primitive block](#mesh-stage-memory-reserves-the-primitive-index-bytes-for-every-primitive-block)), `+0x5f` bytes a primitive.
- **Found on:** [Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md#found-on-this-game): the layout and the point size checked against the guest's address arithmetic in the translated shaders; the paintings not seen in the game since. Pull request #1590 found the layout and the point size.
- **Check:** `python3 dm/gfx/tests/test_dmsh_meshstage.py`.

### Mesh shader per-primitive system values: the primitive count overwritten, the primitive id lost

- **Symptom:** a mesh-shaded draw shows nothing, though its workgroups run: every workgroup ends with a primitive count of 0. Once that is mended, a visibility buffer written from `[[primitive_id]]` holds only the index within the meshlet, and each pixel is shaded from the wrong cluster.
- **Cause:** a mesh shader's per-primitive SPH can enable system-value outputs besides generic attributes: OMAP byte 0x34 and byte 0x35 bits 0..3, attribute 0x40 + 4 × bit (0x35 bit 0 is the primitive id; bit 1 the layer, bit 2 the viewport). `dmsh_meshstage.info` dropped them, so `memory` left them no room, and the primitive count and index bytes began where the guest keeps the per-primitive blocks: its store of primitive 0's id landed on the count byte. The wrapper also gave Metal no per-primitive id, so the fragment stage's `[[primitive_id]]` was Metal's own index, not the value NVN's `gl_PrimitiveID` reads (here `(cluster << 7) | primitive`).
- **Fix:** `info` lists the per-primitive system values (`primitive_system`, `primitive_builtins`); a primitive block holds them first, then the generic attributes, and the primitive bytes follow every block. The primitive id goes to Metal as the mesh primitive's `[[primitive_id]]` (`PRIMITIVE_BUILTINS`), which the fragment stage then reads; the same fragment function in a vertex pipeline still reads Metal's index. The layer and the viewport get room but no Metal output: no mesh shader is known to write them. Ported from uncraftablexd's PRAGMATA bring-up ([PR #1802](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/pull/1802)); its other part, a storage buffer table at `c0` + 0x210 for mesh shaders in the hull slot, is not taken, since `c[26]` there is the mesh stage's buffer bank, its push block 2 ([NVN2 graphics stages' buffer banks](#nvn2-graphics-stages-buffer-banks-c25-to-c29-are-push-blocks)).
- **Found on:** PRAGMATA: 494 of its 539 mesh shaders enable the primitive id, among them the title's lunar terrain (`5aab38bb5ba67ef7`, `28ef097c07ceb429`) and the visibility pass (`765e6d9d2588ce70`, `bc8b4e0fcbb79159`).
- **Check:** `python3 dm/gfx/tests/test_dmsh_meshstage.py`.

### Mesh shaders that also address their vertices as records

- **Symptom:** thin streaks or stray triangles from a mesh shader over parts of the picture its geometry does not cover (PRAGMATA: white vertical streaks over the cockpit window in the opening).
- **Cause:** besides the SoA blocks (`ISBEWR.O.ATTR.SKEW.32`, mode 0xf8), a mesh shader may write its vertices as records (`ISBEWR.O.ATTR.32`, mode 0xb8): vertex `v`'s `n` components at `aos + 4n * v + 4c`, `aos` being the end of its SoA area, with the same values as their SoA words. The translator took mode 0xb8 as a word at the outputs' base, so the records ran over the primitive count and the index bytes.
- **Fix:** `dmsh_meshstage.memory` passes `stage_memory[4..6]` (the records' start, `n`, their size); in a mesh shader `AStageOut` and `AIsberd` (`InstEmitAmpereMem.cs` `AosToSoa`) send a mode 0xb8 address inside the records to the SoA word of the same vertex and component, so both writes coincide. `v = w / n` is a reciprocal multiply where that is exact for every word of the records (`AosVertex` checks it), a division otherwise. Ported from [PR #1802](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/pull/1802).
- **Found on:** PRAGMATA: `6adf14e9a4100082`, `4fcffc73d65dc1ed`, `c0e44513c35d209a`.
- **Check:** `DMSH_TEST_XLATE=<translator> python3 dm/gfx/tests/test_dmsh_ampere_mem.py` (`test_vertex_records_go_to_their_soa_word`).

### Mesh shaders that read their own outputs back

- **Symptom:** `NotSupportedException: Ampere 923 …: ISBERD mode f8 in a task or mesh shader` in the shader build; those mesh shaders are left out and their draws skipped (`[RED] DrawMeshTasks without its task and mesh shaders`).
- **Cause:** ISBERD's mode bits as nvdisasm names them: 0x80 .O (the output area), 0x40 .SKEW, 0x30 .ATTR, 0x08 .32. Modes 0xf8 and 0xb8 read back a word the shader's own ISBEWR of the same mode wrote; the translator took only 0xb8, as a task's payload.
- **Fix:** `AIsberd` reads both from the stage memory's outputs, 0xb8 through the vertex records (previous entry). From uncraftablexd's PRAGMATA bring-up ([PR #1407](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/pull/1407)).
- **Found on:** PRAGMATA: `5aab38bb5ba67ef7` and `28ef097c07ceb429`, the title's lunar terrain, read back each triangle's positions to swap two indices of a mirrored instance's triangles.

### Subword task and mesh payload accesses

- **Symptom:** task and mesh shaders fail translation with `ISBERD mode 34 in a task or mesh shader` or `stage output store mode b4 outside a task or mesh shader`; the host cannot load their libraries when the race uses them.
- **Cause:** the stage-memory emitters accepted only 32-bit attributes and payload words. Bits 74–75 of ISBERD/ISBEWR select unsigned 8-bit, unsigned 16-bit or 32-bit access, independently of the attribute, skew and output bits ([Mesa NAK's encoder](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/chaotic-cx/mesa-mirror/main/src/nouveau/compiler/nak/sm70_encode.rs), `OpIsberd` / `OpIsbewr`). Mode 0x34 reads a 16-bit task-payload entry; 0xb4 writes one. Treating it as a whole word would overwrite the adjacent entry.
- **Fix:** `AIsberd` and `AStageOut` decode width separately from the stage-memory area and use the existing byte/halfword load and store lowering. Subword vertex-record accesses preserve the byte offset within the remapped SoA word. The existing 32-bit paths are unchanged; there is no shader-key or build gate.
- **Check:** `test_dmsh_ampere_mem.py` checks unsigned input extraction, the high half of a word, subword vertex records and correctly sized store helpers. `test_dmsh_shared_store.py` follows a translated halfword store through the Metal rewrite and tests neighboring byte/halfword writes on the GPU.
- **Found on:** Fast Fusion.

### Indexed texture handles in NVN2 compute c0

- **Symptom:** a shader library builds but cannot load: `reflection conversion failed: B binding cp_t2d_cb196608_10568000 has no resolved slot number`, followed by `the dispatched program has no compute shader`. The first race's sky is black.
- **Cause:** NVN2 compute places texture handles in a per-shader c0 layout. Fixed reads already pass through `AMapC0` into the canonical c2 driver tables, but an indexed LDC keeps its native bank and byte offset. `BindlessToArray` turned that read into an array of a game's uniform-buffer handles instead; native c0 has no such uniform slot.
- **Fix:** when the texture-array pass finds an indexed native compute c0 sampled-texture handle, it uses the control section's existing `QueryAmpereComputeC0Layout` metadata to map the recovered index into the canonical c2 combined or separate-texture table. A fixed separate sampler keeps its own c2 word. Reflection and replay then use their existing driver-table array path. Storage images, other constant-buffer banks and shaders without an NVN2 compute layout retain their previous lowering.
- **Check:** `test_dmsh_nvn2_buffers.py` covers indexed combined handles and separate textures ORed with a fixed sampler; both declare c2 resources and retain no c0 read. The before/after shader-source comparison and rendered result are in the game's note.
- **Found on:** Fast Fusion.

### Threadgroup stores in warp-synchronous code

- **Symptom:** a small scan or sort in a compute shader gives wrong results every frame, with no barrier in its loop (Orbitals: Niagara's sorted particle indices); or a compute shader's output differs from one run to the next on the same input, in a few pixels of each tile (agl's NDSR upscaler network in Ocarina of Time: about 0.1 % of its 2560x1440 pixels).
- **Cause:** Ampere compilers rely on lockstep warps and leave the barrier out of small scans: a thread stores its word to shared memory, then loads its neighbour's. For one thread the load and its own earlier store do not alias, so Metal may move the load above the store and read the neighbour's old value. A memory fence orders a thread's own accesses only: on Apple's GPUs it does not make another lane's threadgroup store visible to this lane's load. agl's NDSR network `fa3033d05c0a41d6` exchanges the last tile of its epilogue through shared memory behind one `dmsh_tg_fence()`: run twice on the same input (a harness with the game's push constants and weights, M2 Pro), 2,000 to 3,750 of its 3,686,400 output pixels differed, at the same few places of each 64x32 tile. A threadgroup-scope fence there did not help; a SIMD-group barrier did (0 differences, repeated).
- **Fix:** the translator's `SharedMemoryFence` pass (`Translation/Transforms/SharedMemoryFence.cs`, after the lowering passes) calls the helper `dmsh_tg_fence()` before each shared memory load that some path reaches from a shared memory store with no barrier or fence in between. It is a forward pass over the function's blocks to a fixed point, so a store at the bottom of a loop reaches the load at its top, and a store on one branch reaches a load after the join. One fence orders every store before it, so a run of stores takes one fence. Stores at the end of the shader take none: a compute kernel reads nothing after them, and a task or mesh shader's wrapper puts a barrier after its body. Stage memory, which lies in shared memory, is treated as shared memory. `tg_fence` in `dmsh_rewrite.py` makes the helper's body a simdgroup-scope memory fence (`atomic_thread_fence(mem_flags::mem_threadgroup, …, thread_scope_simdgroup)`). That is not an execution barrier, so it is safe in divergent code. A kernel whose arrays spill to device memory gets the device scope too (`tg_spill`). In a compute kernel (`tg_fence(…, kernel=True)`; not a task or mesh shader, whose body `dmsh_meshstage` wraps later) a call at the top level of `main0`'s body with no `return` before it is `simdgroup_barrier(mem_flags::mem_threadgroup)` instead: every lane of the SIMD-group reaches it, and it is the barrier that makes the other lanes' stores visible. A call in a branch, a loop or a helper function, or after a return, keeps the fence. The build's report counts both (`warp-synchronous shared memory`). On `fa3033d05c0a41d6` (its one call is at the top level) the barrier costs nothing measurable (4.51 ms in the harness either way, against the order of the runs' own 0.3 ms). From uncraftablexd's Orbitals bring-up ([PR #1775](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/pull/1775)). Its first form, a fence after every threadgroup store in the Metal source, gave Ocarina of Time 25,108 fences in 699 shaders and crashed Apple's compiler on an M2 (next entry).
- **Found on:** Orbitals (no profile here), by PR #1775; taken for PRAGMATA's GPU-driven passes. The barrier: [Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md#found-on-this-game)'s NDSR upscaler network.
- **Check:** `DMSH_TEST_XLATE=<translator> python3 dm/gfx/tests/test_dmsh_tg_fence.py` (placement through the translator; the rewrite alone without it).

### A fence after every shared memory store crashes Apple's compiler

- **Symptom:** `[RED] task / mesh pipeline prewarm failed: task … mesh … fragment …: … XPC_ERROR_CONNECTION_INTERRUPTED` once per run, at the prewarm before the first frame; `MTLCompilerService` crashes with `EXC_BAD_ACCESS` in `llvm::MachineOperand::setReg` under `AGCModule<AGX::Impl::MeshProgramKey>::compileLLVMObject`. Runs with the fences also hung on the GPU more often (7 of 9 long runs against 0 of 3), which is suggestive, not proven.
- **Cause:** the first form of the previous entry's fix put a simdgroup fence after every store to a threadgroup array: 17,641 in Ocarina of Time's 500 mesh shaders (most after the stage output writes, which nothing reads before the wrapper's barrier), 85 in its 20 task shaders and 7,382 in 179 compute shaders. Apple's GPU compiler on an M2 (macOS 27.0, compiler 32023.922) crashed on one foliage mesh pipeline in every run; the same Metal source with only those fences removed compiled. An M4 Pro on macOS 27.2, and `metal-tt -arch applegpu_g14g`, compiled main's fenced pipeline, so the crash is in the M2's system compiler.
- **Fix:** fences only where a load follows a store with no barrier between, decided in the translator (previous entry). Ocarina of Time's 699 shaders went from 25,108 fences to 1,566: none in its 500 mesh shaders (the foliage mesh shader `f85bb2e8d78b864c` among them), 1 in its task shaders, 1,565 in its compute shaders, 1,030 of them in two particle shaders (`agl_technique_pfx`) that store and load the same array in a chain of branches. Before that the pass counted each call of the HMMA helper as a store, and Ocarina's movie networks took a fence before every HMMA of a chain (15,536).
- **Found on:** [Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md): task `a7f22b4a795ff4d1`, mesh `f85bb2e8d78b864c`, fragment `72fba2ec8558f6d4` (Foliage.Product.110, var0), reported on an M2 MacBook Air in [GitHub issue #2481](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/issues/2481).
- **Check:** `DMSH_TEST_XLATE=<translator> python3 dm/gfx/tests/test_dmsh_tg_fence.py`; on an M2, the prewarm log has no such line.

### Ampere shader code (Switch 2)

- **Symptom:** a Switch 2 game's shaders do not translate (the translator reads Maxwell: 64-bit instructions behind scheduling words, a 0x50-byte SPH), or, rewritten as Maxwell code, UI text draws as solid boxes or not at all.
- **Cause:** NVN2 ships Ampere (sm_86) code: 128-bit instructions, a 0x80-byte SPH (version 4; its first 0x50 bytes keep version 3's layout), uniform registers UR0–UR62 and uniform predicates, convergence barriers (BSSY / BSYNC) instead of the SSY / SYNC stack, CALL / RET with the return offset in a register, and scoreboards: a variable-latency instruction (one that sets a write barrier, control bits 110..113) writes its registers when its result arrives, and an instruction that does not wait on that barrier (wait mask, bits 116..122) reads the old values. A rewrite into Maxwell code got several meanings wrong: IPA (0x326, 0x526) is IPA.PASS and the shader multiplies by 1 / a[0x7c] itself; IPA's indexed form takes its index from a uniform register; TEX's bit 79 (`.F16`) packs the results as half pairs; an `LDG.128 R0, [R0.64]` must read its address once.
- **Fix:** the translator decodes Ampere natively (`TranslationFlags.Ampere`, job `arch: sm86`; `Decoders/DecoderAmpere.cs`, `Instructions/InstEmitAmpere*.cs`) into the shared IR. Uniform registers and predicates are register types of their own; a CALL into code with no reachable RET is a jump; BRX targets come from the c1 jump table its LDC reads (BRXU's from the uniform LDC of its uniform register), up to the next table another BRX of the program reads: the compiler puts a shader's switch tables back to back in c1, and read on to the end, Donkey Kong Bananza's `SilverStarMaterial` vertex shaders took every later switch's targets, 400 and more nested comparisons, past the Metal compiler's 256 nested brackets (11 shaders). An instruction with a write barrier writes into shadow registers that are copied to its destinations before the first instruction that waits on the barrier, at DEPBAR and at the end of the block (`Translator.EmitOpsAmpere`). Field positions follow Mesa NAK's SM70 encoder, CuAssembler's sm_86 tables and the facts of other projects (Eglantine). Maxwell code takes the old path, unchanged (byte-identical SPIR-V on 7,168 shaders).
  - Semantics that differ from Maxwell: `MUFU.SIN` / `COS` take the angle in turns (the compiler multiplies by 1 / 2π first; Maxwell's came through RRO), of which they use the fraction ([MUFU.SIN and MUFU.COS of a large number of turns](#mufusin-and-mufucos-of-a-large-number-of-turns)); bit 76 of FMUL / FFMA is .FMZ (a product with a zero factor is zero).
  - Warp-cooperative instructions (`InstEmitAmpereTensor.cs`): HMMA (m16n8k8 / m16n8k16, F16 sources, F16 or F32 accumulator; in a compute shader, m16n8k16 with an F16 accumulator goes through Apple's matrix hardware instead: [Ampere HMMA through simdgroup_matrix](#ampere-hmma-through-simdgroup_matrix)), LDSM (1, 2 or 4 8×8 matrices, plain or transposed) and MOVM run per thread: each lane gathers the fragments it needs from the lanes that hold them with subgroup shuffles (Metal's SIMD-groups are 32 wide, as the warp), in the layouts NVIDIA documents for PTX `mma.m16n8k16`, `ldmatrix` and `movmatrix`. HSETP2, HSET2 and HMNMX2 are the packed-half compares; MATCH (.ANY / .ALL, 32- or 64-bit) compares a value across the warp with a ballot of the active lanes and 32 shuffles, and REDUX reduces one into a uniform register the same way.
  - UP2UR takes any mask: bit k of the result is UPk where bit k of the mask is set, as P2R does (Mario Kart World's pixel shaders save UP3 or UP6 with masks 8 and 0x40 and test the register against URZ later). SURED (0x59f) is SUATOM's layout with no result; only SURED.ADD.U32 has been seen (Mario Kart World's `ab77eaee44cfef88`, adding 1 at a 2D coordinate), and another form is refused.
  - Geometry stage: OUT (0x124, emit / cut; none is OUTFINAL) and ISBERD (the vertex index passes through, as on Maxwell). A geometry shader that ends through an exit stub sets it up with LEPC and 0x357, and the stub runs OUTFINAL and 0x957 before its EXIT; per thread there is nothing to finish, so LEPC writes its own address and 0x357 / 0x957 do nothing. This is the translator's (nxnative's geometry lowering used it); the chain translates no geometry shader ([A shader the chain does not translate stops the split and the build](#a-shader-the-chain-does-not-translate-stops-the-split-and-the-build)).
  - NVN2 compute lays out `c[0]` per shader, from the control section: from 0x10 its push constants (+0xa9, in words), then its uniform buffer entries (+0xb1 of them) and storage buffer entries (+0xb2), 0x10 bytes each, then its texture handles (+0xb3), [separate texture and sampler handles](#nvn2-computes-separate-handles-in-c0) and image handles, 8 bytes each; the u16 at +0xba is the total (checked on all 205 of Donkey Kong Bananza's compute shaders). `dmsh_build.py` passes the counts (job `nvn2_cs`) and the frontend maps each range onto the tables the host already fills (`AMapC0`): push constants to `c[24]` (`DMSH_PUSH_BANK`), uniform entries to the compute uniform table, storage entries to the compute storage table, handles to `c[2]`. Graphics stages keep NVN's fixed tables. The push constants themselves come from `nvnCommandBufferPushGraphicsConstants` / `PushComputeConstants` (`OP_PUSH_CONST`, a `DMSH_PUSH_SIZE` block a stage group).
  - A storage address added from two constant buffer values (a `c[0]` entry plus an offset from `c[1]`, by UIMAD.WIDE) put the `c[1]` term second, and `GlobalToStorage` took the second operand as the base; it now prefers the driver segment's operand when only one is from it (Maxwell output unchanged, 28,669 shaders).
- **Found on:** Donkey Kong Bananza: 22,751 of 22,822 shaders translate; its menu renders, and its gameplay, including agl's upscaler network (`agl_ndsr.pck`, 24 compute shaders with HMMA).
- **Resolved:** 0x593 is SUATOM, an image atomic through a handle (`ASuatom`, `InstEmitAmpereMem.cs`): SUST's handle (40..59) and dimension (61..64), ATOMG's operation (87..91), size (73..76) and predicate result (81..83, PT here). Its two shaders, agl's `agl_technique_pfx` compute shaders, take an append counter with it (SUATOM.ADD.U32 on a 1D image, the old value shuffled to the warp, then their SUST), and now translate: all 22,852 of the package's shaders do. Neither has been met in play so far, so the picture is not checked; `test_dmsh_ampere_mem.py` translates the encoding. A handle in another bank or a predicate result is refused.
- **Open:** loads and stores through addresses read from memory, which had no storage buffer to become, go through the memory pools now ([Global memory at an address loaded from memory](#global-memory-at-an-address-loaded-from-memory)).

### Storage words read one by one

- **Symptom:** a lit pass far slower on an M1 than the M4 Pro's figures suggest, the time spread over every material that loops over the scene's lights; dropping one material's draws (`DM_NVN_UB`) saved only its share.
- **Cause:** Ampere reads a storage buffer 64 or 128 bits at a time (LDG.E.64 / .128). The translator splits each read into 32-bit LoadGlobal operations, and spirv-cross writes each word with an index of its own, `uint(int(uint(base + 4) >> uint(2)))`. Metal's front end cannot tell such words are neighbours (each index is the shift of its own sum, then widened to 64 bits), so every word stays a load of its own with its own address arithmetic: about 240 in Splatoon Raiders' Hoian_UBER, most of them in its light loop (80 bytes a light).
- **Fix:** `wide_loads` (`dm/gfx/tools/dmsh_wide.py`, a step of `dmsh_build.py` after `pool_arrays`): the words of one read-only (`const device`) buffer at byte offsets k, k+4, … from one value, with no brace between them, become one read of a `packed_uint2/3/4` at the first word, each word taking its lane. In the AIR that read is still one load per word, but each at a constant offset from one address, so the words share the address arithmetic they each had; computing the index once in the translator (`(base >> 2) + i`, unsigned or signed) leaves every word its own widened index and gains nothing. A packed vector needs 4-byte alignment only, and the shader does not write the buffer through that binding. A run ends at any line that may write memory or change a value (a store through a subscript or a pointer, an atomic, a barrier, an image write, an assignment to an existing variable), so a write through another binding to the same memory cannot fall between its words; the values are the same. In TotK's 28,669 and Wonder's 16,266 built shaders that rule left every run as it was. A run starts on a 16-byte step of the common value and holds at most four words, as the original reads did.
- **Found on:** Splatoon Raiders: 4,558 of its 27,155 shaders change. On an M1 in the opening dialogue scene, the lit pass's median GPU time went from 70.2 to 15.6 ms and the frame's from 91.5 to 30.1 ms (`DM_NVN_GPUPROF=every:60`, 15 and 35 profiled frames in the scene); the 40 shaders that did not build before are the only ones that fail.
- **Check:** `python3 dm/gfx/tests/test_dmsh_wide.py`.

### Device loads from read-only storage buffers

- **Symptom:** fragment shaders spend GPU time on device loads from storage buffers whose addresses are uniform across the draw.
- **Cause:** SPIRV-Cross emits `const device` storage pointers for shaders without writes through buffers. Metal can use constant registers for uniform addresses when the pointer has the `constant` address space.
- **Fix:** `constant_reads` runs after `wide_loads`. It requires read-only storage metadata from the translator and accepts direct word reads and the packed loads `wide_loads` emits. A buffer or element pointer passed to another operation keeps its original address space, as does a shader with a device reference. The argument ID and the host binding are the same. Fragment stages are the default. `DMSH_SCONST=0` disables the pass for comparison and `DMSH_SCONST=all` enables the other eligible stage prefixes. The option is part of the chain and shader cache keys.
- **Found on:** Ocarina of Time on an M2 Air. In the PR author's paired run against the same cut9p test build, GPU encoder time fell from 20.43 to 19.82 ms per frame. The hottest fragment shader was 17.1% faster in isolation with bit-identical output. [Game note](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md#found-on-this-game), [PR #2889](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/pull/2889)
- **Check:** `test_dmsh_wide.py` covers write metadata, stage selection, escaped pointers and references, and Metal compilation of promoted packed loads with and without bounds checks.

## Bindings and handles

- **Note:** the layout of NVN's driver segments `c0` and `c2` (the handle tables, the storage and uniform buffer tables, the words the host fills) and the binding kinds of the reflection JSON have one definition, `dm/gfx/nvn/dmsh_abi.h`. The host includes it (`nvn_mtl.m`, `nvn_refl.m`, `nvn_obj.h`); `dmsh_build.py` reads it through `dm/gfx/tools/dmsh_abi.py`, which stops on any line it cannot read. Both files are part of the chain version. `dm/tools/tests/test_dmsh_abi.py` checks that the tables fit together, that the host converts every kind the build may write, that the translator's own copy of the `c2` layout (`Program.cs`) agrees, and that the values do not come back as literals in the host or the build. The entries below cite the offsets.

### Images and buffer textures with their handle in a uniform buffer

- **Symptom:** `reflection conversion failed: B binding cp_i_cb3_0_ has no resolved slot number` at load, then `the dispatched program has no compute shader`; in the build, `SUATOM handle in bank 3` or `SURED handle in bank 3`, and bindings of an unrecognized kind for a typed buffer. Compute passes are skipped.
- **Cause:** the compiler reads a texture handle from wherever the game put it, and bindless textures in uniform buffers were already [read at bind time](#storage-buffer-addresses-in-the-games-own-uniform-buffer). Images and buffer textures were not, as no game had done it: Unreal Engine 5.4 for the Switch 2 keeps every one in `c[3]`, uniform slot 0 (about 87,000 SURED, 15,000 SULD and 121,000 SUST in one game's shaders, none of them in another bank).
- **Fix:** binding kinds `img_ubo` and `tbuf_ubo` (`dmsh_abi.h`), `NVN_RB_IMGU` and `NVN_RB_TBUFU` in the host, which read the handle as `NVN_RB_TEXU` does and otherwise bind as an image slot or a typed buffer: usage upgrades for written images (`image_prepare`), the uniform shadow check, and the pass logic that treats any bindless shader cautiously. In the translator SUATOM and SURED pass their bank on. With no descriptor to ask at build time, an atomic takes its format from the instruction's size (`UniformHandleAtomicFormat`).
- **Found on:** Yoshi and the Mysterious Book.

### Texture and sampler words in either order

- **Symptom:** a scene lit black with correct geometry. In `DM_NVN_PASSDUMP`, the lighting shader's bindings show `tex … tid=0 dummy` where the G-buffer should be, and a sampler bound where a texture is expected.
- **Cause:** a handle from two words (`fp_t_cb262147_10020016`: words 0x16 and 8, handle type 1) is the OR of a texture and a sampler word, and the GPU does not care which comes first. The build took the first as the texture and the second as the sampler. Unreal Engine 5.4's lighting shaders read a shared sampler's word first, so each G-buffer texture came out as ID 0.
- **Fix:** each half of such a binding carries the other word (`soff`, `sreg`), and the host ORs the two as the GPU does (`ubo_bind_handle`). A shader whose order was as assumed gets the same handle.
- **Found on:** Yoshi and the Mysterious Book: its lighting, with this as the last step from a black scene to a lit one.

### Compute texture handles loaded through uniform addresses

- **Symptom:** `Failed to find handle source for bindless access of type "textureBuffer".` in compute shaders; the build reports deleted reads whose results become zero.
- **Cause:** compute code can load its handle words through the uniform addresses in the canonical c0 table (`DMSH_C0_CS_UBO`), instead of direct constant-buffer instructions. `GlobalToStorage` lowers these loads to storage-buffer operations before `BindlessElimination`, which recognized only direct constant-buffer handle words. A two-word handle then lost its known uniform origin and its buffer fetch was deleted.
- **Fix:** `ResourceManager.TryGetComputeUniformSlot` recovers the uniform slot from the storage binding's descriptor origin. `BindlessElimination.UniformHandleWord` resolves a fixed word in a representable game-uniform slot to the existing constant-buffer binding metadata, retaining both words of an ORed handle.
