- **Symptom:** after a shader input changes, the Mac app build recompiles the affected library but reports `prewarm pipelines: reused (inputs unchanged)`, retaining pipelines made with the previous shader functions.
- **Cause:** the prewarm cache key read `b-<chain>/inputs.tsv` with a normal filesystem path. A full shader build packs that directory into `b-<chain>.img`, so the stamp was silently replaced with an empty string in the key. A profile input such as `dual_want.tsv` changes functions without changing the chain, shader keys or recorded pipeline list.
- **Fix:** `build_app.py` reads the stamp through `shader_lib.view`, as other library readers do. Both folder and image stamps invalidate the captured prewarm pipelines when shader inputs change; identical inputs still reuse them.
- **Check:** `test_nsp_mac_app.py` exercises unchanged and changed stamps in both storage forms. Its image case reproduced reuse before the fix.
- **Found on:** Sid Meier's Civilization VII, adding the second dual-source shader during bring-up.

### Python memory during offline pipeline archive preparation

- **Symptom:** `pso_offline.py` holds about 1.8 GB while `metal-tt` builds an archive of 140,036 bases, and the system's memory view shows the build far larger than that (180.5 GB) before `metal-tt` fails or is stopped.
- **Cause:** the builder made the pipeline script (503 MB at 140,036 bases) as one JSON string and as bytes, read the previous script whole to compare them, and serialized the whole key input once more; the descriptors stayed referenced while `metal-tt` ran. That is at most about 1.8 GB: the 180.5 GB were `metal-tt` and its compiler processes, not Python.
- **Fix:** `write_script` streams the script to `bases.mtl4-json.partial` and replaces the previous script only when the two differ; `archive_key` hashes the same JSON as it is encoded (the keys of earlier builds still match); the descriptors are released before `metal-tt` starts. Measured with 140,036 synthetic bases: peak 1.83 → 0.45 GB, 1.81 → 0.06 GB held while `metal-tt` runs, about 11 s more encoding (Python 3.15), byte-identical script and key.
- **Found on:** Mario Party Superstars ([GitHub issue #1022](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/issues/1022), [PR #1024](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/pull/1024)). Open: why `metal-tt` failed after 76 minutes there, and how its memory grows with the number of bases.
- **Check:** `dm/tools/tests/test_pso_offline.py` (`test_large_script_memory_and_compatible_archive_key`).

### Bounded groups for the offline pipeline compiler

- **Symptom:** the offline archive stops with only `applegpu-nt command failed`, sometimes after `Max Unstruct ID` lines ([a note, not the failure](#front-end-at--o1-apples-control-flow-lowering)); the macOS kernel log reports `no paging space action` and kills `air-nt` as the largest compressed process.
- **Cause:** Apple's GPU compiler can exhaust memory while compiling the complete pipeline list. Lowering the Python builder's memory does not bound the compiler's. RSS excludes compressed and swapped allocations: use kernel resource-kill diagnostics and physical-footprint measurements to distinguish this failure from a compiler rejection or a full disk.
- **Fix:** with more bases than `--batch-size` (default 1,000; 0: one compiler invocation), `pso_offline.py` compiles sequential groups of at most that many, each script referencing only its libraries, then merges all results with `metal-pack -nthreads 1`. The normal single-file output, full-input archive key and runtime stamp stay compatible. Successful groups are cached by their own descriptors, library bytes, GPU, OS and compiler. A failed group or merge publishes neither a new complete archive key nor coverage stamp, and keeps the groups already done. The groups stay in the work folder (`batches.<arch>/`, 1.2 GB for Odyssey), so a run after a failure or after a change to some libraries compiles only what changed. This bounds input count, not compiler memory; an individual pipeline or the final merge may still exhaust resources.
- **Found on:** Super Mario Odyssey, which records the resource-kill evidence, before/after measurements and complete archive validation. [Issue #1022](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/issues/1022) requests archive partitioning for another game's resource-heavy build; that workload remains unverified.
- **Check:** `dm/tools/tests/test_pso_offline.py` covers the default, pilot reuse, compatible full-archive reuse, partial final batches, changed-group invalidation, compile failure and merge failure. `t_pso_bundle` compiles one base per group, opens the merged result, retrieves a base from each group, rejects an absent depth entry and compares the complete coverage stamp with the ordinary build.

### A base the GPU compiler cannot encode cost the whole offline archive

- **Symptom:** `[RED] pso_offline: metal-tt failed for applegpu_… (exit 1): LLVM ERROR: Failed to encode.`, and the NSP script's `[RED] pipeline archive: pso_offline.py failed …; the app is packed without one and builds its pipeline bases on its first start`. On a run from the build folder: `[RED] pipeline prebuild: the base of … failed: Compilation failed due to an interrupted connection: XPC_ERROR_CONNECTION_INTERRUPTED`, a `MTLCompilerService` crash report whose stack ends in a fatal error under `llvm::MCObjectStreamer::emitInstruction` (`Error message SHA256: 01fbda05…`, the hash of `Failed to encode.`), and the compiles in flight when the service ends failing with it.
- **Cause:** Apple's GPU compiler (the same code in `MTLCompilerService` and in `metal-tt`'s `applegpu-nt`) stops with a fatal error on one function, and `metal-tt` ends its whole run on it, so one base took the archive of every other base with it: `compile_archive` stopped the build. What the compiler stops on is in its instruction encoder and is not ours to fix; what is known of the trigger, from PRAGMATA's pixel shader `c4ac3ca598cea32e` (macOS 27.2, toolchain 32023.922):
  - every architecture tried up to `applegpu_g17` fails (g15s, g16s, g16g, g17s, g17p, and g18p for iOS), for a macOS 26 or 27 target; `applegpu_g18m` compiles it;
  - the vertex function has no part (a trivial one fails the same way);
  - the library compiled with `-O0` builds its pipeline. Of the front end's passes (`-mllvm -opt-bisect-limit`), everything up to SROA is fine and the first EarlyCSE on the shader's body makes it fail; with `metal-opt`, `sroa,early-cse`, `sroa,gvn` and `sroa,newgvn` fail while `sroa,instcombine`, `sroa,simplifycfg` and `sroa,instcombine,simplifycfg,gvn,instcombine` do not;
  - it is the merging of repeated loads: with every load marked volatile for the pass (and unmarked again) it compiles, and three loads are enough to bring it back, those of one uniform word tested in two neighbouring blocks (`as_type<int>(c10[2].w) & 1`, once as a condition several later branches use, once for a `? -1 : 0` mask);
  - (found since: the merged load leaves one compare of bit 0 with two users, the mask and the branches, and how many uniforms come before it decides the failure; see [A jump Apple's GPU compiler cannot encode](#a-jump-apples-gpu-compiler-cannot-encode-a-tested-bit-0s-mask))
  - it needs the rest of the function around it: reductions of the source and of the IR that keep the failure stop at about 780 statements and 1,300 instructions with nothing left to name, the same pattern written by hand compiles, and so do synthetic shaders with thousands of statements, uniform reads or an early return.
- **Fix:** `pso_offline.py` finds the pipelines `metal-tt` cannot compile and leaves them out (`compile_isolating`): when a group fails with room on the volume, it is halved until single pipelines fail alone, those are named in a red line each and written to `rest_pairs.tsv`, which the host already builds on the device beside a bundled archive (and whose pairs its probe of the archive skips), and the rest is compiled. A group in which nothing compiles, a failure that does not repeat on the pipeline alone, or more than `MAX_FAILED` (8) failures in one group stop the build as before. A reused archive still names them (`failed.<arch>.tsv` beside its key). On the device such a pair fails in the prebuild, is recorded (`prebuild_failed.tsv`) and left to the draws.
- **Open:** a draw with such a shader still has no pipeline. PRAGMATA's `c4ac3ca598cea32e`, the one shader known to hit it, now compiles: its trigger was found and is rewritten ([A jump Apple's GPU compiler cannot encode](#a-jump-apples-gpu-compiler-cannot-encode-a-tested-bit-0s-mask)); this entry stays the safety net for any other. The general way out would be for the build to try each library's functions through `applegpu-nt` (about 0.2 s each) and compile the ones that fail again without the front end's optimizer; not written: it adds minutes to every game's build for one shader that no draw has been seen to use.
- **Found on:** PRAGMATA: three of its 48,957 bases, all with `c4ac3ca598cea32e`. Measured on 304 of its pairs for `applegpu_g16s` in groups of 100: the three named, 290 bases archived, 74 s.
- **Check:** `python3 -m unittest dm/tools/tests/test_pso_offline.py -k failing -k cannot_compile`.

### A failed pipeline archive stops the whole build

- **Symptom:** `--platform mac-app` or the iPhone build stops at its pipeline archive stage, after every earlier stage has finished: `[RED] pso_offline: metal-tt failed for applegpu_<gpu> (exit 1)`, then `pso_offline.py exit code 1`.
- **Cause:** the archive is an optimization (without one the device builds the bases on its first start, as with `--gpu none`), but the NSP script and `build_ipa.sh` treated its failure as fatal. Whatever made it fail, such as [memory](#bounded-groups-for-the-offline-pipeline-compiler) or a [full disk](#offline-pipeline-archive-on-a-full-disk), lost the whole build.
- **Fix:** both remove the unfinished archive folder, print a `[RED] pipeline archive: pso_offline.py failed` line and pack the app without one. The NSP script ends the stage with a warning (`⚠` in the console, `warn: true` in its JSON event). `pso_offline.py` run by itself still exits non-zero.
- **Found on:** Super Mario Odyssey 1.4.1 on an M1 (`applegpu_g13g`), [LNPANDY/NX2Apple#98](https://github.com/LNPANDY/NX2Apple/issues/98), through [PR #494](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/pull/494).
- **Check:** `dm/tools/tests/test_nsp_mac_app.py` (`test_pso_archive_failure_is_a_warning`, `test_ipa_build_says_its_archive_failed`), `dm/tools/tests/test_pso_offline.py` (`IpaFallback`), `dm/tools/tests/test_nsp_progress.py` (`test_warned_stage_end`).

### Offline pipeline archive on a full disk

- **Symptom:** `[RED] pso_offline: metal-tt failed for applegpu_<gpu> (exit 1)` with only `applegpu-nt command failed` in `metal-tt.<arch>.log`.
- **Cause:** `metal-tt` writes one `.airp` per base and its `translate-*` folders in `TMPDIR` (the work folder), and it does not say when the volume is full. Cruis'n Blast's 33,858 bases failed with 366 MB free. A failed run also left its temporary files in `pso/mac-work`.
- **Fix:** `pso_offline.py` clears those temporary files and stops before `metal-tt` when the work folder's volume has less than about 96 KB per base free (2 GB at least), and names the free space. When `metal-tt` still fails, it clears the temporary files and says when the volume is nearly full.
- **Found on:** Cruis'n Blast (0100B41013C82000, version 1.0.7; no profile in this repository), on a Mac with 366 MB free.
- **Check:** `dm/tools/tests/test_pso_offline.py` (`test_low_disk_space_stops_before_metal_tt`).

### Compute pipelines compiled at first use

- **Symptom:** a game without a recorded prewarm list (a provisional profile) stalls at its first dispatches while Metal compiles their compute pipelines. The Mac app's pipeline archive held render bases only (`archive.tsv`: `bases`), whatever the shader map listed.
- **Cause:** a compute pipeline was only ever built on the device, through the `MTLDevice` (`newComputePipelineStateWithFunction`), at its first dispatch or from the prewarm's `C` rows, and a provisional profile's `pso_manifest.tsv` has none. Yet such a pipeline depends on nothing but its function: the host passes no threadgroup size or other option (the dispatch gives the threadgroup size), so it can be compiled offline for every compute shader of the shader map.
- **Fix:** `pso_offline.py` adds a compute pipeline descriptor that names the function alone for the `<key>_b` of every compute shader: the prewarm list's `C` rows first, then the map's rows of stage `compute`; shaders with function constants are left out (the host specializes them). They compile in groups of their own (`batches.<arch>/c<index>/`, 16 a group, `--jobs` groups at a time), cached by their inputs and merged with the bases. The host looks `<key>_b` up in the bundled archive first (`cps_new`) and builds it on a miss; the prewarm's last lines count both.
- **Found on:** Star Fox (v131072, with a provisional profile before this repository had one), chain `ae59da13e481`, on an M5 Pro, measured on a fork with other host changes: 233 compute shaders give 233 compute pipelines beside 3 bases, a 35 MB archive. Built in 16 groups, 7 at a time, in 268 s, against 453 s as one `metal-tt` run (842 and 829 s of CPU; both at background priority, with load averages of 19-26 from other jobs); unchanged inputs reuse it in 0.3 s. Two headless boots of 90 s to the title took all 7 of their compute pipelines from it and built none, with no watchdog stop. Also [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md#found-on-this-game) (v131072, chain `f9f01e4f9994`, `applegpu_g18g`): 4,125 compute pipelines (480 MB, 204 s), and all 103 compute pipelines of a route to the village came from the bundle with none built; PR #1522 measured what the archive saves with the Metal cache cold, 195 s of submit-thread pipelines (155 s in the title movie's twelve kernels) to 0.9 s.
- **Check:** `dm/tools/tests/test_pso_offline.py` (`Compute`, among them `test_pipelines_from_a_library_image` for one-file libraries, `test_compute_pipelines_are_groups_of_their_own`, `test_compute_pipelines_alone_make_an_archive`); `t_pso_bundle` builds an archive with compute shaders and checks that a map's shaders come from it and that a shader with constants or outside the map is built.

### Compute pipelines outside the bundled archive built at every start

- **Symptom:** a compute pipeline the archive bundled with the app does not hold (no bundle, as in a headless boot or a build without `pso_offline.py`; a shader outside the shader map; an OS update the bundle's binaries do not survive) was compiled again at its first dispatch of every start the system's shader cache had dropped it, and the device's own pipeline archive (`pso_archive`) never held one: `compute pipelines so far: 0 from the archive bundled with the app, N built` start after start.
- **Cause:** `cps_new` built such a pipeline through the `MTLDevice` (`newComputePipelineStateWithFunction`), which no pipeline serializer sees. Only the render bases went through the flush period's Metal 4 compiler, whose serializer captures what it compiles.
- **Fix:** a compute function with a Metal 4 library descriptor (`fn4_note`) is looked up in the bundle, then in the device's own archive files. On a miss it is built through the `MTLDevice` as before, for the dispatch, and compiled again through the Metal 4 compiler into the current flush period on a background queue, one at a time at utility QoS (`arch_harvest`, `arch_capture_cs`): the serializer captures compute pipelines as it captures bases, and the flush writes them. The dispatch does not wait for the capture, which compiles the pipeline whole where the `MTLDevice` gets it from the system's shader cache: a first version that built the dispatch's pipeline through the capture took 95 ms for Star Fox's first 5 compute pipelines, against 1.2 ms through the `MTLDevice`. A period with compute pipelines is a plain file, never a `p<seq>` one, so the lookups skip the prebuild's files. Compaction captures a compute pipeline the prewarm takes from an old file again, as it does bases. Still built through the `MTLDevice` and never looked up: a function with constants and the embedded kernels, which have no descriptor. The log counts them: `compute pipelines so far: N from the archive bundled with the app, M from this device's archive, K built (… ms looking up, … ms building)`, and `pipeline archive: … and M compute pipelines written to …`.
- **Measurement:** headless boots of 90 s to the title (frame 5,100) without the bundle, each series with an archive and prewarm list of its own. Before: 5 compute pipelines built at the first start (1.2 ms in all, from the system's shader cache) and 9 at the second (2.2 ms), none kept. After: 5 built at the first start (0.6 ms), and the flushes wrote them (`2 new pipeline bases and 1 compute pipelines`, `50 … and 4 compute pipelines`); the second start took 8 from the device's archive (the prewarm's `C` rows and the first dispatches; 1.4 ms of lookups in all) and built none. Both reached the same frame with the same five `[RED]` lines. With a warm system cache the two cost about the same; the archive keeps the pipelines when that cache drops them.
- **Found on:** Star Fox (v131072, with a provisional profile before this repository had one), chain `ae59da13e481`, M5 Pro, macOS 27.0, on a fork with other host changes. Also [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md#found-on-this-game): without the bundle, the first start wrote 88 compute pipelines into the device's archive and the second took 88 from it.
- **Check:** `t_pso_archive` (`compute_pipelines`): a compute pipeline is built, compiled into the flush period in the background, written to a plain file, taken from it again without a build, captured again during a compaction, and a function without a descriptor is built through the `MTLDevice`, never looked up and not captured; `t_pso_bundle`: a shader outside the map is captured, one with function constants is not.

### A learned mesh pipeline is built again after prewarm

- **Symptom:** a manifest contains native mesh rows, but the game still logs `task / mesh pipeline: … required at frame` for the same pipelines after preparation.
- **Cause:** a shader row could carry NVN2's task/mesh stage numbers (6/7), while the host uses slots 3/4. The prewarm workers cache a shader by name, so the first worker could publish the raw stage even for a later request from a valid mesh row. A draw using that shader could write stage 7 into its own mesh row; the next launch then rejected that row.
- **Fix:** load standalone shader rows using the stage in the current game's shader map, as the live loader does. Read a stored stage 7 as 4 only in a mesh row; the map, task, fragment and render-state checks still apply. Prepare one library per shader name and one pipeline per normalized mesh state and task shader. Since upstream nxnative 28d41fc9 the prewarm loads no shader row at all: each pipeline row loads its own shaders. The render test exercises raw and host stage numbers together and checks both the first demand and its rendered pixels.
- **Found on:** Mario Kart World.

### Native mesh pipelines compiled at their first draw

- **Symptom:** the first sight of a scene drawn with task and mesh shaders freezes for seconds on a cold Metal cache, on every start once the system's shader cache has dropped the pipelines, although the prewarm finished: `task / mesh pipeline: task … mesh … fragment …, N ms required at frame F`. Before ea0c98df the draw was left out instead, and the foliage stayed missing or white until the pipeline was ready.
- **Cause:** a native task / mesh pipeline (`mtk_pso` in `nvn_mesh.inc`) was built only at its first draw, through the `MTLDevice` (`newRenderPipelineStateWithMeshDescriptor`), and nothing kept it. No prewarm row named one, so the shader rows loaded its libraries and built nothing, and no pipeline serializer saw it, so the pipeline archive never held one. Since ea0c98df the draw waits for its pipeline, so each one met in play is a stall on the submit thread.
- **Fix:**
  - **Rows:** `mtk_pso` records each pipeline it builds as an `M` row in `pso_user.tsv` and the record list. The row holds the task shader's key or `-`, then the `pso_skey` hex of the render state with the mesh shader in the vertex place and the pixel shader, if any; `pso_mesh_skey_parse` refuses a row with a vertex layout, a specialized fragment or the wrong stages.
  - **Prewarm:** the prewarm builds each row on its workers (a task of its own, `kind 3`), so with the prepare step every listed pipeline is ready before the first frame.
  - **Claim:** the submit thread takes the pipeline under the live shader objects when they come from the same library and entry (`warm_mesh_shader_equal`). The program prefetch loads its own objects for the same keys, and claiming by object identity alone dropped prewarmed pipelines (`native mesh prewarm: live shader variant differs`, 0-29 a run on an M2 Pro, reported in PR #1368). A mesh shader loaded with its other entry after a depth mode change (`entry_z`) is not taken.
  - **Archive:** with the Metal 4 compiler, `mtk_build` keeps native mesh pipelines in the pipeline archive as `cps_new` keeps compute pipelines. It looks a pipeline up in the archive files first (a `p` file when a flush holds only such pipelines, as they have no vertex layout). On a miss the `MTLDevice` builds it, which the system's shader cache serves when it holds it, and the Metal 4 compiler compiles it again into the flush period in the background (`arch_harvest`). Compaction captures one the prewarm takes from an old plain file again. A pipeline is whole, with no base: a game has some hundred, each with functions of its own, and a mesh pipeline specialized from a base hangs the GPU ([Metal 4 specialized mesh pipeline bases hang the GPU](#metal-4-specialized-mesh-pipeline-bases-hang-the-gpu)).
  - **Seed rows:** Ocarina of Time's `pso_manifest.tsv` carries `M` rows recorded in play; [its note](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md#found-on-this-game) has the count, the areas and what each set measured.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md#found-on-this-game), v131072. Measured by contributors on hosts that carried the rows and prewarm of PR #1368, not the archive path:
  - **M5 Max, cold cache:** without rows, the Kokiri Forest entry built 63 pipelines one after another on the submit thread, 59.5 s in all, with one frame of 42.9 s. With 88 `M` rows and the prepare step, all 88 were built before the first frame (76.6 s on 18 workers), none at a draw, and the longest frame was 281 ms.
  - **M1, cold cache:** a 300 s walk built 47 pipelines at their draws, 103.5 s in all, the longest 12.76 s. With the rows and a fork's archive, 58 were prewarmed in 7.3 s on 8 workers.
  - **iPad Pro (M5):** 1.9-5 s per pipeline with a cold cache, 0.1-45 ms cached.
  - **M4 Pro:** with the rows, 0 built in play, against 56 without.
- **Check:**
  - `t_pso_skey`: mesh keys round-trip, and malformed or incompatible rows are refused.
  - `t_pso_warm` (`native_mesh`, on both compiler paths): only the canonical row loads, the warm task publishes a pipeline, duplicate live objects claim it without a build, a different depth entry is not claimed and builds its own, and the warmed and demanded pipelines draw the same pixels.
  - `t_pso_archive` (`mesh_pipelines`): a pipeline is built, captured in the background and written to a `p` file, with or without a task or pixel shader. A later start takes it from that file without a build, a compaction captures it again, and a function without a Metal 4 descriptor is built through the `MTLDevice` and not captured.
  - `t_mesh_first_use`: no first draw is left out.
- **Open:** the archive path has run only in `t_pso_archive`, not on a game route. On iOS the Metal 4 path, and with it the archive, is off for a profile without `pso_mtl4 on` (Ocarina of Time has none), so there the rows and the system's shader cache are what keep a mesh pipeline. `pso_offline.py` makes no native mesh pipelines for the archive bundled with the app.

### Metal 4 specialized mesh pipeline bases hang the GPU

- **Symptom:** none in this tree, which never built mesh pipeline bases. In nxnative, with them: `Caused GPU Hang Error (00000003:kIOGPUCommandBufferCallbackErrorHang)`, then `kIOGPUCommandBufferCallbackErrorSubmissionsIgnored` and `exit 70`, on entering areas with native NVN2 task / mesh draws; macOS writes a `gpuEvent-*.ips` report (`bug_type 284`) with `guilty_dm: 1` (object and mesh) and `restart_reason: 10` (`"MMU interrupt"`) or `4` (`"firmware-detected lockup"`, `signature: 577`). `DM_PSO_MTL4=0` avoided it.
- **Cause:** Apple's Metal 4 compiler: a `MTL4MeshRenderPipelineDescriptor` pipeline specialized from an unspecialized base (`newRenderPipelineStateBySpecializationWithDescriptor:pipeline:`) loses its mesh state. On this Mac's M2 Pro (macOS 27.2.1, 26B131), a probe's whole pipeline and its base report `maxTotalThreadgroupsPerMeshGrid` 1024 and the specialized one (`AGXG14XFamilyRenderPipeline`) 0; upstream saw the same on an M4 Pro (`Mac16,7`, macOS 27.2.1, 0 against 1,048,576, even with 1024 set on both). `imageblockSampleLength` is 0 on the specialized pipeline too (4 whole), but that marks nothing: a vertex / fragment base and its specialization also give 0 here and draw correctly. Upstream also saw a specialized mesh base zero per-primitive outputs and `[[primitive_id]]` now and then (M5 Pro, M6).
- **Fix:** a workaround for Apple's defect: native mesh pipelines are built whole and kept whole in the pipeline archive (`mtk_build` in `nvn_mesh.inc`, [Native mesh pipelines compiled at their first draw](#native-mesh-pipelines-compiled-at-their-first-draw)); vertex / fragment pipelines keep their Metal 4 bases. This tree never took nxnative's mesh bases (`685d2aec`), which upstream `8ecae8c4` removed again, so no code changed here. Open: whether a later macOS keeps the mesh state through a specialization.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests check_t_pso_archive` (`mesh_pipelines`): mesh pipelines built whole, and taken again from the archive, keep `maxTotalThreadgroupsPerMeshGrid` above 0 and draw.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md#found-on-this-game) in nxnative, v131072 ([issue #3274](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/issues/3274), Kakariko Graveyard; [issue #3275](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/issues/3275), Jabu-Jabu's Belly), GPU hangs on an M3 Pro, M4 Pro, M5 Max and M6 with macOS 26.6 and 27.2.x; upstream nxnative `8ecae8c4`. On its 45 s Kakariko Graveyard replay (M4 Pro, macOS 27.2.1) the specialized bases ended in `exit 70` with a warm archive and with `DM_PSO_ARCHIVE=off`; whole pipelines, cold and from the archive (33 hits), gave no GPU error.

### A late discard that keeps its depth write

- **Symptom:** parts of a surface missing, the scene behind it or the sky showing through, with straight edges; what should cover them is drawn but stops at an invisible boundary. No red line. A per-draw dump of the depth and colour targets shows a draw that writes depth over pixels where it writes no colour. Ocarina of Time 1.0.0: sky-blue holes in the ground of Death Mountain Trail and Goron City near Link, all-or-nothing per run, in about one run in two.
- **Cause:** on Apple's GPU a `discard_fragment()` late in a fragment shader can drop the fragment's colour and keep its depth write. Measured on an M2 Pro, macOS 27.2.1, with the game's colour terrain fragment `69f9adcb5182492b` (about 7,600 lines of MSL with subgroup shuffles and one loop). It has two discards: an early test against the scene's depth, and later its hole-map test (`_3240`, discard near the end of the body). Over the terrain's cut-out region the shader's own hole test is true: counted on the GPU, it agreed on every one of about 620 M fragments with the depth pre-pass fragment's test (`2f412aa7`) recomputed in the same shader. Yet one draw wrote depth over 1.18 M pixels with no colour. A discard on the same test placed just after the hole map's coordinates (about line 370 of the body) removed that depth; one on `_3240` placed where it is computed (about line 1690) did not; removing the [KK9](../../dm/gfx/tools/dmsh_metalwa.py) guard of the shader's loop changed nothing. Keeping both discards and discarding once more at the entry point's end, after the body, removed it. Where in a shader a discard stops holding the depth write is not isolated, and there is no standalone repro yet.
- **Fix:** a workaround in the chain, since the cause is Apple's: `late_discard` in `dm/gfx/tools/dmsh_metalwa.py` (chain `14495341b2c9`). Every discard stays where it is and also sets a flag: the call becomes the expression `(dm_kill = true, discard_fragment())`, which stands wherever the call did (SPIRV-Cross also writes `gl_HelperInvocation = true, discard_fragment();`), and the flag is passed as `thread bool& dm_kill` through `dm_body_main0` and any function between it and an entry point. Every fragment entry point computes its return value, then discards on the flag, then returns. `dmsh_build.py` runs it in `ps_libraries` on each pixel shader's whole unit, after its side entry points exist, and on the plain source when there is no unit. A shape it does not handle is left as it was, with a red line. It changes 22,047 of the game's 22,150 pixel shaders (every one that discards).
- **Check:** `dm/gfx/tests/test_dmsh_metalwa.py` (`LateDiscard`: the flag through the body, the value computed before the repeated discard, a void entry, comments, refusals, idempotence, and a compile). On the game's frozen Death Mountain route (`agent-runs/save_dmt_1126` in the build folder), with diagnostic per-draw dumps that are not in the tree: before, the cut-out patches wrote 1.16–1.18 M depth-only pixels in every run, in both draw orders; with the rewrite's own output for `69f9adcb`, none in either order, and 0 hole frames in 2 runs of 2. On the whole library of chain `14495341b2c9` (42,816 shaders built, as on the chain before) with the repository's host: 0 hole frames in 76 over 4 runs, 2 of them in the order that had holes in every run before, and only the 4 red lines 1.0.0 always logs.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md#found-on-this-game) 1.0.0 (v0), M2 Pro, macOS 27.2.1, 2026-11-26 and 27: about 400 headless runs over two days, the last ones with per-pass dumps of depth and colour.

### The device's prewarm list unread without a seed list

- **Symptom:** `[RED] pipeline prewarm: seed list does not exist: …/pso_manifest.tsv` at every start, no `pipeline prewarm list …/pso_user.tsv` line, and the same compile stalls every session in places already visited, while `pso_user.tsv` keeps growing; the pipeline archive gains files every start and is never compacted (Donkey Kong Bananza: 88 files, 7 s to open them).
- **Cause:** `warm_start` (`dm/gfx/nvn/nvn_prewarm.inc`) returned as soon as the profile had no seed list, before it added the device list, so neither list was warmed, and the prebuild of every shader pair's pulling base and the archive's compaction, which run after the prewarm, never ran either. Draws then waited for their pipelines on the submit thread with no hybrid stand-in.
- **Fix:** a missing seed list is logged and left out; the device list is still warmed, and the prewarm returns only when neither list exists. Host only.
- **Found on:** Donkey Kong Bananza: before, a 13,200-frame session had 154 frames over 100 ms (122 mostly compiling, the worst 7.1 s), 87 s of compiling on the submit thread and 95 draws with a hybrid pipeline; after, the start warmed the 2,484-line device list in 7.6 s (1,002 bases from the archive), compacted 98 archive files into one and ran the prebuild once (20,647 pairs, 116 s before the first frame), and a 17,700-frame session had 68 such frames (40 compiling, the worst 1.9 s), 12 s of compiling and 5,320 hybrid draws. The game has a seed list now.

### A pipeline a hybrid draw stood in for listed only once built

- **Symptom:** on an iPhone the next start still waits on, or draws with stand-ins for, the pipelines the last session asked for last. Ocarina of Time on an iPhone 18 Pro Max asked for about 400 pipelines in Castle Town and finished about 60 a minute; whatever was still unbuilt when the run ended was not in `pso_user.tsv`, so the next start's prepare step did not build it. HookLink's measurement; we have not reproduced it.
- **Cause:** `pso_user.tsv` took a pipeline's row from the submit thread only once its build finished. While a hybrid draw drew with its shader pair's pulling pipeline, the pipeline it wanted was queued in the background (`pso_pend_demand`), where a build took 5-50 s with the game running on two performance cores, against about 1 s in the prepare step before the first frame.
- **Fix:** `demand_record` (`dm/gfx/nvn/nvn_pso.inc`) writes the row when the hybrid draw first asks for the pipeline, once per key, including an already pending build or one the full build table could not admit. The later row from the built pipeline is the same line, which `pso_user_record` drops. Early recording remembers at most 65,536 keys per process; new keys beyond that retain completion-only recording. `DM_PSO_DEMAND_RECORD=0` records a pipeline only once built, as before. The added rows move work into the next preparation; they do not remove compilation.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md#found-on-this-game), found by [HookLink](https://github.com/dsi-hub/HookLink) (engine branch: https://github.com/dsi-hub/nxnative/tree/hooklink, commit 0ad4e1a9) on an iPhone 18 Pro Max.
- **Check:** `t_pso_demand_record` calls the real `pso_get` hybrid path with synthetic Metal functions and cached stand-ins while its production demand queues are suspended. New and independently prequeued demands persist canonical rows before compilation; an exec child reads them through `warm_parse`. After releasing the queues, completed pipelines replace the stand-ins and record identical rows without duplicates. Separate processes check recording disabled and the 65,536-entry early-record cap (completed pipelines still record beyond it). Removing call-site recording, or recording only newly queued demands, fails matched controls. API validation is enabled. This proves pipeline lookup and process-reload visibility, not power-loss durability, a game draw, fresh-process prewarm compilation, or native performance.
- **Preparation cost:** a matched, controlled backlog of recorded game pipelines was measured through the real prepare step on the Mac; NieR:Automata's note records its inputs, repeated timings and limitations. The Castle Town iPhone preparation increase remains unmeasured.

### Shader libraries loaded when the program is made

- **Symptom:** the first frame that draws with new programs loads their shader libraries on the submit thread (`[longframe] ... shader libraries N, X ms`): tens of milliseconds with the files cached, seconds when the drive is slow.
- **Cause:** a library was loaded when a draw first resolved its program (`prog_get`), or, from the first pipeline miss of a submission, for the whole rest of the submission at once and in parallel (`shader_prefetch_rest`), but still on the submit thread's time. The game names a program's shaders earlier, when it makes the program (`nvnProgramSetShaders`), often while it loads a scene.
- **Fix:** `mtl_prog_prefetch` (called from `nvnProgramSetShaders`) loads each stage's library on a background queue, with a vertex shader's pulling entry as the prewarm does, and the next submission takes it into the shader cache (`warm_claim`), writing it to the prewarm lists as `shader_get` would. A set of the libraries loaded or under way (`progpf_first`, which `shader_get` and `shader_prefetch_rest` also mark) loads each once. A draw that comes before its library has loaded loads it itself, as before. The shader map is read once from any thread (`shmap_read_once`); the prewarm still starts on the submit thread, once (`shmap_load`). A first version put the prewarm inside the same `dispatch_once`, and the pipeline prebuild's own call of `shmap_load` from inside it re-entered it: libdispatch aborted Tears of the Kingdom and Pokémon Scarlet at boot (the regression found it; Donkey Kong Bananza runs no prebuild). `DM_NVN_PREFETCH=0` turns it off.
- **Check:** Donkey Kong Bananza's scripted route, both runs with the drive idle: the first frame's 4 libraries (22 ms on the submit thread) no longer load there; the level opening's 3 still do (0.5-3.9 ms), its programs made just before their draws.
- **Found on:** Donkey Kong Bananza: 3 to 7 libraries a new area, which took up to 4 s a frame in a session with the drive slowed by other work.

### The prepare step inside the shader map's once

- **Symptom:** at boot, `CRASH sig=5 … pc=_dispatch_once_wait.cold.1` right after the `pipeline bases so far` line, with `bt: pso_prebuild __shmap_load_block_invoke … mtl_prog_prefetch h_nvnProgramSetShaders`.
- **Cause:** making `shmap_load` safe from any thread for the [prefetch](#shader-libraries-loaded-when-the-program-is-made) put its whole body in a `dispatch_once`, and that body ended in `warm_start`. With a prepare step (a window, `DM_PSO_PREPARE=1`, or the regression harness's runs), `warm_start` runs the prebuild in place, and `pso_prebuild` calls `shmap_load` again on the same thread inside the once, which libdispatch traps. The prefetch also moved the start of the prepare step from the first submission to the game's first `nvnProgramSetShaders`, on the game's thread. Separately, `warm_start` created the list that finished items go on, so a library the prefetch finished before it, or in a run with no prewarm list, was dropped, and its first draw loaded it again.
- **Fix:** `shmap_read_once` is the `dispatch_once` around reading the map; `shmap_load` reads through it and then starts the prewarm once, outside it. The prefetch only reads the map, so the step runs at the first submission again ([the prepare step](../development.md#the-prepare-step)). `warm_enqueue` makes its own list. Host only.
- **Check:** `dm/gfx/tests/t_shmap_warm.m` needs no game: the map's read leaves the prewarm unstarted, an item finished earlier is kept, and the first submission gets through the prebuild's second call. Before the fix the same sequence trapped with exit 133.
- **Found on:** general to games that run a prebuild: The Legend of Zelda: Tears of the Kingdom and Pokémon Scarlet at boot, Blasphemous under `regress.py`, Hollow Knight with a window. Donkey Kong Bananza, where the prefetch came in, runs no prebuild and never showed it.

### A prebuild stamp written without the pulling libraries

- **Symptom:** after an IPA packed without `_bp.metallib` ran once with `DM_PSO_MTL4=1`, an app with those libraries still stalls on new pipelines: `pipelines up to frame` lines show `draws with a hybrid pipeline 0` and `draws that waited: pulling base not made yet`, and the log has no `pipeline prebuild:` line. The run before it logged `[RED] pulling library of … not usable` and `pipeline prebuild done: … 19838 left to the draws`.
- **Cause:** `build_ipa.sh` left the pulling libraries out when the profile did not set `pso_mtl4 on` (until [Pulling vertex inputs](rendering.md#pulling-vertex-inputs) kept them in every iOS build; `IOS_PULL=0` still leaves them out), while the launcher can still turn the Metal 4 path on. `pre_desc` then returned no descriptor for every pair with a pulling vertex entry, and `pso_prebuild` wrote `prebuilt.tsv` as for a finished run. The stamp covers the chain, the depth mode and the pair lists, none of which change when the app gains the libraries, so the next start took the prebuild as done and no pulling base was ever made.
- **Fix:** the device's `prebuilt.tsv` now ends with what the run found (`pre_device_stamp` in `nvn_prewarm.inc`): `libs all`, or `libs missing <file>` naming one library file a pair needed and the app lacked (`pre_lib_absent`): a pulling library `<key>_bp.metallib`, or a shader's `<key>_b.json` or `<key>_b.metallib`, which a partial shader build can add later. `pre_stamp_done` treats the second as done only while the library still lacks that file, so a package with it builds the pairs. A file counts as there when it is in the library's folder or in its image `b-<chain>.img` (`msl_has`, the lookup `msl_data` and `lib_from_file` make): the Mac app and the IPA carry only the image, so a check of the folder alone would find every library absent. A stamp without the line was written before this check and may hide such pairs: it runs once more, taking the bases already archived. The run also logs `[RED] pipeline prebuild: N pairs need a library file this app does not carry (first: …)`. The stamp of the archive made offline, which `pso_bundle_covers` compares, is unchanged.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_pso_stall` covers a missing pulling library, a later start of the same app, the library arriving, a stamp without the line, and the same with the library packed as an image: its files found there, an absent shader named by its file, and that shader added in a folder beside the image (the first version of this fix checked the folder only and, against an image, built none of the pulling bases and stamped that as done). No device run with the fix yet: the iPad's recovery below was made by overwriting its stamp by hand.
- **Found on:** The Legend of Zelda: Tears of the Kingdom: iPad Pro 11 (M5), 1.4.3. 20,016 pairs: 178 bases built and 19,838 left to the draws without the libraries; with them and the stamp overwritten, 19,779 built in 718 s, and the archive grew to about 1.9 GB. That build raised the thermal state to serious, so the frame step held 30 fps for the first start after it.

### Metal's build folders left by pipeline archive flushes

- **Symptom:** the user cache folder (`getconf DARWIN_USER_CACHE_DIR`) fills with `gpuarchiver-*` folders, one per pipeline archive flush: in `com.apple.gpuarchiver/` for a host from a build, in `org.nativenavi.<title ID>/com.apple.gpuarchiver/` for a built Mac app. Each holds an LMDB store (`PersistentState`) and captured binaries, 0.3–5 MB at first (456 folders, 2.5 GB after two days), later up to about 127 MB (an M4 Pro, 2026-10-29: 19 GB in 423 folders after a day of Mac runs and regression checks); why they grew is **open**. A built app's folders were never removed: Ocarina of Time's app had 4,016 of them, about 285 GB, and one Mac's apps 345.8 GiB in all; each start of Ocarina's Castle Town route left 6 more, 560 MB ([issue #2680](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/issues/2680)). A later compile can fail with `cannot open '…/gpuarchiver-…/PersistentState': No such file or directory`.
- **Cause:** the Metal 4 serializer (`MTL4PipelineDataSetSerializer`) creates a private working folder for each serializer, and the host makes one serializer per flush period (`ep_new`). Metal puts them under the main bundle's ID: `<user cache folder>/<bundle ID>/com.apple.gpuarchiver`, or `<user cache folder>/com.apple.gpuarchiver` for a process without one (`getCacheMainFolder` in Metal; its exported `MTLGetGPUArchiverCachePath` returns the folder, measured on macOS 27.2: a test program without a bundle wrote to the shared folder, with an `Info.plist` beside it to `org.example.gatest/`). In an iOS 27 simulator app the folder was `<container>/Library/Caches/<bundle ID>/com.apple.gpuarchiver`, and `confstr(_CS_DARWIN_USER_CACHE_DIR)` returned nothing there. The host took the folder as `<user cache folder>/com.apple.gpuarchiver` in both cases, so its iOS cold-start cleanup most likely never found a folder, and a built Mac app's were outside both it and `scripts/clean_metal_cache.py`. A former cleanup inferred ownership from the sole new directory between two snapshots of the shared folder; when another compiler created one during the capture or flush, the foreign folder was claimed and deleted. Serialization success concerns the requested output archive, not the lifetime of Metal's private files; the public API exposes neither a scratch-directory selector nor an ownership token. A later start never reads the folders: with them emptied against kept, a Castle Town start's first frame was 2.22 s against 2.32 s and its prewarm 18.2 s against 19.3 s (n=3 each, issue #2680).
- **Fix:** `nvn_mtl.m` asks Metal for the folder (`ga_dir`; the same rule computed when Metal lacks the function). A host with a bundle ID (the Mac app, the iOS app, one rule on both) owns its folder together with the other copies of the same game's app: from before its first serializer to its exit, each holds a shared `flock` on `gpuarchiver.lock` beside the folder (`ga_claim`). At start it first tries an exclusive one; with it, no other copy runs, so it lists the `gpuarchiver-*` folders there, takes the shared lock, and removes them in the background (`pipeline archive: N of M of Metal's build folders left by earlier runs removed from …`). Otherwise it waits for the shared lock, at most a starting copy's listing, and removes nothing (`another copy of this app is running`). A lock held exclusively for the run would not do: a second copy that could not take it would hold nothing, and a third start after the first's exit would delete the second's live folders. Folders are never removed at a flush or exit, and a host without a bundle ID removes none from the shared folder. A run still keeps its own folders until the next start (Ocarina: about 560 MB a start, more in a long session). `scripts/clean_metal_cache.py` removes the shared folder's and the built apps' (`org.nativenavi.*`) folders from outside, for apps not started again: a folder only when nothing in it changed for an hour (`--idle-min`) and its last change is older than the start of every running `dmhost`, and an app's only while no copy of it holds its lock (listed under the exclusive lock, removed after it). `scripts/regress.py` runs it at its start and when the disk falls under its limit. A folder of another program's live compiler that stayed idle for an hour in the shared folder would still go; none is known.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md#found-on-this-game): a built app's folders, 285 GB ([issue #2680](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/issues/2680)). Pokémon Scarlet: the ownership defect is reproduced with an isolated foreign-folder fixture. Its longer house run had 20 missing-`PersistentState` failures, but the deleting process was not observed, so that run's attribution remains open. Earlier cache-growth report: MacNativeGames (`1eff989`, `110e6c6`, Metal 3's `MTLBinaryArchive`, 25 GB overnight on Tears of the Kingdom).
- **Check:** `t_pso_archive` (`make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_pso_archive`) preserves a foreign compiler fixture created during a compiler call through the subsequent flush, and checks the app folder's lock: a first copy lists the earlier runs' folders, a second lists none, and a third start after the first's exit lists none while the second runs. `dm/tools/tests/test_clean_metal_cache.py` keeps folders changed within the idle time or since a running host started, keeps an app's folders while its lock is held, removes the rest, and looks in the shared folder and the `org.nativenavi.*` folders only. The iOS folder is from a simulator app (Metal's function; the simulator has no Metal 4 serializer); no device run yet.

### A compaction flush that crashes, then crashes every start

- **Symptom:** a previously working game exits with 139 on every start after pipeline preparation; `dispatch_retain(NULL)` is called by `-[_MTLBinaryArchive materializeAirNTFromOnlineForSlice:entryList:]` under `airntSerializeToURL` and `arch_flush` ([issue #342](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/issues/342)).
- **Cause:** more than eight run archives enables compaction. The prewarm used `MTL4CompilerTaskOptions.lookupArchives` to recapture their bases into the serializer that also captures newly compiled bases. Metal can crash while materializing that dataset for serialization. It crashes before `arch_compact_finish` removes the old files, so every restart retries it. Framework analysis recorded in issue #342 identifies a nil `MTLBinaryEntry.bitcode`, read from the captured function's library by `bitcodeDataInternal` when the bytes at its bitcode offset are not an AIR wrapper. Which function lacks AIR and why remain **open**. A lead, not a cause: a probe that reused one serializer after each write, with no compile during the write, crashed once in 111 runs with this signature, and a compile under way across a write comes back with its entry's AIR script and bitcode nil ([A compile that does not return stalls every flush and draw](#a-compile-that-does-not-return-stalls-every-flush-and-draw)); the host now never uses a serializer again after its write, and a serializer per period crashed in none of 118 runs; simple synthetic recaptures and a replay with no new bases did not reproduce it. A mixed Tears of the Kingdom replay did, with the reported null call and backtrace, on an M4 Pro with macOS 27.0.1 (26A434).
- **Fix:** work around the framework defect by capturing each compacted base from its original shader functions, with no lookup archives. Metal's ordinary compiler cache still applies. The archive threshold, ordinary archive lookups, deferred flush during prewarm and removal of old files only after a successful write remain in place. A compaction start can do more compiler work; normal starts keep their archive hits. `arch_capture` passes no task options, so captured bases cannot accidentally import archive entries. The recovery guard from the earlier workaround remains: `pso_archive/compacting` records the system build and the capture method before a compaction flush, and is removed when the flush returns. A marker for this build and method suppresses another attempt after a crash. Legacy OS-only markers from archive imports are cleared so the safer capture method can run; a later OS or capture-method change also allows another attempt.
- **Found on:** The Legend of Zelda: Tears of the Kingdom, reported on an M5 Max and reproduced with the local shader libraries on an M4 Pro. This is shared NVN archive code, not a game profile rule.
- **Check:** the same 19-file snapshot, 1,490 archived bases and 44 new bases failed in two of three runs with lookup imports and passed all three when only that option was removed. The implemented fix passed three further compactions and their following restarts: each compacted file supplied all 1,534 bases with zero new base compilations on restart. These are library-only manifest replays using the local `e7245308050d` libraries and a matching diagnostic chain header, not a new gameplay run or an iOS test. `t_pso_archive` rejects lookup imports at the real compiler seam; `test_pso_archive.py` exercises disk-built libraries, concurrent recaptures mixed with a fresh function, function constants, compaction and restart in separate processes (`make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_pso_archive`). The marker tests retain the recovery guard and check migration from the old OS-only marker, including a fresh-process restart with that marker present.

### Pipeline keys omit unused colour state

- **Symptom:** identical render pipelines compile repeatedly under different keys, lengthening startup prewarm and adding pipeline misses during play.
- **Cause:** NVN blend and channel-mask state persists between draws, including state for colour targets absent from the current pass. `pso_desc` skips targets without a format and reads blend factors only when blending is enabled, but the old keys retained unused state. Deduplicating manifest lines alone also misses old rows whose unused state differs.
- **Fix:** `pso_key_norm` in `nvn_pso_skey.h` clears blend factors when blending is disabled and, for normal passes, the blend-enable bit and write-mask nibble of every target without a format. Merged passes retain their logical blend-enable and mask state because `lmap` maps physical attachments to logical targets; a missing physical format does not imply the same logical target is unused. `pso_key_fill` normalizes live keys, `pso_skey_parse` normalizes historical prewarm rows, and `warm_worker` deduplicates the parsed keys across all input lists before compiling. Depth and stencil formats remain in the key because the `MTLDevice` path needs them, even though Metal 4 bases omit them.
- **Found on:** The Legend of Zelda: Breath of the Wild (disabled blend factors), The Legend of Zelda: Tears of the Kingdom (absent-target state, reported by MacNativeGames).
- **Check:** `t_pso_skey` covers normalized round trips, historical rows differing only in unused state, and preservation of merged-pass logical masks and blend bits; `t_pso_archive` checks disabled blend factors with all targets present. `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_pso_warm` loads two synthetic manifests through `warm_worker`, checks compilation counts and draw-time cache hits, and compares GPU pixels with the original descriptors through both MTLDevice and Metal 4. It includes a merged pass whose physical attachment 0 uses logical target 7's blend and mask state.

### Shader cache key covering the pairs

- **Symptom:** a vertex shader whose pairs changed was restored unpadded from `cas/`.
- **Cause:** `dmsh_build.py`'s shader cache was keyed without the pairs.
- **Fix:** the per-shader key covers the pairs, and a paired vertex shader's key also covers its pixel shaders' keys (`shader_key`, `pair_keyed`), so a change to a profile's pairs rebuilds what it affects ([Pixel shader inputs the vertex shader does not write](#pixel-shader-inputs-the-vertex-shader-does-not-write)).
- **Found on:** 13 Sentinels: Aegis Rim.

### Shader cache key missing helper modules

- **Symptom:** an edit to a shader build helper reaches no library: `shader cache: hit` for every shader, and the old MSL is filed under the new chain version. An edit to `dmsh_meshstage.py` did not change the chain version either.
- **Cause:** a cache hit restores a shader's outputs and skips `job_for` and `finish`, so everything they run has to be in the shader's key. `CODE_FP` left out `dmsh_wide.py`, `dmsh_ampere.py`, `dmsh_meshstage.py` and `dmsh_fetch.screen_varyings` (through `raster_facts`), and `dmsh_chain.py`'s `FILES` left out `dmsh_meshstage.py`.
- **Fix:** `FILES` and `CODE_FP` cover them. The modules that stay out of `CODE_FP` are made again from the restored MSL on every build (`dmsh_vin.py`, `dmsh_pull.py`, `dmsh_side.py` with `dmsh_alpha.py` and `dmsh_logic.py`, the rest of `dmsh_fetch.py`), or are covered otherwise (`dmsh_facts.py`: its row is in the key; `dmsh_mtlcache.py` changes no output). The change bumps the chain with the libraries byte for byte as before (Animal Well's 46), so Metal compiles nothing again.
- **Found on:** no game: found reading the code after Mario Kart World added `dmsh_meshstage.py`, then reproduced on ANIMAL WELL (an edit to `wide_loads` reached 0 of 46 libraries before the fix, all 46 after it).
- **Check:** `test_dmsh_code_fp.py` `Coverage` fails when `dmsh_build.py` imports a `dmsh_*` module that neither list accounts for.

### Partial vertex builds missing paired pixel sources

- **Symptom:** a full build reports successful shaders, but `metal-tt` rejects its pipeline archive with fragment inputs not written by the vertex shader. Fifteen cached vertices lacked default outputs in DRAGON QUEST I & II.
- **Cause:** a partial build requested only its vertex keys. `vs_pad` skipped absent paired pixel MSL, then cached the incomplete vertex under a key that already included those pixel shaders' input digests. A later full build restored it before pixel-first finishing and never padded that cache hit again.
- **Fix:** include available paired pixel shaders in partial build keys and output cleanup, then finish pixels before vertices. Do not publish a paired vertex cache entry while any paired pixel MSL is missing. The code fingerprint excludes the old incomplete cache entries; the Metal source cache still reuses unchanged complete libraries.
- **Found on:** DRAGON QUEST I & II HD-2D Remake, while building its Mac pipeline archive.
- **Check:** `test_shader_toolchain_cache.py` requests a vertex alone, exercises dependency cleanup and pixel-first finishing, and checks that padding survives publication and a later full-cache restore. A missing pixel source prevents publication.

### Target selection keeps the shader analysis files in the cache

- **Symptom:** a repeated iOS-only shader build crashes with `FileNotFoundError` for `<key>_b.json` in the Mac output directory; requesting only Mac libraries with `IOSOUT` set still compiles iOS side libraries.
- **Cause:** target selection excluded the Mac directory from the per-shader cache entirely, but cache-hit processing, paired vertex padding and the profile tables still read its MSL and reflection. Side-library paths tested `IOSOUT` rather than the selected targets. Prefilling the Metal cache also compiled validation samples for unrequested targets.
- **Fix:** cache and restore the Mac MSL and reflection for every target; require and copy metallibs only for selected targets. A cache entry without its analysis source is a miss. Clear `IOSOUT` when iOS is not selected and restrict prefill validation to selected targets. The NSP script writes only the requested target's reuse key.
- **Found on:** Celeste, while reviewing PR #275 (resolved).
- **Check:** real Metal output and cache comparisons are recorded in Celeste's shader note above. `test_shader_toolchain_cache.py` covers the restored base outputs, incomplete entries and prefill selection; `test_nsp_mac.py` checks target-specific reuse keys.

### An iOS build after a Mac-only build kept no Mac libraries

- **Symptom:** after `scripts/build_app.py <NSP folder> --platform mac`, a plain (iOS) run of the script on the same folder succeeds, but the Mac run command the first build printed then logs hundreds of `[RED] failed to load shader <key>_b: cannot open …/shader/cache/b-<chain>/<key>_b.metallib` lines, until `--platform mac` is run again.
- **Cause:** both builds write the Mac sources and reflection into `<id>_build/shader/cache/b-<chain>/`, which also holds the Mac libraries. A full `dmsh_build.py` run first removes every `<key>_b*` output there, so that a key that left the map or a shader that no longer builds keeps no old library, and then restores or compiles only the targets in `DMSH_TARGETS`. Since target selection ([Target selection keeps the shader analysis files in the cache](#target-selection-keeps-the-shader-analysis-files-in-the-cache)) the NSP script asked an iOS build for `ios` alone, so the Mac `.metallib` files were removed and only their `.metal` and `.json` came back.
- **Fix:** the NSP script (`build_shaders`) asks an iOS build for `mac,ios` when the folder holds the current chain's Mac libraries, and then writes both reuse keys, so a later `--platform mac` run is reused. The Mac libraries are rebuilt, not kept as they were, so none is stale: the Metal compile cache gives back every one whose MSL was compiled before, and only a changed MSL is compiled. The first such build of a folder may translate again, because the per-shader cache entries an iOS-only build left hold no Mac library. A folder without Mac libraries (only ever packed as an IPA) still compiles only the iPhone's. `dmsh_build.py`'s rule, that a full build leaves only what it built, is unchanged; it is part of the chain version, so the fix stays in its caller.
- **Found on:** Team Sonic Racing (resolved).
- **Check:** `test_nsp_mac.py` (`test_ios_after_mac_keeps_mac_libraries`, `test_ios_after_mac_of_another_chain`).

### Old shader library folders grew a game's build folder with each chain change

- **Symptom:** the build folder of a game (`<id>_build/shader/`) gains about 2 GB for Breath of the Wild with every upstream update that changes the shader chain: after two updates `shader/cache` held `b-157e1b96c8da` (2.1 GB) and `b-a78f966a7b38` (2.3 GB) besides the current one, and the next build added a third.
- **Cause:** the libraries are written to `b-<chain>/` (`shader/cache` for the Mac, `shader/ios` for iOS, with the reuse keys `shader/ios/b-<chain>.key` and `shader/mac-b-<chain>.key`) and a chain change starts a new folder. Nothing removed the old ones. The host loads only the current chain's folder, `dm/mac/pack_app.py` and `build_ipa.sh` pack only it, and `regress.py` reads the folder of its own run, so an old folder is dead weight. `dmsh_build.py` took libraries of older folders into the Metal compile cache (`dmsh_mtlcache.py`) before it translated, and `shader_regressions.py` compared the new build with the newest older folder's `_b_build.json`.
- **Fix:** the NSP script (`prune_stale_chains`, called last in `build_shaders`) removes the folders named `b-<12 hex digits>` of other chain versions in `shader/cache` and `shader/ios`, and their keys, once the libraries, their tables and the key of the current chain are in place (a reused build prunes too). It logs one line, `removed old shader libraries b-… (N GB)`. A build that stops or fails earlier keeps the old libraries. The regression check has run by then, and the next chain change compares with the folder this build keeps. The compile cache already holds what the old libraries compiled, since `dmsh_build.py` took them in at the first build of the new chain. The only loss is for a chain that comes back (a checkout of an older commit): its shaders compile again where the cache does not hold their MSL. The compile cache itself (`shader/cache/cas/metallib`) is keyed by the MSL and the compiler, not by the chain, so entries are shared across chains and it is not pruned. It grows only with MSL that is new, and no code removes an entry.
- **Found on:** Breath of the Wild (resolved).
- **Check:** `test_nsp_mac.py` (`test_prune_stale_chains_after_success`, `test_failed_build_keeps_stale_chains`, `test_prune_ignores_other_names`).

### Old per-shader cache entries grew a game's build folder with each chain change

- **Symptom:** `<id>_build/shader/cache/cas/shader/` holds several entries for each shader, and grows by a full set with every upstream update that changes the shader chain. Tears of the Kingdom ([seedds/nxnative#1033](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/issues/1033)): 172,014 entries after six builds for 28,669 shaders, 11.85 GB of `.metal` and `.json` that no build read; the test Mac's copy held 934,955, of which the prune (dry run) found 906,286 unused: 70.2 GB of sources and reflection, scanned in 18 minutes on an external SSD.
- **Cause:** each shader's cache digest (`dmsh_build.py` `shader_key`) covers the chain's code and tools, so a chain change misses every entry and publishes a new one. [Old shader library folders](#old-shader-library-folders-grew-a-games-build-folder-with-each-chain-change) removed the old `b-<chain>/` folders, not these entries. `cas/index/<key>.json` cannot tell which entry is in use: a cache hit does not rewrite it, so after a checkout of an older commit it can name a newer digest than the one the build restores. The digests also cover the iOS compile convention, so a Mac-only and an iOS build of one chain use different entries, and `regress.py` builds other chains into the same cache.
- **Fix:** a full `dmsh_build.py` run lists the digests it used, `b-<chain>/_b_cas.txt` beside the Mac outputs (every full build writes those; the iOS folder goes whole into the IPA). The NSP script (`prune_shader_cache`, called with `prune_stale_chains` after a successful or reused build) removes the `shader/<64 hex digits>` entries of the compile cache that the list does not name and that are older than it, and logs `removed old shader cache entries: N (X GB)`, the space of their sources and reflection (an entry's libraries are clones of the compile cache's). No list (libraries built before it), a failed or stopped build, or a regress run: nothing goes. The Metal compiles are keyed by the MSL; since [Old chains' compiles stayed in the one compile cache file](packing.md#old-chains-compiles-stayed-in-the-one-compile-cache-file) each chain has its own file, and the files of other chains go whole, so this prune now covers only the chain's own file, where an edit of the shader map leaves old entries. A partial `dmsh_build.py` run (named keys) does not rewrite the list, so the next prune can remove the entries it published; the next full build translates those shaders again.
- **Found on:** Tears of the Kingdom (resolved).
- **Check:** `test_nsp_mac.py` (`test_prune_shader_cache`, `test_prune_shader_cache_needs_success_and_the_list`).

### A shader build that keeps its packages in memory runs one worker at a time

- **Symptom:** the NSP script's Mac or iOS shader step for a game with very large shader packages runs at one or two shaders a second whatever `J` is. Its Python process sits at one full core, mostly system time, while few `metal` compiles run. Pokémon Pokopia (4.4 GB of packages, 131,426 shaders): players reported ETAs of 20 to 200 hours ([seedds/nxnative#1036](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/issues/1036)).
- **Cause:** `dmsh_build.py` read each package whole and kept its records as separate copies for the whole build: 105,118 of them for Pokopia's 4.25 GB `ps_rdb.bin`, leaving the process at 8.7 GB. Every child process (spirv-cross, `metal`, `metallib`, several per shader) starts with a fork of the builder, which then took 75 ms instead of 3, and Python forks with the GIL held, so the `J` workers queued behind each other. The same bytes in one buffer fork in 3 ms: the cost comes from the many separate allocations.
- **Fix:** `record_index` scans a package for its record markers through a read-only mapping it closes again, and keeps only the offsets; `rec_of` reads one record with `pread` when the build needs it and keeps none. The outputs are byte-identical.
- **Measurement:** a cold build of 1,001 Pokopia shaders on an M4 Pro (`J=10`): 528 s before, 240 s after (1.9 to 4.2 shaders a second); the builder's Python went from 100% to about 46% of a core, and 8 to 10 Metal compiles ran at once instead of about 3. At that rate the whole map would take about 9 hours on that Mac (not run). The largest packages of the other local games are 241 MB (Splatoon Raiders) and less, where a fork costs a few milliseconds.
- **Found on:** Pokémon Pokopia (resolved).
- **Check:** `test_shader_toolchain_cache.py` (`test_package_records_are_read_from_the_file_at_their_offsets`).

### Each Metal compile instantiates Metal's texture classes again

- **Symptom:** the Mac or iOS shader step of a game with a large map still takes many hours with every worker busy, and a `metal -c` of a translated shader takes about 0.7 s of CPU, nearly all in the front end. Optimising adds about 0.1 s, and an empty shader takes 0.025 s. Pokémon Pokopia's 131,426 shaders: about 9 hours on an M4 Pro ([seedds/nxnative#1036](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/issues/1036)).
- **Cause:** a translated shader's argument buffer names several texture types, and Metal's texture classes are large templates. The compiler's module cache holds the templates but not their instantiations, and each shader is a new `metal` process, so it instantiates them again, along with a `memory_coherence_device` copy of each it uses: 722 of a typical shader's 902 ms of front end by `-ftime-trace`, against about 180 ms for the shader's own text. Declaring the argument buffer alone took 0.37 s of that shader's compile.
- **Fix:** `dm/gfx/tools/dmsh_pch.py` makes one precompiled header per compile convention at a process's first compile (`dmsh_build.py` asks for them before it forks its workers), holding those instantiations, and `dmsh_build.py` compiles with `-include-pch`. The header adds only the two includes the sources start with and declares no name, and a class left out of it costs only time. The output is byte-identical, so the compile records and the Metal compile cache's keys stay as they were. `make` checks this on a test shader and builds without the header if a compiler differs, and a compile that fails with the header runs again without it, so a failure reports what a plain compile reports. `DMSH_PCH=0` turns it off.
- **Measurement:** cold builds of 1,019 Pokopia shaders on an M4 Pro (`J=10`, with other jobs on the Mac): Mac child processes 2,343 to 975 s of CPU, wall 253–263 s to 176–182 s; iOS child processes 981–1,077 to 574–605 s, wall 179–233 s to 130–153 s. At that rate the whole Mac map would take about 6.4 hours instead of about 9 (not run). All 37,611 Mac files of 8,157 Pokopia shaders and all 3,675 iOS files of 1,020 came out byte-identical with and without the header, as did 1,600 compiles of eight games' shaders.
- **Open:** wall time fell less than CPU, as the builder's Python took about 0.12 s a shader under the GIL ([Rewrites that try every character of a large source](#rewrites-that-try-every-character-of-a-large-source) lowered that).
- **Found on:** Pokémon Pokopia.
- **Check:** `test_shader_pch.py`.

### Rewrites that try every character of a large source

- **Symptom:** the shader step of a game with large translated sources has fewer Metal compiles running than `J` asks for, and `dmsh_build.py`'s own process stays busy on one core between them. Pokémon Pokopia's sources average 220 KB, and the builder spent about 0.12 s of Python on each shader, all but its system calls under the GIL, which alone would have come to about 4.2 hours of its full build.
- **Cause:** patterns with no literal start, which `re` can only try at every character: `_once` in `dmsh_fetch.py` began with a lookbehind and ran 24 times a pixel shader at about 4 ms (38% of the Python, on shaders that mostly make no variant), the attribute pattern ran on every shader though only vertex shaders have attributes, and patterns beginning `\b` or an alternation did the same in `reflect`, `fs_uint` and `check_msl`. `dmsh_side.py` `_close` looped in Python over each character of a function body, and `uses_image_atomics` over each SPIR-V instruction.
- **Fix:** the same questions, answered from the text: `str.find` and a look at the character before it (`_once`, `has_word`), one pass over a shader's stores instead of one for each output (`fs_uint`), the lines that hold attributes (`attributes`), bracket to bracket (`_close`), and a test for a word every match must hold before the pattern runs (`check_msl`, `uses_image_atomics`). The outputs are byte-identical.
- **Measurement:** cold builds on an M4 Pro, old and new taking turns, never on a quiet Mac. Pokopia's 8,157 `shader_seen.tsv` shaders, Mac and iOS (`J=14`, load 9 to 19): wall 1,426 to 607 s, the builder's Python 1,121 to 463 s of CPU. 1,001 of them, Mac only: `J=14` at load 5 to 8, wall 141 to 68 s, Python 116 to 52 s; `J=14` at load 26 to 39, wall 194 to 94 s; `J=10` at load 31 to 64, wall 177 and 173 s to 92 and 93 s, Python 150 to 160 s to about 49 s. The child processes' CPU stayed about the same (4,842 to 4,789 s on the 8,157; 2 to 13% more on the 1,001, with more compiles running at once). At those rates the whole Mac map at the NSP script's `J=14` would take about 2.5 hours instead of about 5 (not run). Identical: all 31,836 Mac and 23,678 iOS files of those 8,157, and `regress.py chain` on all of Breath of the Wild's 19,592 shaders (0 changed); old against new, step by step, 0 differences on 27,325 sources of seven games, 8,197 of their fetch variants among them.
- **Open:** a pool of worker processes for the finish step gained only 6 to 8% more on top of this here (1,001 shaders, `J=10` and `J=14`, also with half the compiles modelled) and was not built then; on Macs with more cores it gained much more, and the build now has one ([A shader build's Python finishes one shader at a time](#a-shader-builds-python-finishes-one-shader-at-a-time)). `posix_spawn` (`close_fds=False`) takes `Popen` from 13 to 3 s, but without `pipe2` on macOS another thread's child can inherit a pipe and hold up its reader, so it would need a lock around every launch. The reflections a full build keeps (about 2.5 GB for Pokopia) raise the cost of each fork (of each child the build launches; its worker processes are forked once, before the first result); a micro-benchmark gives 0.7 ms at 176 MB and 1.8 ms at 2 GB, and no full map has been timed.
- **Found on:** Pokémon Pokopia (resolved).
- **Check:** `test_shader_rewrites.py`, which also holds each replacement to the pattern or loop it replaced on random text.
- **Further scan reductions:** `uniform_tests` starts its pattern with `as_type<` so the regex engine can skip to casts. The lookbehinds still check the characters before each cast, with the same match spans and captures. `select_guards` always checks for inline guards and scans for leftover choices only when it found size-comparison variables. The gather-offset and threadgroup-fence rewrites skip their patterns when the required helper name is absent. The rewrite tests cover matches, no-ops and errors.
- **Rewrite CPU ([PR #2897](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/pull/2897)):** Measured on final MSL from a built library, where most scans find nothing. On an M5 Pro with Python 3.13, the 16 measured passes used 16.97 to 7.76 s for Breath of the Wild's 10,560 pixel shaders, 24.17 to 11.22 s for Pokémon Scarlet's 15,006 and 27.65 to 13.53 s for Tears of the Kingdom's 11,969. The estimated savings were under 1% of total shader-build CPU for those three games. On an i7-10700K, dpadr measured 61.6 to 39.8 s for 2,018 sampled Ocarina of Time shaders. The PR's static chain comparison found identical MSL, reflections and variants for all 19,592 Breath of the Wild, 30,142 Pokémon Scarlet and 28,669 Tears of the Kingdom shaders. Ocarina of Time's 42,848 MSL files were also identical.
- **`select_guards` scans:** After those reductions `select_guards` was the largest of the 16 passes (4.5 of 7.8 s for Breath of the Wild's pixel shaders), and its time went to patterns tried at every character. `re` skips through text only to a pattern's literal start. The two size comparisons began with `\b`, the choice pattern is an alternation whose first form begins with a lookbehind, and the leftover-name pattern begins with one, so each ran at every character: about 10 ns a character on a source without bounded reads and 35 ns with them (M5 Pro), at any source size. The comparisons now begin with the text `bool `, and a lookbehind over that text stands for the `\b`. Choices and leftover names are tried only where a match can start: at the last `_` before ` ? (*spvDescriptorSet0.` or before ` ?`, and at a parenthesis before it. The pattern itself decides there, so the matches, their order and the result are those of `sub` and `finditer`. `GuardScans` in `test_shader_rewrites.py` holds each scan to its earlier pattern on random text. Measured on final MSL from built libraries (M5 Pro, Python 3.13): 4.66 to 0.32 s for Breath of the Wild's 10,560 pixel shaders, 6.72 to 0.47 s for Pokémon Scarlet's 15,006 and 8.22 to 0.53 s for Tears of the Kingdom's 11,969. Estimated for a whole build (every shader once, cut at its second entry point, each bounded read's select written back as the choice so the rewrite fires): 12.9 to 1.1 s, 19.8 to 1.6 s and 22.0 to 1.7 s, under 1% of shader-build CPU. Old and new gave the same output or error on all 78,403 final sources of the three libraries, on the 19,146 with selects written back (698,877 selects) and on 40,000 random sources. **Open:** not measured on Ocarina of Time's sources or in a whole build; bounded storage reads are rare in these libraries (249 comparisons, in Tears of the Kingdom's vertex shaders), so the tests hold most of that form.

### A shader build's Python finishes one shader at a time

- **Symptom:** a cold shader step leaves about half the cores idle whatever `J` is, and `dmsh_build.py`'s own process stays at one full core from start to end. Ocarina of Time's whole Mac map, 42,848 shaders: 3,506 s on an M3 Max with 16 cores (`J=16`, the build at 104% CPU, 4 to 5 cores busy in all), 3,002 s on an M5 Max with 18 cores.
- **Cause:** two. Finishing a shader (the MSL rewrites, its reflection, its outputs and the cache) is Python, about 40 ms a shader on Ocarina of Time, and the threads that did it share one GIL, so one core finished every shader while the Metal compiles the threads waited on left the others idle ([Rewrites that try every character of a large source](#rewrites-that-try-every-character-of-a-large-source) lowered that cost; free-threaded Python 3.14t was slower, not faster). And the shaders went in batches of `4 × J`: the next batch started when the slowest shader of this one was done, with every other thread idle.
- **Fix:** `dmsh_build.py` finishes the shaders in `DMSH_PROCS` worker processes (default `J`; 0 finishes them in threads, as before). The workers are forks of the build, made once, after the cache check and before its first thread, so they have every table and fingerprint it read; spawned workers would run the script again, and its start clears the outputs. No worker opens `cas.db`: the build makes their cache calls on its one connection ([A forked shader worker dies opening the compile cache](#a-forked-shader-worker-dies-opening-the-compile-cache); as first written each opened its own). The precompiled Metal headers are made before the fork, or each worker would make its own. The build still translates the batches itself and hands a worker each shader's job; the worker's result and counts come back and are added in batch order, so the summary, the progress file and every output are those of the threads. The next batch is handed out before this one is done (two at most), so no worker waits for a batch's slowest shader; paired vertex shaders still wait for every other shader (`vs_pad` reads their pixel shaders' MSL). A worker that dies stops the build with one `[RED]` line. The outputs are byte-identical; the change is to the chain's code, so it changes the chain version once.
- **Measurement:** cold builds, empty cache, each against the main it was written on.
  - Ocarina of Time's whole Mac map on the M3 Max (`J=16`): 3,506 to 1,826 s; with every shader restored from the cache 888 to 191 s. Every 8th key (9,133 shaders): 544 to 292 s, 6.4 to 13.4 cores busy; 417 s in threads with the batch overlap alone; 403, 400 and 388 s with 12, 16 and 20 workers.
  - An M6 with 12 cores and 16 GB (`J=12`, another game running beside it). Red Dead Redemption's 3,628 shaders: Mac 54 to 35 s, Mac and iOS 84 s to 53 and 63 s; with every shader restored from the cache 11 to 13 s (the headers are made though nothing compiles). 1,920 of Metroid Prime 4: Beyond's shaders, whose compiles are much longer: 105 to 72 s, and 89 s with `DMSH_PROCS=6`.
  - Memory, the summed resident size of the build and everything it runs, sampled every 0.5 s on the M6 (it counts the pages the forks share once for each): Red Dead Redemption 0.45 to 0.8 GB, the Metroid Prime 4 shaders 1.1 to 3.4 GB, and 2.0 GB with `DMSH_PROCS=6`. A worker itself held about 35 MB; the rest is `J` compilers running at once, which the threads never reached.
