With a header made in each worker, as first written, Red Dead Redemption's build peaked at 1.4 GB.
  - Identical apart from the chain version and `_b_cas.txt`: all 173,316 files of Ocarina of Time's map, and Red Dead Redemption's Mac and iOS libraries (15,542 and 11,912 files).
  - Another design, measured and not taken ([PR #2035](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/pull/2035)): the build as `J / 3` copies of itself, each with a share of the shaders. Ocarina of Time's map on the M5 Max 3,002 to 1,298 s, and 1.41 times faster on an M3 Pro with 11 cores for 1.3 GB more; on the M6 it gained less than the forked workers (Red Dead Redemption: Mac 75 to 58 s, Mac and iOS 104 to 95 s, against its own older main) and compiled about 9% of the Metal sources twice.
- **Open:** not measured on a Mac with 8 GB, where `DMSH_PROCS` below `J` trades the gain for memory, nor a whole large map with iOS libraries. Two workers can compile the same Metal source at once (about 10 of Red Dead Redemption's 2,159 compiles), which costs time only.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md#found-on-this-game).
- **Check:** `test_shader_cache.py` (`test_forked_workers_share_it`), `test_shader_toolchain_cache.py` (the batch loop with a substituted translator).

### A forked shader worker dies opening the compile cache

- **Symptom:** the Mac shader step stops a few seconds after it starts, every time, with `concurrent.futures.process.BrokenProcessPool` (since 8b55ebb3; the `[RED] a shader build worker process stopped` line once the build prints it), and `DMSH_PROCS=0` builds. The worker's crash report: `SIGSEGV` in `_os_log_preferences_refresh` ← `os_signpost_enabled` ← `os_signpost_id_make_with_pointer` ← `openDatabase` (`libsqlite3.dylib`) ← Python's `sqlite3`. Only with a Python whose `_sqlite3` module links Apple's `/usr/lib/libsqlite3.dylib` (`otool -L` on `_sqlite3.__file__`): Xcode's, and by the reports some Python 3.12+ builds; Homebrew's Python has its own SQLite and builds.
- **Cause:** Apple's SQLite calls `os_signpost` when it opens a database, and Apple's logging does not survive a fork without an exec once the process has used it: the child reads the parent's logging preferences at an address it does not have. The build opens `cas.db` at its start, and each forked worker opened a connection of its own (`Store.forked`). The same on macOS 27.0.1, 27.2 and 27.3, any game; `OS_ACTIVITY_MODE=disable` does not prevent it. A fork whose parent had opened no database opens one without harm, but the build needs the cache before its tables are complete, so the fork cannot come first.
- **Fix:** only the process that opened the cache uses it (`shader_cache.Served`). Each worker has a pipe to a thread of the build, which makes the worker's calls (`get`, `has`, `names`, `put`, `delete_prefix`, a batch) on the one connection under its lock, where the worker's thread stood when shaders were finished in threads; files are read and written by the worker (`put_file`, `to_file`). A worker's batch is its thread's transaction: whole or absent, and rolled back when the worker dies inside it. The workers stay forks, which is safe while they call nothing of Apple's frameworks themselves: their compilers and tools are exec'd.
- **Measurement:** Cuphead's 3,118 shaders on an M4 Pro (`J=14`, Homebrew's Python, an external drive), against main's workers with a connection each: an empty cache 49.5 and 44.9 s to 50.1, 53.3 and 47.4 s (61.8 s in threads), every shader restored from the cache 18.3 and 18.4 s to 18.8 and 16.7 s; the runs differ by more than the two designs do. `regress.py chain` on Cuphead: 3,131 of 3,131 shaders the same.
- **Open:** the reporters' Python builds are not confirmed (asked on the issue). Workers that are spawned, which would not depend on what a fork may call, were weighed and not built: each would read the build's tables again, which a fork shares. Up to the fork point the build takes 0.4 s and 58 MB on Cuphead and 3.7 s and 346 MB (peak resident) on Ocarina of Time's map, against about 35 MB for a forked worker, so 16 spawned workers there would hold up to about 5.5 GB instead of 0.6 GB; and `dmsh_build.py` would have to become a module whose start does not run a build (134 statements at its top level, the output clearing and the cache check among them).
- **Found on:** DRAGON BALL: Sparking! ZERO ([issue #2354](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/issues/2354); also Ocarina of Time on macOS 27.3, [issue #2362](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/issues/2362)'s side note).
- **Check:** `test_shader_cache.py`: `test_forked_workers_with_apples_sqlite` runs the forked workers under `/usr/bin/python3` when its SQLite is Apple's (with a connection in each worker, all of them are killed), `test_worker_dying_in_a_batch`.

### A library that depends on its source's file name

- **Symptom:** two builds of the same shaders give different `_bl` or `_bf` libraries for some keys, of the same size, 46 bytes apart, while their MSL is the same. A byte comparison of two library folders reports them, and `regress.py` can report them as changed variants.
- **Cause:** clang names a source's static initializer after its file (`_GLOBAL__sub_I_<key>_bl.metal`), and any function constant brings one, so the name and the library's stored hash change with the file's base name (its folder does not matter). Sources without function constants do not depend on it. The MSL-keyed Metal cache takes the same text for the same bytes, so shaders that share a source took the library of whichever compiled first, or their own when they compiled at once. In Breath of the Wild 3,936 of 10,560 pixel shaders share their `_bl` source and 1,710 their `_bf` source.
- **Fix:** `dmsh_pch.compile`, which every compile of a shader library goes through, passes the source on standard input (`-x metal -`), so no file name reaches the compiler. Metal's messages then say `<stdin>`, and a failed compile also prints the source's path. Libraries from an earlier compile keep their bytes in the Metal cache: a cold build differs from them once, in `_bl` and `_bf`. The compile records and the chain version did not change, as the code the libraries hold did not. `_check` in `dmsh_mtlcache.py` samples only `_b`, and the offline pipeline archive leaves function constants to the device, so neither ever saw the difference.
- **Measurement:** cold builds at `J=4` and `J=14` of 471 Breath of the Wild shaders chosen from shared sources: 15 of 2,595 Mac files and 13 of 2,123 iOS files differed, all `_bl`; with the source on standard input none did. Pokopia's 1,001 at `J=6` and `J=14`: none of 4,581 Mac and 3,579 iOS files.
- **Found on:** Pokémon Pokopia (resolved), The Legend of Zelda: Breath of the Wild (resolved).
- **Check:** `test_shader_pch.py` (`test_a_mac_library_does_not_depend_on_the_source_name`, `test_each_run_reads_the_source_from_its_start`).

### One compile for a pixel shader's side entry points

- **Symptom:** the Mac or iOS shader step of a game with many pixel shaders spends most of its Metal compiles on libraries that few draws load: Pokémon Pokopia's whole map took 366,805 compiles, about 57% of them for `_ba` and `_bl`, and its runs opened none of the 5,775 of each.
- **Cause:** the alpha test and the logic operations were each a whole copy of a pixel shader in a library of its own, compiled apart. Every pixel shader needs them, since which ones draw with either shows only in play: Xenoblade Chronicles 2, Skyward Sword HD, Dark Souls Remastered. Nothing in a game's files tells: every NVN game's binary names both functions, since the loader lists them all. A list per game would turn a miss into a wrong picture ([The alpha test through a library of its own](rendering.md#the-alpha-test-through-a-library-of-its-own), [Logic operations through a library of its own](rendering.md#logic-operations-through-a-library-of-its-own)), and compiling at run time has no `-O1`: GPU code grew by a median of 12% and up to 59%, Default and Size alike, and `-O2` took Odyssey's `alRenderMaterialUber` 25 minutes.
- **Fix:** `dm/gfx/tools/dmsh_side.py` makes one compile unit from a pixel shader's source: each entry point's body once, as a static function, and thin entry points around it, the shader's own and `<entry>_a` (`dmsh_alpha.py`) and `<entry>_l` (`dmsh_logic.py`). `metallib` gives every entry point a complete module whether or not the body is shared, and `metal-tt` compiles every unspecialized entry point of a library, which made the pipeline archive twice as large (1.85 to 3.84 MB for 25 bases). So `cut` splits the library in two, `<key>_b` with the shader's own entry points (+0.6% of archive) and `<key>_bx` with the others, loaded on first need (`at_fn`, `lo_fn`). A cut of one function is byte-identical to `metallib --function-name`, which takes only one name. The Metal cache keeps both pieces under the unit's digest (`compile_split`), so outputs stay clones. `<key>_b.metal` stays the plain source, and `dmsh_mtlcache.py` fill leaves out a library with a `_bx`, which was not compiled from it. A shader whose unit does not build gets its base from the plain source, no side library and a red line, and the host draws it without them. The readable-tile entry is a third piece of that same compile ([Cold Mac shader builds compile each pixel shader more than once](#cold-mac-shader-builds-compile-each-pixel-shader-more-than-once)).
- **Cost:** a shared body changes the inliner's decisions (a helper now has two callers), so `main0` is no longer byte-identical: GPU code +2.1% at the median and +3.7% at most, on 125 shaders of six games on an M4 Pro, pipeline builds 62.5 against 60.6 ms. Keeping `<key>_b` as it was with a second compile for `<key>_bx` would cost 66-68% of the three compiles' CPU instead of 34-36%.
- **Measurement:** Metal CPU per pixel shader on 125 shaders of six games, three compiles to one: Pokopia 1.503 to 0.512 s with the Mac's flags and 0.830 to 0.285 s with the iPhone's; the others 0.324 to 0.114 s and 0.245 to 0.087 s. Cold builds of 1,021 Pokopia shaders (`J=10`, other jobs on the Mac): Metal compiles 2,444 to 1,095 on the Mac and 2,445 to 1,094 for iOS; the whole build's CPU 1,399 to 670 s and 959 to 451 s; wall 273 to 195 s and 232 to 127 s; libraries 359 to 358 MB. All 1,021 `_b.metal` came out unchanged, and their reflections but for the chain version and the entry point names. Not measured on a device.
- **Found on:** Pokémon Pokopia.
- **Check:** `test_dmsh_side.py`, `test_dmsh_alpha.py`, `test_dmsh_logic.py`, `make check_t_alpha`, `make check_t_logic`.

### Cold Mac shader builds compile each pixel shader more than once

- **Symptom:** the first Mac shader build of a title, with an empty output directory and CAS and no keys, takes tens of minutes while `J` Metal workers stay busy. On an Apple M2 MacBook Pro at `J=8`, Mario Kart 8 Deluxe took 22:46 (1366 s) and Super Mario Odyssey 30:02 (1802 s).
- **Cause:** a pixel shader's code went through the Metal compiler several times. The readable-tile version `<key>_bf` was a source and a compile of its own (`fetch_finish`): 13,666 of Mario Kart 8 Deluxe's 29,651 shaders and 10,512 of Odyssey's 27,984. The integer entry points `main0_u` / `main0_m` (`fs_uint`) and the dual-source `main0_d` (`fs_dual`) were each a copy of `main0`'s body, which the compiler built again for each: 4,990 of Pokémon Legends: Arceus's 6,160 pixel shaders have `main0_u`. And each compile launched `metal` and `metallib` through `xcrun`, which looks the tool up again every time (about 4 ms a launch).
- **Fix:** for every game. `dmsh_side.shared_body` makes `main0`'s body one `always_inline` function, `dm_body_main0`, which `main0`, `main0_u` and `main0_d` call. `dmsh_fetch.fold` rewrites the rewritable samples in that body alone, as `(fx_i == k ? fb_k : sample)` with `fx_i` and `fb0`–`fb7` parameters of the body, and adds `main0_f`, which passes its function constants and the tile's colours; every other caller passes -1 and zeros, which the inlined body folds back to the plain sample, so `main0` and the side entry points declare no function constants. `ps_libraries` cuts `<key>_b`, `<key>_bx` and `<key>_bf` from the one compile (`dmsh_side.cut` with groups, `dmsh_mtlcache.compile_split` with a list); the host's `bf_fn` loads `main0_f`. What does not build is left out alone: a unit whose readable-tile version does not build is compiled again without it, one that does not build at all gives the base from the plain source, each with a red line. A shader with no rewritable sample gets `main0_f` only when its key is in `bf_want.tsv`, as before. `dmsh_build.py` finds `metal` and `metallib` once (`xcrun -f`) and passes `-isysroot` with the SDK, which gives the same `.air` and `.metallib` bytes as `xcrun -sdk macosx`; `MAC_RECORD` still names the `xcrun` command. Two consequences: a library cut with a `_bf` is not taken by `dmsh_mtlcache.py` fill (its base was not compiled from its `.metal`), and `dmsh_side.py` is part of the shader cache key (`CODE_FP`), since `fs_uint` and `fs_dual` call it when a shader misses the cache.
- **Measurement:** cold builds, `own_compilers`, `DMSH_TARGETS=mac`, no keys, `J=8`, Apple M2 MacBook Pro, of PR #1520's first form (the same fold, with copies still in `main0_d`): Mario Kart 8 Deluxe 22:46 (1366 s) to 14:47 (887 s), 1.54×; Super Mario Odyssey 30:02 (1802 s) to 22:23 (1343 s), 1.34×; every shader built in both. Metal CPU on 60 of Odyssey's pixel shaders (Apple M6): 25.8 to 13.6 s for 40 with a readable-tile version (0.53×), 30.5 to 19.6 s for 20 with integer entry points only (0.64×; `007d7e411ef14ebd` 24.5 to 13.6 s); the fold applied to the same 60 shaders as before; `main0`'s bitcode 5.7% smaller at the median and 0.7% larger at most. The `xcrun` lookup was about 4 of a launch's 10 ms with 2 GB resident; launching with `posix_spawn` instead of `subprocess` saved another 0.4–1.2 ms, which is not worth code of its own. Partial cold builds of the same pixel shaders on the previous chain and this one (Apple M6, translation included): Pokémon Legends: Arceus 239 in 24 to 18 s, Metroid Prime Remastered 182 in 26 to 19 s, each shader with the same libraries, entry points, side and readable-tile records and reflection, none failing (MPR's `ab809a7456f59614` has both `main0_d` and `main0_f`). GPU code and pipeline build time not measured.
- **Found on:** Mario Kart 8 Deluxe, Super Mario Odyssey.
- **Check:** `test_dmsh_fetch.py` (`OtherEntryPoints`: a `main0_d`, a copy of the body and a compile unit beside `main0_f`; `OutputMappingOnly`), `test_dmsh_side.py`, `test_shader_toolchain_cache.py`, `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_feedback check_t_alpha check_t_logic`.

### One compile for a vertex shader's libraries

- **Symptom:** a cold Mac or iOS shader build spends most of its Metal compiles on vertex shaders' copies of one body. Breath of the Wild's 19,592 shaders took 21,552 compiles, 2,405 of the build's 3,692 CPU seconds (240 s of wall on an M5 Pro at `J=18`): 839 s of that went to pulling libraries and 109 s to shadow-input libraries, each a second and third compile of a body `<key>_b` already holds three times.
- **Cause:** a vertex shader's body went through the optimizer nine times in two compiles, twelve in three for a shader with integer inputs. `<key>_b` held it in `main0`, `main0_z` (`vs_depth`) and `main0_gs` (the geometry capture entry nxnative's chain made); the pulling library `<key>_bp` (`dmsh_pull.py`), a source and a compile of its own, in `main0`, `main0_z` and twice in each wide-line entry; the shadow-input library `<key>_bv` (`dmsh_vin.py`) in three more. A pixel shader's entry points had the same shape before [One compile for a pixel shader's side entry points](#one-compile-for-a-pixel-shaders-side-entry-points).
- **Fix:** for every game. `dmsh_side.vs_unit` makes one compile unit of a vertex shader that has a pulling library: the body once, as `dm_vs_body`, `always_inline` in the entry points pipelines are made of (`main0`, `main0_z`, the pulling `main0_p` / `main0_zp`, the shadow-input `main0_v` / `main0_zv`), and one out-of-line copy, `dm_vs_body_o`, for the wide-line entries `main0_wl` / `main0_z_wl`, which few draws take and which ran the body twice each. `vs_libraries` in `dmsh_build.py` compiles the unit once a target and cuts `<key>_b`, `<key>_bp` and `<key>_bv` from it (`dmsh_side.cut`, `dmsh_mtlcache.compile_split`), so each library holds the entry points it held and `<key>_b.metal` stays the plain source. One unit cannot name its entry points all `main0`: the reflection's `pull` and `vin` name them, and the host (`side_entry` in `nvn_shader.inc`) and `pso_offline.py` (`vertex_entry`) read the names. What stays as it was:
  - The rarer entry point `main0_discard` (`dmsh_entry.vs_discard`, for a shader with writes) is a whole copy of the body: it returns nothing, where the body returns the outputs. So were `main0_gs`, `main0_tf` and the layer entry points, which the chain no longer makes ([A shader the chain does not translate stops the split and the build](#a-shader-the-chain-does-not-translate-stops-the-split-and-the-build)).
  - A shader without a pulling library (no stage input, or one `dmsh_pull.variant` does not fit) compiles its plain source.
  - The shadow-input library `<key>_bv` comes from the unit whenever the shader has one. A shader with layer entry points kept it a compile of its own, as that library repeated `main0_l` and one unit cannot hold it twice (8 of Pokémon Scarlet's shaders, none of the two Zelda games'); the chain makes no layer entry points any more.
  - With `DMSH_IOS_PULL=0` the iPhone's unit leaves the pulling entry points out, and with no shadow-input library either its `<key>_b` is the plain source's compile.
  - A unit that does not build, or a source of a shape `vs_unit` does not know, gets the three compiles with a red line (`its libraries do not build from one compile`). None of the 38,402 shaders tested used this fallback. `dmsh_mtlcache.py` fills the standalone cache from earlier builds. Each target's reflection records `chain_metal_cut` when its base came from a unit, so the cache skips that base even after iOS packing removes its pulling library. A synthetic test packs that image with two standalone shaders that pass the cache's sample check and verifies that a later standalone compile produces its own library.
- **Forms measured and not taken:**
  - The body as a plain `static` function that every entry point calls is one optimization of the body for all of them (64% less Metal CPU a vertex shader, against 41%), but the GPU compiler then decides the inlining for each pipeline: `main0`'s bitcode grew 4% at the median and pipeline builds took 1.3 to 3.7% longer. Not without a measurement on a device.
  - The out-of-line copy is never `noinline`. Marked so and shared with `main0_gs` (then made), it changed that entry's captured words in 24 of 52 shaders, by up to 12,544 units in the last place.
  - `main0_gs` calling the body, with its capture epilogue once after the call instead of at every return, saves more (53% instead of 41%) and captured other low bits in 1 of 52 shaders (Tears of the Kingdom's `0ef972796032c959`, six returns: 186 words of 18 of 6,000 vertices, by up to 1,778 units in the last place). `main0_gs` returned nothing and was not invariant; the chain no longer makes it.
- **Cost:** every output compared on the GPU is bit for bit what the three compiles gave (Measurement). The libraries are not byte-identical. Entry point by entry point, over the 38,402 shaders of three games: `main0_gs`, `main0_discard`, `main0_tf` and the layer entry points are the same bitcode in every shader; `main0_z` in 36,250; `main0` in 7,628, since it applies the depth line once, after the call, where the copy had it before every return (0.3 to 0.4% smaller at the median, between 2.4% smaller and 0.8% larger); the pulling and shadow-input entry points have other names, within 1.2% smaller and 0.5% larger; the wide-line entries, which call the out-of-line copy, between 3.4% smaller and 7.3% larger (0.1 to 0.3% smaller at the median). `<key>_bv` is half its size (Breath of the Wild's 50.1 to 27.0 MB): it no longer repeats `main0_gs`.
- **Measurement:** on an M5 Pro, macOS 26.7.3, GPU `applegpu_g17s`.
  - Metal CPU for a vertex shader's libraries (compile and link, the best of three, 40 shaders evenly spread a game): Breath of the Wild 216.3 to 127.0 ms with the Mac's flags (41.3% less) and 218.4 to 128.1 ms with the iPhone's; Tears of the Kingdom 225.7 to 135.3 ms (40.1% less) and 225.3 to 135.5 ms.
  - A cold Mac build of Breath of the Wild (`own_compilers.py`, `DMSH_TARGETS=mac`, `J=18`, an idle Mac), the commit before against this one: wall 240.4 to 188.8 s, CPU 3,692 to 2,859 s, Metal compiles 21,552 to 13,957 (2,405 to 1,685 s), links 21,550 to 13,955 (413 to 311 s). All 8,888 shaders with a pulling library came from a unit.
  - `regress.py chain` on Breath of the Wild, Tears of the Kingdom and Pokémon Scarlet: every shader builds on both sides (19,592, 28,669 and 30,142), no MSL changes, and the reflections and side libraries of 8,888, 14,549 and 14,965 vertex shaders change (the names); no pipeline base's interface fails.
  - On the GPU, 26 vertex shaders of each Zelda game (16 evenly spread, the 4 largest sources, 6 more with integer inputs), 2,000 random triangles each (6,000 vertices; random but valid buffers, uniforms and textures, not the games' own data), 512 × 512: the depth image and every `float4` output of `main0`, `main0_z`, `main0_p` and `main0_zp` (26 of 26 shaders a game) and of `main0_v` and `main0_zv` (13 of 13 and 9 of 9) are bit-identical to those of the plain source's `main0` / `main0_z`; so are `main0_wl`'s images (26 of 26) and `main0_gs`'s 22,464,000 captured words a game. Controls: `x` one unit in the last place larger is seen in 49 of the 52 shaders, the depth line's 0.5 one unit larger in all 52.
  - Open measurements include iPhone GPU comparisons, device pipeline build times and production archive sizes.
- **Found on:** Breath of the Wild, Tears of the Kingdom.
- **Check:** `test_dmsh_vsunit.py` (the unit's text, its cut libraries, `main0_discard` byte for byte the plain source's, the build step with its fallbacks), `test_shader_toolchain_cache.py` (the Metal cache's fill), `test_pso_offline.py`; `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_vs_unit` (every entry point of a unit against the three compiles on the GPU, with a control), `check_t_pso_bundle` (the reflection's names in the host and the offline archive), `check_t_pull_inv check_t_wline check_t_vin`.

### Concurrent warm tasks prepare each shader once

- **Symptom:** startup tasks can prepare the same shader more than once, even though only one object enters the warm dictionary. A measured Tears of the Kingdom startup avoided 320 duplicate preparations; this is not a steady-frame-rate claim.
- **Cause:** an `S` task and an `R` task, or two shader pairs, can miss the same key before either publishes. `shader_load` and the pulling-entry preparation used to run independently for each miss. Discarding the losing `NvShader` does not undo the strong function-descriptor registrations (`fn4_note`, `fn_spec`) or constant-bank reservations (`c1_store`). These ownership mechanisms are deliberate; source inspection alone does not establish a leak or its size.
- **Fix:** `warm_shader` installs a per-key `WarmLoad` under the dictionary lock. The owner prepares the library, reflection, functions, argument encoder, c1 storage and optional pulling entry outside that lock, then publishes before releasing its waiters. A cached lookup allocates nothing. Other keys can prepare concurrently. A failed warm load stays `NSNull` only for that prewarm, matching the previous warm-call behavior; `warm_finish` drops warm dictionaries after all tasks join, and demand loading retains its existing retry behavior. This does not coalesce demand/prefetch loads racing a background prewarm (`DM_PSO_PREPARE=0`).
- **Measurement:** a Tears of the Kingdom startup probe observed 1,537 distinct shader keys. The old racing path performed 1,857 preparations and discarded 320 successful duplicates; per-key coalescing performed 1,537 preparations and discarded none. Observed startup times were 6.63 s before and 6.51 s / 7.15 s with coalescing, so these runs establish duplicate avoidance, not a wall-time improvement or a memory leak. A Breath of the Wild 1.9.0 probe on the same Mac and chain went from 844 preparations of 819 keys to 819, at 3.07 s before and 2.78 s / 2.83 s with coalescing: the same limit applies.
- **Found on:** Tears of the Kingdom, through source review of the shared prewarm lookup/load/publish path and a normal-start Mac probe; Breath of the Wild, the same probe.
- **Regression seam:** `t_pso_warm` parses actual `S` and `R` manifest tasks sharing a vertex key and gates the first real `MTLDevice` library load. A test-owned dictionary proxy observes the second same-key lookup under the production dictionary lock before the owner is released; a third key must finish independently while the owner is held. Actual device library-load counts check that the shared key is prepared once and cached hits do not reload it; both callers retain one shader identity. It also checks warm negative caching and a later successful demand retry after reflection appears. The existing `check_t_pso_warm` target runs both MTLDevice and Metal 4 paths.

### A prebuild pool sized from the Super cores

- **Symptom:** the Mac's pipeline prebuild (`DM_PSO_PREBUILD=tool`, the NSP script's `pso_prebuild.log`) runs on far fewer workers than the Mac has: `1 workers` on an M6, where Metroid Prime Remastered's 20,233 pairs took 1,725 s and Link's Awakening's 17,742 took 667 s, and 4 on an M5 Pro, where Donkey Kong Bananza's 21,225 took 6.5 to 7.5 minutes.
- **Cause:** each worker waits on one synchronous compile, and the tool's pool was the "performance cores" less one, read from `hw.perflevel0.physicalcpu`. On the chips with three core levels, level 0 is only the Super cores: 2 on an M6 (beside 4 Performance and 6 Efficiency), 5 on an M5 Pro (beside 10 others). Metal runs 6 and 14 compiles at once there (`maximumConcurrentCompilationTaskCount`).
- **Fix:** the tool has `maximumConcurrentCompilationTaskCount` workers (`compile_tasks`; the active cores where Metal gives less than 2). `DM_PSO_PREBUILD_THREADS` still sets them. The prepare step keeps the active cores: extra workers only wait, and no iPhone has reported its count. **Open:** one rule for both pools needs an iPhone's count and first-start time. The background pools (`pp_bg_queue`, the prebuild without a prepare step) still take a share of level 0, one queue on an M6; nothing has been reported from that.
- **Measurement:** M5 Pro, macOS 27.0 (26A428), the prebuild tool on Bananza's app libraries (chain `2612ffd16287`) with hosts built from that app's commit without and with the change, other jobs' Metal compiles running beside both. All 21,225 pairs: 393 and 452 s on 4 workers, 187 and 272 s on 14 (1.8 times faster on average). M6, macOS 27.2, Skyward Sword's 2,125 pairs from an empty archive, another session's shader build beside them, in the quietest of three rounds: 45.1 s on 1 worker, 28.0 on 3, 19.2 on 6 (Metal's count), 21.3 on 9, 18.8 on 12. The host with this fix, Metroid Prime Remastered's first 2,000 pairs on the M6: 52 s on its default 6 workers from a cold cache, 194 s on 1 worker afterwards. **`shouldMaximizeConcurrentCompilation` is not set:** Metal reports the same count with it and without it on both Macs, its effect did not rise above the noise on either (M5 Pro, from a cold cache: 62 pairs/s on 4 workers without it, 53 with it, 98 on 14 without it, 112 with it), and [Apple's guide](https://developer.apple.com/documentation/metal/using-the-metal-4-compilation-api.md) gives it as the Metal 3 setting, while the prebuild compiles through `MTL4Compiler`.
- **Found on:** Donkey Kong Bananza.
- **Check:** `t_pso_warm` checks that the tool's default follows Metal's count.

### Function constants specialized at load

- **Symptom:** `fragmentFunction main0 cannot be used to build a pipeline state` (a Metal validation assertion). Later, the same text in a `SIGABRT` under `validateWithDevice` while shaders load and Metal 4 pipelines build on other threads.
- **Cause:** SPIRV-Cross declares an optional function constant, `spvLinearTextureAlignmentOverride`, in shaders that read a texel buffer, and Metal refuses any function with function constants until it is specialized. The abort: the loaders (`shader_load`'s entries, `side_lib_entry` for `pull_load` and `vin_fn`, the `_wl` entry, `at_fn`) noted each entry in `g_fn4` unspecialized (`fn4_note(f, …, nil)`) and only then specialized it (`fn_spec`). `g_fn4` is keyed by the `MTLFunction`'s identity, and Metal hands out one `MTLFunction` per function content, device-wide, across libraries (on this Mac's M2 Pro with macOS 27.2.1, two libraries made from one source give the same object). So loading a shader again, or another with the same entry, put an unspecialized descriptor back for a while over the specialized one a pipeline build on another thread was reading.
- **Fix:** `shader_load` specializes such functions, with `dm_blend_off` false when declared and the other constants at their defaults. On the Metal 4 path the pipeline's descriptor specializes them, not the library: [Functions with function constants specialized by the pipeline, not the library](#functions-with-function-constants-specialized-by-the-pipeline-not-the-library). Every loader takes its entries through `fn_load` (`nvn_shader.inc`), which specializes before it notes anything (`shader_load`'s main, dual-source, discard and mesh object entries, the side libraries' and the alpha test's); `fn4_note` makes no unspecialized note for a function that declares constants and never replaces a specialized one with one.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests check_t_pso_archive`: no note for an unspecialized function with constants, a specialized note kept against an unspecialized one, and four threads of `fn_load` and `fn4_of` that never see an unspecialized note.
- **Found on:** Pokémon Legends: Z-A (one imgui pixel shader). The abort upstream, nxnative `5491ae39`.

### Functions with function constants specialized by the pipeline, not the library

- **Symptom:** with Metal 4 pipelines on (the Mac; on iOS `pso_mtl4 on` or `DM_PSO_MTL4=1`), Apple's performance HUD says `Compiled 2 shaders. Total compilation time is 102.70ms. Consider adopting MTL4Compiler for more fine grained compilations` during play, when a shader with function constants loads.
- **Cause:** a function that declares function constants must be specialized before it goes into a pipeline ([Function constants specialized at load](#function-constants-specialized-at-load)). The host did that through `MTLLibrary`'s `newFunctionWithName:constantValues:error:` when it loaded the shader (`shader_load`) and the libraries made from it (the pulling and shadow-input vertex entries, `pull_load` and `vin_fn`; the alpha test, `at_fn`). That call compiles through the MTLDevice, synchronously, on the thread that loads the shader. A Metal 4 pipeline does not need it: `fn4_note` already gives MTL4Compiler an `MTL4SpecializedFunctionDescriptor` with the same values.
- **Fix:** `fn_spec` in `nvn_shader.inc`. With the Metal 4 compiler (`pso4_compiler`) the `MTLFunction` stays unspecialized and is noted with its constant values, so MTL4Compiler specializes it with the pipeline. Its reflection and argument encoders are the same as the specialized function's. A pipeline the MTLDevice builds after all (`pso_build`'s fallback, when a function has no Metal 4 descriptor, such as the `DM_NVN_HILITE` override) takes the function `fn_dev` specializes through the library, once per function. A stable record per function serializes overlapping first uses, so they receive the same specialized function; unrelated functions can specialize concurrently, and registering the entry again preserves its cached result. Unchanged:
  - Compute shaders are still specialized at load: the MTLDevice builds their pipelines.
  - Without the Metal 4 compiler, functions are specialized as before.
  - The variants that differ only in their constant values still specialize through the library: the logic operation entries (`lo_fn`) and the readable-tile versions (`bf_fn`). Their pipelines would need one function object per set of values, and `newFunctionWithName:` may hand back the same object.
- **Found on:** Animal Crossing: New Horizons on an iPhone 17 Pro Max with Metal 4 pipelines turned on: the HUD warning when a shader loaded during play. On the Mac, with a probe that logs each MTLDevice compile, the specializations came from `shader_load` during the prepare step and again when a shader loaded during play. After the fix none were left, and the picture was the same.
- **Check:** `t_pso_archive`. A function with a constant stays unspecialized, its descriptor specializes it, and a pipeline is made through the Metal 4 compiler. With a fragment function that has no descriptor, the pipeline goes through the MTLDevice with the vertex function specialized once. A gated library holds the first specialization while a second caller requests it: both receive one shared result, an unrelated function progresses, and registering the same entry again does not specialize it again.

### The precompile look-ahead restores the scissor

- **Symptom:** the picture bright green and white from some scene on, in runs with pipelines compiled on demand (prewarm lists off or short) and in none with `DM_NVN_PREDICT=0`.
- **Cause:** after a pipeline miss the replay looks ahead through the rest of the submission, replaying state only, to compile the next pipelines in the background (`pso_predict_rest`). It restored `S`, but `state_op` also sets the scissor, which lives in `R`: the replay went on with the last scissor of the submission until its next `OP_SCISSOR`, so the draws in between were clipped to another pass's rectangle.
- **Fix:** the scissor is saved and restored with `S`; the fusion probe (`fz_probe_cov2`) already restored all of `R`. Every game.
- **Found on:** OCTOPATH TRAVELER II: from Castti's opening flashback and the battlefield of Hikari's opening on.
- **Check:** found by bisecting with four runs started at once on copies of one save: `DM_NVN_PREDICT=0` fixed it, a look-ahead that compiled nothing did not, and of its parts only the state ops brought it back; four runs green before the fix, four right after it.

### Pipeline look-ahead must leave future attachments unresolved

- **Symptom:** a crash in `-[AGXG16XFamilyRenderContext_mtlnext setRenderPipelineState:]` from `draw_` with direct Metal encoding. The pass dump shows a pipeline miss and an end in `gpu_read_wait` before the draw reaches its pipeline bind.
- **Cause:** `pso_predict_rest_` resolved the attachments of future `SetRenderTargets` records to get their formats and sample counts. Resolving a sparse texture can read GPU-written storage for a CPU upload. Its wait ends and commits the open pass, so the demanding draw retains an encoder that has ended. Deferred encoding can hide the fault because its replay clears the real encoder when processing the end record.
- **Fix:** use `tex_made` for format and sample-count lookups. Skip predictions for a pass with an unresolved attachment and resume when the scan reaches known targets. Actual replay resolves textures at their first use. This also keeps speculative uploads and GPU waits out of pipeline prediction.
- **Check:** `t_pso_pending` sends the look-ahead through an unresolved target and then an existing one. On the old code it attempts a texture allocation and queues a pipeline for the wrong attachment set. With the fix it queues only the pipeline for the existing target.
- **Found on:** Mario Kart World 1.8.0.

### The look-ahead hands every later draw's pipeline to the background

- **Symptom:** on entering a new area or opening a menu for the first time, with no archive or prewarm list covering it, frames of 0.2–2 s whose `[longframe]` line is nearly all pipeline work, and `draws that waited: pulling base not made yet` in the `pipelines up to frame` lines.
- **Cause:** after a submission's first pipeline miss, the look-ahead (`pso_predict_rest`) handed at most two of the rest to the background (`PSO_PREDICT_BUDGET`, `PSO_PEND_ACTIVE`), a bound set without a measurement. It applied to the keys of the draws the look-ahead met, with their attachments as they are, although those draws come later in the same submission. A draw with no stand-in whose pipeline was not admitted compiled it on the submit thread, so a frame with dozens of new pipelines compiled them one after another.
- **Fix:** each of those draws' pipelines is admitted (`pso_pend_kick_next`, bounded by the 384-slot table), after the same exclusions as before: a key whose base is made (`pso_base_made`), one that already drew with a stand-in (`g_pso_hy`) and one whose pulling stand-in is ready (`hybrid_get`) take nothing. Only the guesses with other depth / stencil attachments keep the bound of two. The draw's own pipeline is built before the look-ahead runs, and a draw whose build is still queued makes it itself (`pso_pend_take`), so none waits behind the others; the Metal compiler service builds them side by side. Host only, every game.
- **Found on:** FINAL FANTASY RESONANCE (PR #1344, Apple M5 Max): a save loaded into Grandshelt Castle, then the System Menu and the World Map, with no archive, lists or prebuild and the system's Metal cache off: 2.78–2.81 s of pipeline waits, worst frame 2.19–2.23 s, before; 0.74–0.79 s, worst 0.71–0.74 s, after (two runs each). Bounding all of them by 16 instead gave 1.9–2.0 s. Repeated on OCTOPATH TRAVELER II (Apple M4 Pro, `bench.py`, the hosts alternating, two runs each): its smoke route from the settings save through New Game and Hikari's opening, 290 s, cold the same way: draws that waited 200–205 for 5.0–6.0 s before, 120–128 for 3.0–3.3 s after; 64–70 builds admitted against 130–151; the look-ahead's own time 0.03–0.17 s either way; the longest frame in the window 1.32–1.58 s against 0.62–1.07 s (the long frames left are the game's loads, with no pipeline work); median frame time unchanged, no red line. The same builds on a fixed set of workers taking them in the draws' order, the draw making any it reached still queued, waited longer in a three-way A/B of one host (two runs each): 10 workers 2.61–2.64 s, 5 workers 2.78–2.95 s, against 2.50–2.53 s with every build started at once. Even with 5 workers almost no draw reached its build still queued, so the order was not what held the draws up; the Metal compiler service builds more of them side by side.
- **Check:** `dm/gfx/tests/t_pso_pending.m` (`test_next_draws_pass_the_guess_limit`). Without `MTL_SHADER_CACHE_SIZE=0` a second run of the same route finds every pipeline in the system's cache and waits almost nothing either way, so cold comparisons need it, with `DM_PSO_ARCHIVE=off`, `DM_PSO_MANIFEST=off` and `DM_PSO_PREBUILD=0`.

### GPU frame captures need pipelines built through the device

- **Symptom:** a GPU frame capture (`DM_GPU_CAPTURE`, or Xcode with `MTL_CAPTURE_ENABLED=1`) fails with `Capturing Render pipeline specialization is not supported`, or the capture layer crashes on the pipeline archive; a capture of the `MTLDevice` is refused. Before the fix below asked Metal, a run that did not capture but had `MTL_CAPTURE_ENABLED=0` or `DM_GPU_CAPTURE=0` left in its environment logged `hybrid pipelines off`, and every pipeline it had not built yet stalled its frame (an M1 Ocarina of Time session: 467 stalls of about 170 ms, one 84 s freeze with 255 pipelines in a submission).
- **Cause:** Metal cannot capture pipelines made from Metal 4 bases (`DM_PSO_MTL4`, the default on the Mac), and its Metal 4 to 3 conversion for the capture layer crashed on the pipeline archive. A device the replay drives through Metal 4 cannot be the capture object.
- **Fix:** a run that Metal lets capture builds its render pipelines through the `MTLDevice`, without bases or archive (`pso4_on`). Metal answers that itself, once at start (`MTLCaptureManager supportsDestination:` a GPU trace document or the developer tools): on the Mac when `MTL_CAPTURE_ENABLED` reads as a non-zero number (`1`; unset, empty, `0`, `true` or `YES` do not), and when Xcode starts the run for capture. `DM_GPU_CAPTURE` does not decide it: alone it cannot capture, and `DM_CTL` can set it later in a run that can. The log says which path the run took: `render pipelines through the MTLDevice: Metal may capture GPU frames in this run (…)` or `render pipelines from Metal 4 bases and the pipeline archive`. `gpu_capture_frame` `gpu_capture_frame` captures the Metal 4 queue (`g_q4`): work on the old queue (present, some uploads) is not in the trace. Such a run builds pipelines more slowly than a normal one, so its frame times are not the game's. Every game.
- **Found on:** The Legend of Zelda: Breath of the Wild (Mac and iPhone); the leftover-variable stalls on [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md) (M1, [#2688](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/pull/2688)).

### A cold Metal cache looks like a long load

- **Symptom:** the first boot sat on the loading clock for about a minute, the CPU idle, with every file read done after 12 s.
- **Cause:** that run compiled its Metal libraries and pipelines on first use (cold cache). With a warm cache the title is up after about 15 s; what looked like a 40-second wait in the read log (`DM_FSLOG`) was the title screen itself, which reads nothing.
- **Check:** screenshots every few seconds (`ctl.sh snap` from a script) told the two apart.
- **Found on:** Bayonetta 3 (SDK 13.3.2).

### Native mesh draws omitted while their pipeline compiles

- **Symptom:** missing geometry or bright patches just after loading, which disappear as background pipeline compilation finishes.
- **Cause:** `mtk_pso` started an asynchronous native task/mesh pipeline build and returned no pipeline; the draw was omitted until a later frame found the completed pipeline. This copied the geometry-mesh optimization's behavior without its equivalent compute fallback. Native mesh draws have no such replacement. Metal validation also rejected the helper descriptor assigning a mesh function to `vertexFunction` before copying its raster state.
- **Fix:** compile the demanded native mesh pipeline before issuing its first draw, then reuse the cached pipeline. The shared descriptor helper leaves `vertexFunction` unset for a native mesh shader; the mesh descriptor receives it as `meshFunction`. A cold pipeline can delay the first frame but cannot silently remove its draw. `t_mesh_first_use` exercises real NVN submissions with mesh-only, task/mesh, depth-only and ordinary vertex shaders: the old host failed 10 of 42 pixel/state checks; the fixed host passes all 42 with Metal validation in both encoding modes.
- **Found on:** [Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md), M4 Pro startup white patches. The synthetic GPU regression verifies the missing-draw mechanism; the corrected game's startup remains to be checked with the queue fix.

### The host's own shaders compiled from source at run time

- **Symptom:** Apple's performance HUD (`DM_METAL_HUD=1`) says `Compiled 2 shaders. Total compilation time is 102.70ms. Consider adopting MTL4Compiler for more fine grained compilations` during play, with a dip in the frame-rate graph at that moment, for example when a new room first issues a scissored clear.
- **Cause:** the host compiled the shaders it writes itself from MSL source at run time (`newLibraryWithSource:`): clears, copies, `nvnCommandBufferDrawTexture`, the RGB565 red / blue exchange, index conversions, the present and the GPU deswizzle (and, in nxnative, the built-in kernels, geometry packing, transform feedback and the OpenGL layer's clear quad). The thread that asked waits for the compiler. The clear triangles were one library per colour slot and type (`snprintf`), up to 24, each compiled when a clear of that kind was first met: about 50 ms each on an iPhone, on the submitting thread. `MTL4Compiler`'s `newLibraryWithDescriptor:` with a source is no way around it: it calls the device's `newLibraryWithSource:` itself (a check with a swizzled device shows the call), and the HUD counts that too.
- **Fix:** the sources are `.metal` files in `dm/gfx/msl` (the clears in one library: `clr_fs_<slot>_<float|uint|int>`). `dm/gfx/tools/host_msl.py` compiles them when the host is built (`make -C dm/host`, `MSL_SDK` `macosx`). It writes `dm/host/gen/msl-<sdk>/host_msl_gen.h`, which holds each library's metallib and source. `host_msl_lib` (`dm/gfx/nvn/host_msl.m`) loads one by name with `newLibraryWithData:`, once per device, so nothing is compiled; the pipelines are made as before. A build without the Metal toolchain carries the sources only: the host then compiles each library once and logs a `[RED]` line (`compiled from its source in … ms`). The first load logs `host shader libraries: N, compiled for <sdk> by <metal version>` (11 `.metal` files now). The diagnostic overrides (`DM_NVN_HILITE`, `DM_NVN_MAGSCAN`, `DM_NVN_TEXDUMP`) still compile theirs from source.
- **Found on:** Animal Crossing: New Horizons, iPhone 17 Pro Max: the HUD's compile warning in play, from clear-triangle libraries compiled when a new room first issued a scissored clear.
- **Check:** `t_host_msl` loads every library, with a swizzled `newLibraryWithSource:` that counts 0 calls, and finds all 24 clear functions. `t_deswz_gpu` uses the built libraries.
- **Build inputs:** Mac libraries use `MACOS_MIN` from `dm/mac/min_os.mk`, matching the host's deployment target; otherwise a newer SDK emits libraries older supported Macs cannot load and the host falls back to runtime compilation. Every build rechecks the compiler, SDK, deployment target and source contents, reusing the header only when all match. Installing the Metal toolchain after a source-only build therefore regenerates the libraries without a clean build.
- **Build callers:** the packing script and regression harness generate the Mac header before checking their cached host executables, and include it in the host fingerprint. A dry run alone does not generate the header.

### Raw pool table words that predate the pools

- **Symptom:** a raw global memory read of a memory pool made after the shader's arguments were encoded gets 0, though the pool exists when the GPU runs it; a task shader that reads both ends of a range this way loops `(0 - 1) >> 5` times and hangs the GPU. The memory report's `raw pool table words at commit` line counts the words that were out of date.
- **Cause:** an argument block holds the address of the pool table that was current when it was encoded (`gmem_map_gpu`). A pool made later in the same submission, or on another thread while it is encoded, is not in that table, and the table is rebuilt only for the next block that is encoded. A save load makes more than 4,000 pools in a burst, so many blocks are encoded with a table that lags.
- **Fix:** the words are noted as they are encoded (`gmem_slot_note`) and, at each commit of the command buffer, whole, segment or the commit before an indirect-count multi-draw waits for its count (`mdi_wait`), all of which end it through `cb_commit`, pointed at a table of the pools as they are then (`gmem_commit_patch`). A new table is built when the pools changed, not refilled in place, as earlier segments may be reading the old one; the replaced one goes through the deferred release. The write log of a block is a bitmap of the entries of the table its shader searches, so the log follows its table (`NvGmlLog`): `gml_read` decodes its bits against the table the block holds at the commit, not the one it was encoded with. The idea is #1407's, which rewrites the words at commit in its own stack.
- **Found on:** The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time (`0400486024DEC000`, profile in PR #1299): on the author's stack, loading the outdoor save, 5,729 of 154,107 words held an older table than the current one by frame 5,100 and 2,582 held the current one behind the pools. Whether this is what is left of the hang in issue #1326, minutes after a clean table growth, is open.
- **Check:** `t_gmem_words` (words noted, a pool made after them, an older table, a table that lags, a commit with no words, the commit before a count wait).

### Extended products behind spirv-cross's optnone helper

- **Symptom:** integer divisions by a constant cost GPU time that the compiler should have folded away. The MSL holds calls such as `(spvMulExtended<_2, uint>(uint(_5), 16u))._m1`, and spirv-cross defines `spvMulExtended` as `[[clang::optnone]]`, so each product stays a call to an unoptimized function.
- **Cause:** spirv-cross writes `OpUMulExtended` and `OpSMulExtended` through one template that it keeps unoptimized, because Metal's compiler may fail on an inlined `mulhi` (its comment at `SPVFuncImplMulExtended` in `spirv_msl.cpp`). Switch 2 shaders divide by constants through the high word of such a product: Ocarina of Time's MSL has 348,587 of these calls, all scalar `uint` by a literal and 98% of them by a power of two. Four Switch 1 games checked (Fire Emblem: Three Houses, Minecraft Dungeons II, Octopath Traveler II, Sonic Superstars) have none.
- **Fix:** `mul_extended` in `dmsh_build.py` adds overloads for scalar `uint` and `int` operands beside spirv-cross's helper. They are always inline and take the halves of the 64-bit product, with no `mulhi`. Overload resolution prefers them to the template, so every scalar call leaves the barrier and the compiler folds its constant; a power of two becomes a shift. Vector calls keep the template. A helper that is not exactly as spirv-cross writes it leaves the shader unchanged. [PR #1741](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/pull/1741) first took out the calls with a literal other than a power of two (5,679 of them); that left the gameplay route below unchanged.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md#frame-rate). On an M4 Pro, the Kokiri Forest deck route's GPU time fell from 15.55 to 14.76 ms a frame and its frame rate rose from 54.0 to 59.1 fps. The title movie's GPU time fell from 12.96 to 12.44 ms. On an M1 Pro, PR #1741's partial version took the title movie from 26.57 to 28.40 fps.
- **Check:** `test_dmsh_extended_multiply.py`. On the Mac GPU, it compares unsigned and signed products by literals (power of two or not) and by variables, over 65,536 input pairs including the edge values, with 64-bit products on the CPU. It compiles the kernel for macOS and iOS and checks that only the vector call is left calling the template.

### Signed integer overflow in translated shaders

- **Symptom:** integer results in a shader come out wrong where the game's code relies on signed arithmetic wrapping: a packed field unpacked by hand, an overflow test, a sign extension. In Ocarina of Time, one section of an otherwise dark wall in the Deku Tree (Gohma's room) was lit brightly and shimmered while the camera moved. Only models drawn with task / mesh shaders were affected; the vertex-shader models beside them were lit correctly.
- **Cause:** SPIR-V, GLSL and the Switch's GPUs wrap on signed overflow. spirv-cross writes SPIR-V's `OpIMul` / `OpIAdd` on signed values as plain `int` arithmetic in MSL, where overflow is undefined, so Metal's front end optimizes as if it cannot happen. Ocarina's mesh shader `35785cddc971290c` (task `a5288d0a6c4971fd`, pixel `f4f7e6955d37f40c`) sign-extends the low half of a word for the lightmap / light-probe UV: `a = as_type<int>(w) * 65536`, then `(a >> 31) << 16 | uint(a) >> 16`. The compiler, assuming the multiply did not overflow, took the sign bits from bits 15-30 of `w` (`(w << 1) & 0xffff0000`) instead of from bit 15. The UV's x then came out far too large (13184 / 18432 / 31744 where y gave 0.20 / 0.28 / 0.48), the probe texture was sampled at the wrong texels, and the pixel shader's spherical-harmonics ambient term read about 8 instead of 0.001. On an older chain the translator wrote the extraction without the sign extension, and the fold did not happen.
- **Fix:** every Metal compile of translated shaders passes `-fwrapv`: `dmsh_build.py`. The flag also drops `inbounds` from pointer arithmetic, because Metal's clang has no separate `-fwrapv-pointer`. It also makes the compiler keep loop bounds that may wrap (an extra `smax` before a loop). In a sample of 1,000 of Ocarina's shaders, compiled to LLVM IR on an older chain, 12 came out identical, 746 differed only in their `nsw` / `nuw` / `inbounds` flags, and 242 had other changes. None of it shows on the GPU. On Ocarina's `kokiri-deck` route ([`bench.tsv`](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/bench.tsv)) on an M6 (12-core GPU, 2026-11-09), both sides built from the same main with their own libraries and warmed pipeline archives, the medians of three runs a side were 9.94 / 10.51 ms GPU p50 / p95 and 59.4 fps without the flag, and 9.91 / 10.43 ms and 59.6 fps with it. Runs of one side differ by about 0.1 ms.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md#found-on-this-game), found by nawangwe ([PR #1844](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/pull/1844)) on an M4 Pro.
- **Check:** `test_dmsh_wrapv.py` compiles the sign extension, an overflow test (`x + 0x7fffffff < x`) and a doubling and halving with `dmsh_build.py`'s command and checks the results on the Mac's GPU over 65,536 inputs. It also checks that the iPhone command keeps the shift and that all four tools pass the flag. Without `-fwrapv`, the GPU check and the iPhone check both fail.

### A jump Apple's GPU compiler cannot encode (a tested bit 0's mask)

- **Symptom:** building a render pipeline with one pixel shader ends Apple's compiler service: `[RED] pipeline prebuild: the base of … failed: Compilation failed due to an interrupted connection: XPC_ERROR_CONNECTION_INTERRUPTED. This error occurred after multiple retries.`, the pair in `prebuild_failed.tsv`, and a `MTLCompilerService` crash report (`EXC_CRASH`, `SIGABRT`) whose stack is `fatalErrorHandler` ← `llvm::report_fatal_error` ← `llvm::MCObjectStreamer::emitInstruction` ← `AGCLLVMUserObject::compile` ← `AGCModule<AGX::Impl::FragmentProgramKey>::compileLLVMObject`. The shader translates and its library builds; every vertex shader paired with it fails the same way, and the compiles in flight when the service ends fail with it. Offline, the toolchain's `applegpu-nt` says `LLVM ERROR: Failed to encode.` for the same library.
- **Cause:** a compiler bug, in the pixel shader alone (no vertex shader is needed to show it). The translator makes a predicate of bit 0 twice: the compare `bool b = (x & 1) != 0`, and where the Switch code keeps the predicate in a register, its integer form `b ? -1 : 0`. Where the shader also branches on `b`, the compare has two users in the AIR, a sign extension and a branch. The GPU compiler of the M3 and later (`applegpu_g15`, `g16`, `g17`, and `g18p` for the iPhone; the macOS 26 and the macOS 27 back end alike) can then select a jump on the bit taken straight from a uniform register, and its encoder has no form for that instruction. It happens only where `x` is a uniform the compiler's per-draw constant program has loaded (it holds about 250 values) and the test itself found no room there, so it depends on how many uniforms the shader reads before the test: a few lines more or fewer and the same shader compiles. `-O0` compiles, and so do `applegpu_g13` and `g14`. What was ruled out on the way: the warp shuffles of its derivatives, its border colour reads, the discard and the early return by themselves, the size of the shader, its vertex shaders' outputs.
- **Fix:** `bit0_mask` in `dmsh_rewrite.py` (run by `dmsh_build.py` on every shader): the mask is written from the bit, `-(x & 1)`, the same value, and the compare is left with its branch. It rewrites only `= b ? (-1) : 0;` where `b` is declared as `a != 0` and `a` as `… & 1`, neither assigned again, within one function; a shader without that comes back unchanged. Other bits and other uses of the compare (a select of two constants, a conversion) did not show the bug in the reduced shader, and are left as the translator wrote them.
- **Found on:** PRAGMATA: pixel shader `c4ac3ca598cea32e` (`/re_chunk_000.pak#e1487969619ddb7a#SDF#var15`) tests bit 0 of a constant buffer word (`fp_c10[2].w`) after 6 texture reads and a discard, keeps the predicate and branches on it twice. The front end's EarlyCSE, which merges the repeated loads of that word, is what leaves the compare with its two users ([A base the GPU compiler cannot encode cost the whole offline archive](#a-base-the-gpu-compiler-cannot-encode-cost-the-whole-offline-archive), which keeps such a pipeline out of the offline archive). Reduced with a line-level delta debugger against `applegpu-nt` to the 100-line shader of the test; the failing instruction was read with a debugger on a copy of `applegpu-nt` (a conditional jump whose operand is a uniform register, emitted once, as the last instruction before the abort). M4 Pro, macOS 26.6.2, Metal toolchain 32023.922.
- **Check:** `python3 dm/gfx/tests/test_dmsh_bit0_mask.py` (the rewrite, what it leaves alone, each function by itself). With `DMSH_TEST_METAL=1` the reduced shader goes through `metal` with the chain's flags and `applegpu-nt`: as translated it must fail with `Failed to encode` (the test is skipped where a later toolchain compiles it), rewritten it must compile at `-O1` and `-O2` for `applegpu_g15g`, `g16s` and `g17g`.

### Prebuilt pipeline archives not opened during the first run

- **Symptom:** after a first start's prepare step built the shader pairs' bases into `pso_archive/p*.mtl4a`, the same run still has draws that wait with `pulling base not made yet` and builds those bases again in the background (`hy_base_kick`); the next start has none of it.
- **Cause:** `pso4_init` opens the archive files once, before the prepare step has written any, and `g_arch_ix` / `g_arch4p` stay as they are for the run; the prebuild compiles each base, writes it to a `p` file and drops it.
- **Fix:** `prepare_prebuild` waits for the last compiles too (up to `g_pre_tail_ms`, 10 s, so a compile that never returns still holds the start no longer; without a limit once every worker has returned, `g_pre_flushing`, and only the write is left), then `arch_adopt_prebuilt` opens the `p` files `pso4_init` did not (`g_arch_opened`), oldest first so a newer file's key wins, within `arch_vm_room`'s 2 GiB reserve, indexes them into `g_arch_ix` / `g_arch_ixf` / `g_arch4p`, and clears `g_pbase_miss`, all before the first frame, so the lookups stay without a lock. The files stay out of `g_arch_use`, so `arch_sweep` does not move them out before play has asked for them. Found upstream (nxnative `4add5fbb`).
- **Found on:** Resident Evil 4 in nxnative ([issue #3030](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/issues/3030)), on an M5 Pro: a first start that prebuilt 15,764 bases in 1,100 s still waited 4.4 s over 36 draws for bases not made yet. This game reaches it on a first start with a new chain and no bundle (a development build), when the prepare step builds about 33,000 bases; the app's bundle holds them all.
- **Check:** `t_pso_archive --adopt-prebuilt` (a `p` file captured, opened in the same run, the miss cleared, the base found with no compile, out of the sweep, a second call a no-op); `t_pso_stall` (the whole `prepare_prebuild`, and a held compile that ends the wait after `g_pre_tail_ms` with no adoption); `t_pso_admission` (the reserve).

### Apple's driver crashes when threads build pipelines of one vertex function at once

- **Symptom:** the app stops at a pipeline build with SIGTRAP ("unbalanced call to dispatch_group_leave") or SIGSEGV at 0x30 in the block of `createVertexProgramVariant` (Apple's G16X driver), a few seconds into a cold start or while the prewarm runs. Three Ocarina of Time runs, the first at 18 s.
- **Cause:** found by uncraftablexd (commit b31ed1a5, PR #2079, no longer reachable). The driver keeps one entry per vertex program variant. The thread that creates it compiles the variant or reads it from an archive, and a completion block stores the result and leaves the entry's dispatch group. With several threads building pipelines of one vertex function at once (a prewarm worker's whole pipeline beside others' bases, a compacting start's recaptures) that block ran for an entry already left or emptied. Why the driver does this is not known.
- **Fix:** builds that share a vertex function are made one at a time (`vs_enter` and `vs_leave` in `nvn_pso.inc`): one of 1,024 locks, picked by the function's library name and entry name (`lib_tag`, as the archive key names them; a specialized function by the function it specializes), held across a whole pipeline's compile, a base's archive lookup and compile, a prebuild pair and the bundle probe. Builds of other vertex functions stay parallel. The waits are bounded: a lookup that must not compile never waits (the base is left to a caller that can compile, and nothing is recorded as missing), a game thread (user-interactive QoS) waits 50 ms at most, any other thread 20 s; one that waited that long marks the stripe stalled (a `[RED]` line) and its builds go on unguarded until the holder returns. A specialization of a base compiles no vertex function, and a mesh pipeline has none: not guarded. `DM_PSO_VS_SERIAL=0` builds in parallel as before.
- **Found on:** Ocarina of Time on an Apple G16X GPU (three crashing runs). `t_vs_serial` checks the stripe and the waits, and that threads building bases of one vertex function through `pso_build` are never inside one stripe together; `t_pso_stall` and `t_pso_archive` keep their cases (the unrelated base of the archive test uses another vertex function). Not checked on the crashing runs themselves: the crash depends on timing in the driver.
